Foreword

FOREWORD

After the publication of Nova Science’s Instantaneous Action-at-a-Distance in Modern Physics: Pro and Contra, one of the three editors, N.V. (Viv) Pope, was approached by some of the contributors to the book and asked to convene a meeting of those who were ‘pro’, particularly those who supported unmediated IAAAD.

Consequently, this select group was invited to the meeting, called a Workshop, which was held at the University of Wales, Swansea, in July 2001, under the agreed title of Unmediated Instantaneous Action-at-a-Distance (UIAAAD). The success of the meeting was such that a second Workshop was arranged for April, 2002. In between the meetings the debate was continued by

email and letter.

This book is the record of these often vigorous arguments. It is very different from the usual sorts of Proceedings in that its intention was for the papers for publication to be written following the debate, not prior to it. This was to allow the presenters to modify their arguments in the light of what transpired

both at the meetings and in the follow-up e-mail and postal discussion.

In this way, it is hoped, the book presents a coherent account of whatever

consensus has been achieved and of where disagreement remains.

A. Mary Pope (Workshop Organiser) XVit

Preface

PREFACE

This book is the record of a follow-up workshop to earlier discussions for and against so-called Action at a Distance. For the follow-up it was decided to bring together some of those who were ‘for’, so as not merely to reproduce the former arguments. What is interesting, important and surprising about this is that such a selection of participants did not result in an arid overall agreement, Instead, it

produced worthwhile differences to stimulate a further synthesis.

That some discussion of distant simultaneity is needed after a century cannot be doubted, and this is confirmed by the variety of different scholarly approaches shown here. One such approach seeks to use angular momentum for this. This is not without problems, but related, though not springing from it, are Roscoe’s surprising results on spiral galaxies, Starting from Mach’s Principle. An attempt is made by Rowlands to take this to a fundamental level involving quantum mechanics, and discreteness also plays a fundamental role in Galeczki’s intervention. But the careful argumentation in these and the other papers makes this an important source alike for those in general agreement and for those who differ.

Professor C. W. Kilmister, Department of Mathematics, King’s College,

London.

Chapter 1: Introduction

In science, our knowledge of nature is, of course, essentially heuristic. No scientist, properly so called, can ever sensibly claim that ‘the universe’ is an algorithm to be solved by purely analytical methods of logic and/or mathematics.

It is not to be expected, therefore, that any scientific solution to the problem of understanding nature, however satisfactory it might seem at the time, will survive for ever without having to undergo some serious and perhaps even radical modification. The thirteenth century A.D. synthesis of church dogma and Aristotelian science by Thomas Aquinas, which included the Ptolemaic earth- centred account of the motions of astronomical bodies, ended early in the sixteenth century with the Copernican revolution, which shifted the centre of reference for planetary motion from the earth to the sun. This revolution became established in the seventeenth century by Kepler, Bacon, Galileo and Descartes -- and, of course, Newton — and it lasted for just over two hundred years till the next revolution was created by Einstein and Planck. That new radical solution has lasted now for nigh on a century before the first stirrings of vague dissatisfaction with it, initiated by Ernst Mach.' Sustained by the likes of Niels Bohr, this has begun to burgeon into something like a clarion call for radical change.

The trouble is that during the interim between these revolutionary changes, society settles into a paradigm state in which, with science at its philosophical centre, the concepts of even the most unscientifically minded man-in-the-street become so socially ingrained that no-one is aware of the peculiar nature of the prevailing scientific philosophy, any more than fish are aware of water. The common language moulds itself around the prevailing paradigm, which gradually assumes the status of a permanent fixture. These conceptual settlings may — indeed, they invariably do — end in stultification. Like the tensions in rocks which precede an earthquake, the need for radical restructuring may become so intense

that no further progress can be made without the trauma of a social revolution. In the meantime, the status quo naturally reacts ever more strongly against any attempts to change it. But progress cannot be halted forever. Sooner or later, Science must cast off the constraints that socialisation places upon it. This cannot take place in a single leap on the part of Establishment Science. It has to be done in a piecemeal way, by individuals, the so-called ‘dissident’ scientists, each of

whom, in his own social wilderness, seeks to confront nature directly, for himself.

This, of course, is difficult since in order to obtain funding for expensive researches, these scientists need the financial support of the very establishment they threaten to overturn. (Whoever heard of heretics being paid and promoted for their efforts, even those which, after their days, may prove most beneficial to society?) Whatever it is, then, that motivates these true scientists, it is certainly not any seeking of the usual rewards for excellence in social conformity. They are driven by nothing more than a deep and abiding uncompromising curiosity concerning the things that most people take for granted.

Inevitably, however, like-minded people, learning ‘on the grapevine’ as it were, about one another’s dissatisfactions, get together and compare notes. This initiates a general conceptual drift towards some alternative new paradigm which forms itself in opposition to the old one. Like ants on a monument, these individuals may have little or no notion of the logical shape of the paradigm they are contemplating, or in what radical, philosophical respects it differs from the one they subscribed to previously. But men are not ants. Sooner or later they become aware, if only vaguely at first, of the logical topology of the new paradigm, to the extent that, eventually, they may confidently abandon the old one

altogether and embrace the new.

Such transitions, however, tend, at first, to be confused, with there being as many disagreements, perhaps, among the dissidents themselves as between them and the Establishment. Indeed, it may be no more than the vaguest intimations of something fundamentally wrong with the existing paradigm that leads to a general exodus from the old towards the new. In this way, full logical

articulation of the new way of thinking may take some time, during which, progress in the new direction may be inhibited by non-scientific concerns about ‘precedence’, ‘copyright’ and so on for jealously maintained and guarded pet ideas. Such ‘antler-clashings’ may all but succeed in bringing scientific progress to a halt.

Nevertheless, in one way or another, the new paradigm, if it is a logically viable one, must eventually assert itself. We, however, at the commencement of this third millennium, are sure of very little except that some such paradigm transition is definitely afoot. It is on the basis of these, perhaps vague, expectations of some final ‘Eureka!’ of philosophical realisation that the July 2001 Swansea Workshop was convened. The aim was, by remobilising the logical method that led originally to Science, to resolve disagreements between otherwise like-minded individuals and thereby articulate progress in the new direction. In this way it was anticipated, by the organisers of this workshop, that by a dialectical honing of the various presented ideas, with the time allocated to free- ranging discussion rather than the usual reading of prepared papers, there would take place a logical winnowing-out of concepts — even traditionally venerated ones — which prove to be either self-contradictory or unnecessary and that there would be an unlamented rejection of hypotheses which are observationally and experimentally refuted. It was hoped that by such a clearing-away of conceptual clutter something of the true logical structure of the commonly anticipated New Paradigm might be revealed. Whether, or to what extent, this expectation has been fulfilled may be judged from the following chapters.

Notes and References

' Ernst Mach was the 19th-century Austrian physicist and philosopher whose ideas provided the philosophical basis for moder relativistic physics. In the minds of most scientists, he is associated with supersonics (the Mach number) and with ‘Mach’s principle’, as named by Einstein in relation to the General Theory of Relativity. The latter ‘principle’, originating with George Berkeley, is that all motions of masses, both local and non-local, are relative to the distant stars. For Mach, this action-at-a-distance principle determines the motions of bodies according to the distribution and amount of matter in the universe.

This principle is now regarded as a physical principle. However, like Berkeley, Mach was

essentially a phenomenalist, and it is from this philosophical root that the ‘principle’ stems. In phenomenalism, physical objects, including ourselves as observers, interact directly, not indirectly as in traditional ‘realist’ or mechanistic physics. In this monograph Mach is referred to in both the physical and philosophical (phenomenalist) regard.

Mach’s published works include Contributions to the Analysis of the Sensations, (1897) and Knowledge and Error (1905).

Chapter 2: What the Swansea Workshop Was About

2.1 The historical problem Take a good look at Modern Physics. Who cannot be impressed by its awesome theoretical complexity, its sheer raw power and potential for changing the face of

this planet?

However, in being’so impressed, what do we compare it with? The science of the Medieval alchemists and astrologers was awesome in its time, and it is only in the light of later knowledge that it can now be seen for what it really was, a primitive, mightily mistaken and highly presumptuous, academically elevated view of nature. This was a view which, in its canonised form, all but completely

prevented every rational attempt to change it.

What brought that science to its grinding halt was when the curiosity about nature that was its life’s blood gave place to a Narcissistic study of its own intellectual successes — in a word, scholasticism. At that stage, only on pain of death could anyone even suggest that that science was not the epitome of

understanding that it presumed to be.

That is the problem that presents itself at almost every stage in the development of science, when the subject lurches between overconfidence and indecision, when Authority, seeking self-preservation against what is sees as ‘anarchy’, turns on its critics — ‘shooting the messenger’, as it were — and letting nature and reason go hang. That, in its philosophical essence, is the problem to which the Swansea Workshop was addressed, the problem of pursuing the aims of true curiosity about nature against the back-pressure of an almost terminally self- Satisfied scholasticism. This is why the first consensus view recorded at the

Workshop was: ‘There is something radically wrong with contemporary physics’.

The trouble is that seeking to set Modern Physics right from a

‘professional’ standpoint which rests nowhere but in that Physics itself is like trying to lift oneself by one’s own hair. The best one may do, therefore, to obtain some clue as to where to go next, is to return to philosophical ‘square one’, where all this scientific curiosity started. This, historians tell us, was with the Greeks of the sixth century BC. Theirs was the first systematic use of reason in interpreting

nature. 2.2. The historical search for physical reality

What this reason told them was that nothing can come from nothing, and that nothing can become nothing. So, they concluded, whatever there is always is,

always was and forever will be.

What is, or exists, in that absolute sense the Greeks called substance. In that original sense, true substance, they reasoned, is all there really is. And by the same token, what is not, they said, cannot be. This meant that substance is one,

without gaps or voids or any other divisions.

In other words, for those Greeks, nothing that truly is cannot either be, or become, is not. The problem this created was that it made all change impossible. Everything was forever one, indivisible and unchangeable. And because there were no gaps or voids, there was nothing for the substance to move into. This meant that there could be no such thing as motion. Everything was jammed solid. (This alleged impossibility of motion is expressed in the notorious ‘paradoxes’ of Zeno of Elea.)

Among those pioneering Greek thinkers there were two who addressed this problem in their different ways. One was Democritus, who tackled the immobility (stasis) problem. He argued that since, obviously, motion actually is (exists), the is not, or room for that motion, must also exist. He was therefore the first to identify the substance (the fundamental is) with matter, and the non- substance (the is not) with space, void or the vacuum. The other, Aristotle, tackled the change (immutability) problem. The fundamental substance, he said, may change its form without any change in the substance itself. To say, therefore, “X is

not’, he argued, is not necessarily the same as saying “X does not exist’. It may simply be to say ‘X is not Y’ or some other non-X. In this way, a thing may change its shape, position, character or whatever, so long as this does not entail any change in the underlying substance, which Aristotle called proto ousia (which, unfortunately, he did not define).

2.3 The ongoing search

Now whatever we think, nowadays, of that ancient — and to us, perhaps, very strange and complex — argument there is no escaping from the fact that it was what shaped the thought-patterns and associated language of what we now call “Physics’. Nor has that historical line of enquiry, even today, been satisfactorily concluded — as testified by the growing numbers of conferences in theoretical physics aimed at solving this very same long-time issue. For a while it was assumed, in the tradition of Democritus, that the fundamental ‘substance’ was matter — what we now know as mass — of which it used to be said that it could neither be created nor destroyed. But now we know that mass can be both created and destroyed, that it can be converted into energy and vice versa, so that neither matter nor energy are substances in the original sense of the word but more in the

nature of Aristotle’s forms.

2.4 Analysis of the three-day Workshop dialogue

Is mass-energy, then, the fundamental substance, the proto ousia of Aristotle? Some contributors to the 2001 Swansea Workshop debate (the details of which follow in section 2.6) argue, in effect, for a plurality of substances, others for a unification of these variously proposed substances. Some, for instance, maintain that the unifying substantial principle is mass-energy, since that is currently regarded as conserved under all conditions of motion and change. However, there are other proposed ‘substances’ that also fit the description of conserved quantities. For instance, some contributors point out that momentum is also regarded as conserved under all conditions of motion and change. Others argue that angular momentum is even more fundamental. Others, again, list electric charge is a substance, if not the one and only, most basic substance. Opponents of

this view argue that ‘charge’, measured in coulombs, can be interpreted more fundamentally as a spin kinetic energy in joules, and that, as such, ‘charge’ is no more than a species of more fundamental mechanical energy or angular momentum.

In this way, each contributor to the debate puts forward his own preferred candidate for fundamentality, together with his reasons for this preference. It was intended that these reasons would be presented in such a way as to be rationally negotiable, hence to facilitate debate with a view towards achieving, if possible, a publishable rational consensus of the group. The extent to which this intention was fulfilled may be judged from the record of what actually transpired. Regardless, however, of the remaining differences among the debaters, one common factor which emerged, and on the basis of which the Workshop was convened, is that overall conservation entails some form or other of what some physicists describe as ‘instantaneous action-at-a-distance’. Whether or not that should be more properly called ‘instantaneous correlation-at-a-distance’ was one of the issues debated. Also debated was whether the instantaneous distant affect of atom A on atom B and the follow-on instantaneous affect of B on C, or D, E ... or whatever, are simultaneous throughout (i.e. ,what logicians call transitive) or whether those instantaneities are intransitive, delayed in the process of mediation by the relaying atoms. Some argue that the distant instantaneity has to be transitive because, otherwise, we would have to think of the ‘universe’ as a fragmented polyverse, hence a chaos of completely localised and disparate space- time frames, with no overall present-moment of existence. On that basis, these objectors regard intransitive instantaneity as not only inconceivable but also a contradiction of the unity implicit in the term ‘universe’. Accordingly, they opt for a global instantaneity, or universally advancing present moment. Others oppose this ‘globalising’ of instantaneity by arguing that if instantaneous affects are transitive, then everything affects everything else at the same instant, which resurrects the original Eleatic problem of immobility or stasis that Democritus solved with his atomising of physical substance. Accordingly, these opponents of

‘universal time’ opt for the Heraclitan, as opposed to the Eleatic, approach to unity, which is that of the statistical ‘unity in diversity’, or ‘permanence in change’ manifest, as Heraclitus described it, in the serene stillness of the violently

burning candle-flame.

Other main candidates that were presented for consideration as the fundamental substance were various interpretations of the ‘wave-function’ of contemporary ‘standard model’ quantum theory. The difference in this case is that the ‘substance’ is one of pure mathematical abstraction, which its opponents reject as ‘metaphysical’ (i.e., un-empirical) and therefore, as they judge, ‘not physics as we know it’. Its adherents, on the other hand, argue that only by some such means, that is, by matching mathematical abstraction to what they conceive as ‘the Mathematics of Nature’ can we ever hope to comprehend the natural order of things. The forms of abstract logic and mathematics, they claim, have their own sort of permanence and immutability and therefore qualify for the description of a substance — literally a ‘standing under’ the natural order of everything, including both the world as it is and (when we get it mathematically right) as we observe it. Mathematical relations such as Pythagoras’ theorem, for instance, might therefore be proposed as part of the underlying substance of physical reality. This, of course, identifies the abstractionist or Mathematical view as Platonic, as opposed to Aristotelian Empiricism. That is to say, whereas for Aristotle, forms are in things, for Plato they are separate from and independent of things. In the Aristotelian way of thinking, therefore, forms and things cannot be distinguished in the dualistic, or separatist, way that the Platonic mathematicians assume. Moreover, the ‘Aristotelians’ say, the basic elements of mathematics and of nature are never assuredly the same, so that purely mathematical entities such as ‘combinatorial hierarchies’, ‘wave-functions’, ‘quaternions’, ‘nilpotents’ and the like, are confined to Platonic realms of pure mathematics. As such, their opponents say, they can have little or nothing to do with true physics. Moreover, they argue, unlike universes of mathematics, nature is no algorithm to be solved

by any purely formal or a priori (i.e., Platonic or Rationalist) method but has to be

approached heuristically (i.e., empirically) in both the micro- and macroscopic

directions of our scientific probings into nature.

2.5 The emergence of hidden philosophical divisions

In the Swansea Workshop, differences of this Rationalist/Empiricist kind soon arose between those who, on the one hand, placed the emphasis of their theories on pure mathematics and those, on the other hand, who placed their opposite emphasis on empirical ‘observation’. At the empiricistic extreme, those dubbed by the other participants as ‘experimentalists’ maintained that all that matters in any scientific study of nature are the pure data supplied by observation and experiment and that all ‘theoretical’ arguments are secondary, if not entirely superfluous. This extreme view was contested by others on the grounds that different theories unavoidably entail different interpretations, even of what seem to be the plainest of observations. For such reasons, say these opponents, theories are unavoidable, not only in forming our expectations of how nature works but also in designing apparatuses for testing the theories. These more moderate experimentalists claim that the primary importance of experimentation is to clarify our scientific understanding of nature by sifting-out, according to Popper’s method of refutation, the ‘wheat’ from the ‘chaff among all the various competing observational interpretations, hypotheses and theories relating to the

experimental data.

Be that as it may, in the Workshop, these ‘experimentalists’, whether hard- line or moderate, separated themselves into two groups, each seeking to establish, in their own distinctive ways and, in some cases with no offered philosophical underpinning, the existence of instantaneous far action as an incontrovertible fact. In the first group, !et’s call them the ‘Electrodynamics Group’, the chosen means of verification was that of experimenting with electrodynamical apparatuses, with measurements made in electrical units such as volts, coulombs, amperes and so on. For the second group the preferred means was that of experimenting with spinning bodies with anticipated effects measured in terms purely of mechanical

units such as kilograms and joules. There was also a third group (very thinly 1]

represented, unfortunately) for whom the two types of emphasis are equally relevant and should therefore be unified and coordinated by negotiating a common language suitable to both. As a commentator put it, ‘I am puzzled that these groups should be so adversarial, since there may well be ‘evidence’ for instantaneous far action in both electrical and mechanical phenomena.’ Attempts were made to answer this and similar puzzles about confrontation, both in this Workshop and in science generally, by extending the range of discussion from its

concentration on “physics” to include issues of psychology and sociology.

A further split was manifest between two sub-groups of the mechanical group. The one — let’s call these the ‘Gravity-field’ sub-group — was concemed with the instantaneous affect of spinning masses on classically conceived “gravitational fields’ (which they called gravito-magnetic spin-orbital coupling). The other, the ‘Angular Momentum Non-Field’ group, was concerned with instantaneous effects of those same spinning masses in situations of overall- conserved pure angular momentum, omitting concepts of ‘gravity’ altogether. So far, however, all experimental options still remain open — which, of course, is why there needs to be a continuation of the debate and a gathering of as much relevant

information as may be available.

Another category of input to the debate that has so far not been mentioned is from what might be called Linguistic Analysis as an approach to solving some conceptual problems of physics. The aim of this approach, in the tradition of linguistic philosophers such as J. L. Austin, G. Ryle and L. Wittgenstein, is to prevent the proliferation of what Wittgenstein’s followers called ‘scientism’. This they see as a Babelian-like proliferation of pure jargon and an abandonment, by physicists especially, of those category rules that are essential for maintaining sense in common language. The flagrant breaking of these rules they regard as a primary and definitive source of nonsense in physics — as, for instance, ascribing concrete properties, such as ‘turbulence’, ‘infinite zero-point energy’ and:so on, to a vacuum, and describing interactions between particles as ‘particles’ in the same

sense, talking about such things as ‘the beginning of time’, ‘black holes’,

‘wormholes in space’ ... and so on, uses of language which the linguistic analysts regard as over-vivid and more in keeping with fairy stories than with physics. Much tension was generated at the Workshop and afterwards between those who, on the one hand, claimed a licence for Physics to do with language ‘what the hell it likes’ and, on the other hand, those who, in the interests of maintaining the integrity of common understanding, insisted on challenging ‘professional’ scientistic statements in the Grand Socratic logical/linguistic manner. Part of this ‘Socratic’ concern was to untangle and distinguish the unstated philosophical presuppositions underlying the various presented theories, as, for example, by insisting that they should at least prove, on this sort of Socratic analysis, to be logically consistent both within themselves and with the external, observational/

experimental situation.

The stated aim of the Workshop was, of course, to combine, in a balanced way, all these different inputs not only from differing sectors of physics but also from sociology, psychology, philosophy and, not least, commonsense. The common factor in all these widely different inputs was thus seen to be the sort of logic that is supposed to prevail among jurors from all walks of life in a court of law. In other words, the intention of the Workshop had been to produce something like a ‘Proceedings’ but which is not the usual collection of independently produced papers but the record of a debate. As it is said, “There’s no making omelettes without breaking eggs.’ The same applies to debates. Any true debate has to be more than just a collection of theoretical ‘eggs’, not to be broken but to be presented purely for decoration (‘Fabergé eggs’, one might say, bejewelled with jargon, with all shell and with all substance removed). The Swansea Workshop was intended to be something of a rational consensus, on the shared view of instantaneous action-at-a-distance (IAAAD), to be prepared for public consumption by logical reasoning among a small group of people all sharing the common view of true researchers that every scientific assumption, theory and philosophical approach is and remains logically negotiable. In other words, the

issue in any true debate has to be not whe is right but what is right. Unfortunately,

some bits of theoretical ‘Fabergé eggshell’ — and, in a small number of cases, whole ‘shells’ ~ did get mixed-in with the ‘omelette’, making the result, to that extent, what some may judge unpalatable. Nevertheless, the reader might at least agree, from studying the record of the debate, that in the end, the serving was at

least, as the curate said, ‘good in parts’.

2.6 The Swansea Workshop

2.6.1 Invitation and Introduction to the Workshop

The publication of the book: Instantaneous Action-at-a-Distance in Modern Physics ... Pro and Contra, Eds, A. E. Chubykalo, V. Pope and R. Smirnov- Rueda (Nova Science, N.Y., 1999) led to requests from a number of the

contributors for follow-up discussions on certain issues raised in the book.

Among the papers presented in the Nova book there are, excluding the contras, two main categories of pro instantaneous action-at-a-distance (IAAAD), namely:-

1. theories of mediated IAAAD (e.g., the field and ether theories of classical

electrodynamicists such as Weber and Helmholtz);

2. theories of un-mediated or direct-action IAAAD (distant-correlation, non-

electrodynamical or purely mechanical theories such as, e.g., that of Mach).

In preliminary discussions, at Oxford in England and Cologne in Germany, it was decided that, unlike a full conference, the purpose of a follow-up workshop should be to place a limitation on the numbers of conflicting views presented, in order freely to pursue some promising line of enquiry on which some group of contributors may be more or less agreed. While recognising the freedom of other groups to follow in this way their own selected lines of enquiry, the one to which this particular workshop was to be devoted was that of theories in the second category, that of unmediated or Machian JAAAD. The emphasis of the Workshop was not so much on the presentation of prepared papers as on free dialectical argument aimed at reaching positive conclusions, hence on proposals rather than

statements. In other words, whereas the norm seems to be that of presenting long

papers followed by very short discussions, we preferred the presentation of very short papers followed by comparatively long discussions, in which the accent was on the openness and negotiability of ideas, as opposed to that of theoretical entrenchment. Papers, therefore were written in retrospect, to be presented for publication after the Workshop discussions. (This, of course, did not preclude the prior provision of handouts.) By this means we sought to avoid the continued irresolution that is so much a feature of theoretical physics conferences, nowadays and with which more and more members and organisers declare their

dissatisfaction.

In short, the aim of the Swansea Workshop, through its efforts in one small but significant area at least, was to avoid the preoccupation of Modern Physics with its own historical image, and take another good look at nature as it presents

itself in the round.

It was agreed by the Workshop steering committee that an initial focus for discussion might be the proposal that a prime candidate for the explanation of IAAAD is angular momentum, in which bodies are automatically paired and balanced in the way Mach would have wanted, without need of mediating agencies such as fields and forces in vacuo. Also, being fundamentally quantised, it fits the factual requirements of quantum physics as well as (for some members)

those of Special Relativity (SR).

This did not exclude the Electrodynamicists. Full consideration was given to the fact that the vector-orientations of motion in an angular momentum nexus conform to much the same rules (i.e., Fleming’s rules) as are ascribed to ‘field- forces’ by electrodynamicists. It might well have transpired, therefore, that the most fitting solution to any conflict between the ‘instant correlation’ and ‘electrodynamical’ factions of IAAAD would turn out, in the end, to be no more

than a logical revision of traditional language and terminology.

The main efforts of the Workshop were directed towards producing and

publishing a second compilation of papers signifying some measure of agreement

or, at least, the sticking-points preventing such agreement. In any event, the aim of the Workshop was to identify and remove whatever barriers of convention and tradition there must be that are continuing to withhold a natural solution to the

long-unsolved problem of how bodies interact at a distance.

2.6.2 The question the Workshop addressed

Is there a universe in any true sense of the word or is it all no more than a multiplicity of disunited bits? This, basically, was the subject to be debated at the Swansea Workshop. In other words, the question addressed was: do bodies form systems, i.e., wholes in which they interrelate with one another immediately (non- locally) or must they wait for perhaps aeons to be informed of each other’s presences by local processes such as wandering light-waves and other dissipated

relics of those far-off and long past events?

The following is a summary of the proposals received in advance. These, it must be stressed, are summary statements of how the various proposals relate to the issue in hand, indicating where, in the debate, the proponent was ‘coming from’, philosophically speaking. All finer details were left to be filled-out in the discussion. In this Workshop the mathematical competence of members was to be taken on trust, on the basis of their qualifications in the subject. This curtailment on displays of mathematical dexterity left more time to discuss the real, more basic and notoriously neglected logical and conceptual issues. In this way it was anticipated that the meeting would prove more fruitful and that we should all enjoy the ground-breaking privilege of finding that the papers written for the follow-up to these discussions would be a definite advance on those which, in the

normal way, we would have written prior to them. 2.6.3 Proposals received

From the proposals received, the arguments presented were to be as follows:-

Peter Graneau (under whose initiation the Workshop was convened) will produce a novel argument that the force of inertia is related to the Newtonian law

of universal gravitation in an isotropic distribution of matter, both visible and

‘dark’. All non-Machian (Cartesian) theories, he says, fail because they cannot provide, in the way Mach does, the experimentally observed instantaneous transport of energy and momentum that is compatible with conservation. He also provides a formula for inertia demonstrating this interrelation in terms of the gravitational constant G, the distance between interacting particles, and the sum of all uniformly distributed bits of dark matter. This inverse square law of inertia, Graneau says, is utterly dependent on a uniform, steady-state (i.e., non-expanding) distribution of dark matter, and it removes a major difficulty regarding earlier

theories of Machian inertia.

George Galeczki distinguishes between an isolated system and a confined system which, he argues, is never truly confined, but is always radiationally interactive with other systems. The isolated system, says Galeczki, presents a uniquely privileged reference-frame which is the centre-of-mass coordinate frame (“alpha frame’) of the system. He concurs with Peter Graneau that, as Galeczki says, in order to talk about conservation within the isolated system ‘we have to assume distant simultaneity from the very beginning’. He contends, however, that Newton’s third law, of simultaneous action-reaction, implies distant correlation, rather than distant interaction, because interaction implies, in accordance with the law, a balance of forces and the transport of energies, which is traditionally time-

dependent.

Martin Miiller (who attended but did not write a paper) says we should ‘avoid at all costs’ the traditional belief in the existence of the four in vacuo forces, gravitational, electrostatic, magnetic and nuclear, also the idea that electrons orbit a nucleus in the way planets orbit the sun (i.e., by means of counteracting in vacuo forces). Miiller will argue that at the level of elementary particles, angular momentum conservation requires that these ‘spin-half arrangements have a tangential velocity which is superluminal. He also wishes to address the philosophical implications of the fact that there are two ‘very different kinds of in vacuo motion’. The one is wave-like, with the exclusive speed c. The other is

particle-like, with a true velocity V (which is unlimited) and an observational

velocity v which is always less than c. All these motions, Miiller maintains, are subject to gravity, hence to gravitational collapse into black holes, where the second law of thermodynamics (the law of entropy) becomes invalid. The black hole thus reverses the rule of entropy, providing a balancing negentropy and

thereby ensuring the continuation of life as a whole despite its transience in parts.

Peter Rowlands proposes a synthesis along the lines of “group symmetry’ of the concepts (parameters), space, time, mass and charge. He takes ‘charge’ as a general term for the sources of the conventional ‘electromagnetic’, ‘strong’ and “weak” interactions, and it forms the imaginary part of an algebraic system (a three-part Dirac algebra) whose ‘real part’ is mass. Charge, he says, must be conserved in orientation as well as in magnitude. Non-conservation of direction in space is equivalent to the conservation of angular momentum, and therefore equivalent to the conservation of the type of charge. The conservation laws

applying to each state are those of angular momentum.

Neal Graneau says that the most difficult issue to be explained in terms of any theory of instantaneous distant interaction is the existence of measurable delays between cause and effect. Contact forces are instantaneous; radiation forces are not, and are conjectured to be transported at the ‘finite speed c’. This conjecture prompts us to seek for ‘the entity that is travelling’, which creates paradoxes. However, says Graneau, the measured effects of delayed-action propagation at this “finite speed c’ can be explained on the basis of instantaneous interactions. So, also, he says, can the phenomenon of inverse-square diminution. All this, he maintains, can be experimentally confirmed, which suggests that the concept of a delay-mechanism consisting of simultaneous many-body interactions may be

closer to nature than what we traditionally conceive as ‘radiations’.

Peter Kiimmel will describe an experiment with equal, homogeneously spherical, fast-spinning masses, which shows that their common centre of gravity shifts in accordance with their spin angular momentum orientation. The ordinary inertial motion of heavy masses, he argues, is the result of the tandomising of

multidimensional rotations in many-particle systems.

Martin Kokus proposes a purely classical theory of the unification of all natural phenomena. It is based, he says, on an ‘ether’ model in which particles are actually vortices in rotational hierarchies extending right out to as far as may be observed. This, says Kokus, presents a preferred reference-frame for linear but not rotational motion. The slowing of clocks and shortening of sticks can be explained, he says, in classical terms in which the space and time (of the preferred reference-frame) does not change. Although this theory runs counter to the specified theme of the Swansea Workshop, it was included because it presents an angular momentum account, albeit of vortices in a continuous medium, not of free-moving distance-separated particles in the way Mach envisaged. In a paper which preceded this submission, Kokus cited some very interesting and highly relevant observational evidence for the ways in which orbital and spin angular momenta are interrelated in the manner described by Mach. Besides, the difference between Kokus’ ‘vortices’ and the neo-Machian ‘wheels-within- wheels’ description of angular momentum may well tum out to be negotiable on the grounds of linguistic analysis, as projected in the original Workshop

specification.

Anthony Osborne will argue (in collaboration with Pope) that in assuming that rectilinear inertial motion is the ‘natural state of motion’, Newton inferred that orbital motion is ‘unnatural’, so that it had to be caused by some invisible ‘gravitational force’ acting in vacuo, begging the question as to how that ‘force’ is transported. However, if a system is assumed to be in a state of overall balance, then that balance has to be maintained holistically, with no question of any delayed-action transference of that holistic connection by means of any in vacuo force. This offers a new paradigm description of all free motions as naturally orbital and correlated in an overall-conserved angular momentum interrelation. This contradicts SR, in which no such correlations can take place due to the limit imposed on all physical interaction by the ‘speed c’, Our angular momentum nexus, says Osborne, includes spin as well as orbital configurations, and its vector

orientations as well as magnitudes add and subtract in a systematic way. This

provides a self-sufficient explanation (without in vacuo forces), not only of the correlated motions of stars and planets but also of atoms and sub-atomic particles in situations traditionally described as ‘electric’ and ‘magnetic’. The predictions of this approach may be compared with the results of the experiments to be described at the Workshop by Peter Kiimmel in relation to the work of Manfred Lang.

Viv Pope proposes, in collaboration with Osborne, that angular momentum, in itself, provides a universally preferred reference-frame for all motions, from those of atoms and sub-atomic particles to galaxies. Newton’s rectilinear momentum, he says, is an idealised case of angular momentum. All motion is thus naturally orbital, with no need of invisible in vacuo ‘forces’, of ‘gravitation’, ‘electrostatics’ or whatever. Forces appear only when bodies are prevented from following their natural orbits. Nor, he says, is there any such thing as ‘charge’. These measures, in coulombs, he exchanges for measures, in joules, of the intrinsic spin energy of sub-atomic particles. Vector additions and subtractions of these spin-orientations then account for the ‘attractions’ and ‘repulsions’ of these particles, with no need of any ‘static force-field’ assumptions. The ‘speed of light’, he argues, in corroboration with Herman Bondi, is not the speed of any thing or substance in vacuo, but is the speed of quantum ‘ripples’ in the overall angular momentum balance. The speed of transport of these atomic micro- disturbances is intrinsically instantaneous, in accordance with angular momentum conservation, but has the finite value c in observer-time. This observer-time, for motion in general, is relativistically dilated, as in SR, but in an ascending scale from least to most, with the least dilated being that of Mach’s ‘fixed stars’

reference frame.

David Roscoe will describe how an analysis of a very small sample of spiral galaxy rotations led him to formulate an hypothesis that the dynamics of galaxies are coherently well-defined, and that they occupy discrete states like those of the electron-orbits in atoms. He will then describe how this hypothesis was tested

against three larger samples of galaxy rotations, which produced extremely

powerful confirmation of the hypothesis, Possible mechanisms for this will be

discussed at the Workshop.

Philip Kanarev (in absentia) analyses the law of conservation of angular momentum and finds that it has a fundamental role to play in the formation of electrons, atoms and molecules. He has also carried out experiments which demonstrate how this law changes the gravitational force on rotating gyroscopes. He claims that these experiments show that during the clockwise (looking down) rotation of a gyroscope its weight and free-fall acceleration to earth are reduced, the cause of which, he says, ‘is unclear’. His analysis of the law of angular momentum conservation shows the fundamental role of Planck’s constant in the formation of angular momentum systems. Also, its intrinsic right-handedness manifests itself, he says, in other natural phenomena such as the twisting of the DNA helix and the majority of mollusc shells. Copies of his paper ‘The Law of Conservation of Angular Momentum’ will be distributed at the meeting in lieu of his talk. He declares his belief that time is absolute. This, he says, is because there is nothing in nature which can influence time, as, e.g., by accelerating or

Chapter 3: A Reiteration of Aims and Objectives

Viv Pope

The following is the introductory talk given by Viv Pope at the start of the July 2001 Swansea Workshop.

Let us first remind ourselves of the original intentions of this meeting as set out in the invitation which you all received and, presumably, approved. The workshop title ‘Instantaneous Action-at-a-Distance’ was chosen very carefully to signify a consensus of agreement among those attending, that there is, in the nature of things, an overall coherence of masses and their movements, which is completely at variance with the predictions of Einstein’s Special Theory of Relativity.

Now, having said that, I trust we are agreed that we wish to dissociate ourselves from an altogether tiresome, self-styled ‘dissidence’ that has burgeoned in some areas of what these people fancifully call ‘science’. Some of you will have witnessed, for instance, some blatant examples of sheer bigotry that have appeared on the Internet in e-mail exchanges between members of certain reactionary organisations. Under their banner of ‘dissidence’, these people have created a partisan conflict which smacks more of politics and personal pique than of true science. The trouble is that these arrant anti-Einsteinians (I have somewhere named them ‘Einstophobes’) have created a completely ill-conceived ‘those who aren’t with us are against us’ situation in which it becomes virtually impossible, without falling foul of the one faction or the other, to continue the critical appraisal of Einstein’s ideas in the way which, as a conscientious scientist, he would have naturally anticipated (and the blocking of which, by his all-too-

slavish followers, he would no less naturally have deplored).

Hopefully, then, we will find none of those reactionary ‘Einstophobes’ in

this meeting. Nor, hopefully, wil! we find any of the oppositely entrenched

‘Binstophiles’. What we require is a balanced and open-minded, critical debate on the current state of modem theoretical physics which has fallen into complete confusion in ifs interpretations of certain observational and experimental facts

relating to the very basics of our existence, namely, space, time and motion.

What is certain is that the progress of theoretical physics cannot have ended with Einstein. One of the first to realise this was Mach, the man Einstein acknowledged as his philosophical mentor. So far as Mach was concerned, Einstein’s version of relativity theory was no more than a ‘transitory inspiration in

the history of science’.’

It was also, Mach judged, fundamentally wrong. In a notorious preface to his posthumously published book: Prinzipien der physikalischen Optik, Mach

wrote:

I gather from publications which have reached me, and especially from my correspondence that I am gradually becoming regarded as the forerunner of relativity. ... I must, however, as assuredly disclaim to be a forerunner of the relativists as 1 withhold from the atomistic belief of the present day. ?

Now why was Mach so implacably opposed to Einsteinian relativity? Very few physicists, it seems, know the reason for this, which is why it needs to emphasised here at the outset. It was that although Einstein claimed that his relativism was a natural expression of Mach’s philosophical ideas, and although he claimed to be, like Mach, a philosopher first and a physicist second — as all truly innovative physicists ever were — he, Einstein, had completely mis-

understood Mach’s relativistic philosophy which he claimed to be expressing.

Let us see why this is so. The first move towards modern relativism was made, in the eighteenth century by George Berkeley. The physics of Berkeley’s era was based on the atomic materialism of Democritus, approximately three hundred years B.C., which had been introduced into nascent Western science, by the seventeenth century philosopher René Descartes. In that mechanistic way of thinking, the reality of physical objects did not lie in the appearances they present

to us in observation (phenomena), but in their inscrutable (and purely theoretical)

atomic constituents. What Berkeley did, presaging the relativism of Einstein, was to invert that Democritan way of thinking, giving precedence instead to the observational information-data — the phenomena — as opposed to what Democritus had envisaged as absolutely solid and self-sufficient, absolutely localised bits of matter. In other words, for Berkeley, the reality of anything consisted, first and foremost, in being observed — or ‘perceived’, as he unfortunately expressed it. Anything else we may think of or talk about, ‘space’, ‘time’ and even the existence of atoms and the alleged passage of light between them, is projected,

primarily, from information supplied by the senses.

We say that Berkeley’s choice of the word ‘perceived’ was unfortunate because it made it seem that in order to exist, a material object has actually to be observed by some human observer (ultimately, oneself), which made the object’s unobserved existence as a thing in itself indescribable. The problem Berkeley thus bequeathed to his followers was that of how we can ever be assured of the reality of anything other than our own subjective impressions, or ‘ideas’, as Berkeley

called them.

That was the problem which was handed down, via Berkeley’s successor, David Hume to Immanuel Kant. By taking Berkeley’s system of ideas (idealism) to its logical conclusion, Hume had shown that if ‘ideas’ are all there are, then there can be no ‘system’ that can hold them together other than one’s own pure ‘habits’ of perceiving. This reduction to absurdity, by Hume, of Berkeley’s ‘way by ideas’ galvanised Kant. He could not see how it was possible, if what Hume had deduced was true, that the mechanistic science of Isaac Newton had been so

spectacularly successful.

To reconcile Berkeley’s phenomenalism with Newtonian science, Kant dispensed with Berkeley’s ‘ideas’ and replaced them with ‘concepts’. To dispose of Hume’s ‘habits’ as the sole supplier of connections between these entities he sorted them into a system of logical classes and categories designed to paraphrase,

in his phenomenological terms, the science of Descartes, Galileo and Newton.

In this way, Berkeley’s initial move towards modern relativism was brought, by Kant, to its second stage of development. Unfortunately, Kant’s ‘concepts’ were no improvement on Berkeley’s ‘ideas’. Nor was his system of ‘categories’ any advance on Hume’s ‘habits’, because it was all still rooted in the human psyche. As viewed from the standpoint of objective science, therefore, Kant’s version of ‘relativism’ was, for most commentators, no more than just

another form of ‘subjective idealism’.

The third stage in the move towards modern relativism was made by Emst Mach, the philosophical mentor of Albert Einstein. Mach replaced Kant’s psychological system of ‘concepts’ with a system of objective relations between objects as viewed in different, inter-communicated observational frameworks, or perspectives. This, in effect, was the system that became the famous Special

Theory of Relativity presented by Albert Einstein in 1905.

To Mach’s consternation, however, Einstein took the backwards step of basing his relativism on the atomic materialism of Democritus, which was why Mach wrote his notorious disclaimer. The reason for his rejection was that light, in the sense simply of illumination, was for him no more nor less than the fundamental sensory/instrumental data from which the phenomena of space, time and motion are communally projected. In contrast, Einstein presented light, not as an integral part of the phenomenal scenario but as something which, in the manner of the atomists’ ‘streaming substance’, acts as a sub-phenomenal or ‘behind-the-scenes’ and invisible conveyor and mediator of bits of observational information. For Einstein, this underlying ‘streaming substance’ took the form of esoterically conceived ‘wave-particles’, which he called ‘photons’, issuing from the sub-world of atoms that Democritus had supposed was light’s underlying root source. This so-called ‘Einstein separation’ between the world of self-sufficient atoms and the ordinary world of the observer had simply reconstituted the materialistic science of Democritus, making matter the primary reality, and our

information regarding it as, at best, a secondary and entirely incidental ‘spin-off’.

This, for Mach, meant that the philosophical advance that Berkeley had initiated from Democritan materialism to modern relativism had stalled. For him, Einstein’s version of ‘relativity’ had ended-up in a ‘hung state’ in which it was neither the one thing nor the other, which was why Mach saw it as no more than a ‘transitory inspiration in the history of science’. This opinion of Mach’s is now supported by the growing number of conferences, nowadays, convened to accommodate the increasing dissatisfactions of scientists with Einstein’s idiosyncratic version of Machian relativism, not least on account of the well- known paradoxes that the ‘Einstein separation’ generates when employed in contexts of quantum physics. This testifies to the fact that, despite the well-known practical scientific advances made by Einstein and their proof of the undoubted genius of the man, the philosophical transition from metaphysical materialism to true relationism — that is, from a classical matter-based to a truly empirical, information-based science — remains to be completed. I see the business of this conference as being to break the chronic deadlock in current theoretical physics, which confines it in the conceptual cul de sac of conventional Special and General Relativity, and to allow the subject to continue its rational progress towards the

consistent philosophical position indicated by Mach.

Finally, it needs to be said that only in the context of Machian relationism can the title of this Workshop make any sense. For anyone thinking in the traditional Democritan way, no bits of matter can interact at a distance other than by exchanging some sort of delayed-action ‘streaming substance’, either in the form of particles travelling in a void or by waves propagating in an ‘ether’. For these traditionalists it is anathema to suggest that there can be any kind of instantaneous correlation between atoms or between bodies like the planets of the solar system without mediation by space-travelling, quasi-mechanical ‘forces’, such as those of the ‘gravitation’ invented by Newton and the ‘electrodynamics’ invented by Faraday and Maxwell. For Machian relationists, however, there is no such problem, because for them nature works as a whole, not as an assemblage of

self-sufficient and unrelated, entirely ‘localised’ parts. This currently splits the

science community into those who are ‘for’ and those who are ‘against’ unmediated (called ‘non-iocal’) distant correlation, as in the Nova Science compilation which led to this present Workshop. This Workshop, as its title indicates, was convened in order to coordinate, in the consistently philosophical way that Mach would surely have approved, the ideas of those who are for instantaneous distant interaction. My job, as Coordinator will be to keep these discussions, so far as possible, logically coherent and on the same track. In this way, bits of experimental ‘evidence’ which now seem to fit nowhere, or else only vaguely into perfunctorily heid theoretical positions, may be sorted logically, into their proper philosophical categories. Let us hope that in this way the results of our discussions will be decisive in affirming either the one paradigm or the other — that is, the holistic one of Mach or the reductionistic one of Democritus. Being a small group of like-minded people should give us a much sharper edge than the usual bigger and blunter groups. Too many people attending these large conferences do so for the sole reason of presenting their papers in a fait accompli fashion, leaving no more than a few minutes, if any, for true dialogue and then just departing, as on holiday, to ‘visit the sights’. This makes those conferences, as someone has said, about as useful as a convention of tape-recorders. By contrast, this Swansea Workshop, with its emphasis on dialogue as opposed to the usual ‘bang-it-in-and-away’ sort of presentation, should help break the current deadlock in our attempts to understand nature. At least, if we don’t succeed in

doing something along those lines, then it will be no fault but our own.

Chapter 4: Towards a Consensus

Anthony D. Osborne

The advance notice of the July 2001 Swansea Workshop on Unmediated Instantaneous Action-at-a-Distance (UIAAAD) stated that the main aim of the Workshop was ‘to identify and remove whatever barriers of convention and tradition there must be that are continuing to withhold a natural solution to the long-unsolved problem of how bodies interact at a distance’. It was decided by the Workshop steering committee that a limitation should be placed on the number of conflicting views presented. Accordingly, the Workshop was to be devoted to unmediated instantaneous action-at-a-distance, with all the participants being ostensibly proponents of unmediated LAAAD, as opposed to mediated IAAAD. The emphasis placed on the Workshop was not so much on the presentation of prepared papers as on ftee discussions aimed at reaching as many positive

conclusions as possible.

It was decided by the steering committee that such discussions should initially focus on holistic conservation laws as an explanation of UIAAAD with conservation of angular momentum, in particular, as a possible candidate for consideration as the instantaneously correlating principle in action-at-a-distance. Such holistic conservation laws are implied by particular versions of Mach’s Principle. Another point of discussion was to be how UIAAAD is possible when all ‘action’ and all ‘information flow’ appear to be subject to a universal finite “speed limit’ c. It was the wish of the steering committee that discussions should

not only focus on the macro-scale but also on the fundamental (quantum) level.

Accordingly, towards the end of the Workshop, by common assent, the chairman Jon Blay recorded a list of fifteen propositions signifying what points of concutrence or part-concurrence the contributors thought had emerged from

discussions along these lines. These propositions were as follows:

Something is radically wrong with contemporary Physics.

2. Holistic conservation laws imply instantaneous correlation of motions {‘action-at-a-distance’} and thereby refute Einstein’s naive version of Machian relativism that was called ‘Special Relativity’.

3. Angular momentum conservation is a likely candidate for consideration as the instantaneously correlating principle in action-at-a-distance.

4. Angular momentum is fundamentally discrete {i.e., quantised at the level of elementary particles).

Matter (mass) is ultimately discrete (quantised). The experimentally established constant c remains a significant part of any future Physics.

7. Bondi’s interpretation of c as no more than a dimensional constant, or ‘distance-time conversion-factor’, is a logically viable alternative to Einstein’s interpretation of c as a ‘velocity in vacuo’.

8. The constant c, whether in Einstein or Bondi’s interpretation, entails time-delay in our observations of distant events.

9. Some version or another of Mach’s principle applies.

10. There is a universally preferred, or objective, reference-frame for the positions and motions of physical objects.

11. There is no distance, or space, except in relation to material objects. 12. There is no time except in relation to processes involving material objects. 13. Instantaneity/simultaneity is universal (i.e., transitive).

14. Critical arguments and hypotheses should be stated clearly and unambiguously so as to be unequivocally refutable by logical/mathematical tests of consistency and/or experimental observation (Popper’s criterion).

15. Another conference/workshop needs to be convened to iron out the remaining differences.

Following the Workshop, participants were asked to signify their level of agreement or disagreement with these propositions. Their answers were then to be supported by an explanatory statement, to be circulated for further discussion and then used, if so wished, as the bases of the papers in the eventual publication of the proceedings of the Workshop. All but three of the ten participants at the July Workshop (excluding the chairman) responded to this request. Those who responded were George Galeczki (GG), Neal Graneau (NG), Peter Graneau (PG), Martin Miller (MM), myself (ADO), Viv Pope (VP) and Peter Rowlands (PR). A comprehensive discussion on these propositions, which lasted for about four months, then followed as part of a longer email debate. Philipp Kanarev (PK), who was unable to attend the Workshop, and Michel Grosmann (MG), a participant in a second workshop, also registered their agreements and

disagreements with these propositions.

The analysis of the approximate percentage of agreements and disagreements to these propositions is tabulated below. The second table provides a breakdown of responses by individuals. It is to be noted that Peter Rowlands both agreed and disagreed with Proposition 13, since in his view, there are two different meanings to this proposition. Note also that the two most contentious

issues within the group concerned Propositions 3 and 13.

In what follows, I have provided, for each proposition, a summary of any supporting statements supplied by participants, together with what I see as the essential points of the on-line discussion that followed. I should make it clear from the outset that this is very much my own account of some of the viewpoints and discussions and is not intended to be a comprehensive treatment in any way. In this context, the views and responses that I have attributed to participants are not always reported verbatim, nor are they necessarily in the order in which they originally appeared. Sometimes a précis, or occasionally, a paraphrase, of a

articipant’s view is given. P p g

Proposition 1: Something is radically wrong with contemporary Physics

In view of the aspirations of the Swansea Workshops, it is hardly surprising that there was unanimous support for this proposition from the participants at both the July 2001 Workshop and the follow-up workshop, held in April 2002. Some participants were more explicit than others in identifying specific areas of Physics that they believe are seriously in need of attention. Here are the views of some of

the participants.

Neal Graneau:

I fully concur with Proposition 1 and believe that something is indeed wrong with both the concepts and methodology of modern physics. I am personally very aware of how the scientific community is capable of ignoring facts that contradict accepted theories and is collectively unwilling to reinvestigate well-held beliefs. The manifest existence of longitudinal electrodynamic forces which have no representation in modern textbooks is cause enough for a thorough re-evaluation of the subject.

Peter Graneau: Much is wrong. To document what is wrong we published two books, namely Newton versus Einstein and Newtonian Electrodynamics.

George Galeczki:

Special Relativity (SR), General Relativity (GR), Copenhagen Quantum Mechanics (CQM) and the Big Bang Theory (BBT) all contain contradictions and are therefore wrong. The first postulate of SR is wrong, since the ‘proper frame of reference’ of an object and the ‘centre of mass frame of reference’ of a system of objects are always privileged systems, in which all laws of nature take their simplest form. The second postulate of SR is wrong since the ‘velocity of light’ depends on the (absolute) velocity of the observer/detector, as revealed by stellar aberration and the Sagnac effect. GR, CQM and BBT need a separate (and longer) discussion. There are already very promising alternatives.

Martin Miiller: There are at least ten or eleven severe errors in physics, which have been entrenched in physicists’ minds for a generation or more.

Peter Rowlands:

There is a lack of will to tackle physics from a fundamental viewpoint. Unification schemes, such as string theory, have deductive bases, are model-dependent, and over-elaborate mathematically. In my view, a fundamental theory should be entirely abstract and totally devoid of any model-dependent element. Contemporary physics also takes seriously a cosmology that builds upon assumption in a manner which entirely violates Ockham’s razor.

David Roscoe: My particular problem has to do with General Relativity (GR) and its ‘space-time manifold’ concept. In fact GR does not address what we mean by space or time, and nor does modern physics in general.

Peter Kummel: Textbook physics is in some areas way behind the acknowledged achievements of research results. Authors with research achievements not integrated into the hierarchical establishment of institutionalised physicists are largely neglected. However, freelance authors have the advantage of

doing research without the awkward and prejudiced criticism from above and by competition. In paying too much attention to the outer surface formalities as well as ‘fashion’ publishers, many promising theories and concrete evaluations, verified by experiment, are lost.

Here serves the subject of ‘gravity’ as an outstanding example. Though commonly known as something highly fundamental, institutionalised physicists encounter difficulties when asked about major questions concerning the ‘centre of gravity’, particularly ‘natural and artificial centre of gravity shifts’. Serious answers are totally avoided, since mainstream physicists do not want to take risks by committing themselves on this matter. The gravitational field structure, entirely dependent on the position of its centres of gravity, represents an obscure phenomenon to most physicists.

Personally, I believe that something is certainly wrong with contemporary theoretical Physics and I do not think it is simply a problem of language. In my view, Proposition 1 should be contemplated particularly with reference to UIAAAD, which was of prime concern at the first Swansea Workshop. A major problem in Modern Physics is that there is a major inconsistency between Einstein’s Special Relativity (SR), which limits the speed of any ‘action’ and ‘information’ conveyed to the constant c, and instantaneous action-at-a-distance. This inconsistency is made explicit by the Einstein-Podolski-Rosen (EPR) paradox on the macro-scale and the continuing irreconcilability (despite claims to the contrary) of SR and Quantum Mechanics (QM) on the micro-scale (since QM requires instantaneous correlations). Discussion on this inconsistency developed at the Workshop under Proposition 2 (see below). Pope’s view is the same as mine on this point,

Viv Pope: I agree entirely with Proposition 1. In my view, something is quite definitely wrong at the very root of contemporary Physics. My main reason is that SR, which is one of the two main pillars on which contemporary Physics rests, takes no account of the instantaneous correlation of distant

actions that is demanded by the various (holistic) conservation laws — in particular, the law of angular momentum conservation.

Of course, there are many other apparent inconsistencies in contemporary Physics, such as the apparently continuous nature of Relativity versus the discrete nature of QM.

Discussions at the first Workshop moved on to the general approach that we should take in addressing certain inconsistencies in contemporary Physics. Viv Pope, as discussion coordinator, was clear in his view that we should ‘remobilise the logical method that led originally to Science, to resolve disagreements between otherwise like-minded individuals and thereby articulate progress in the direction of a new paradigm’, by ‘logically winnowing-out concepts which prove to be either self-contradictory or unnecessary and rejecting hypotheses which are observationally and experimentally refuted.’ It was his hope that ‘by such clearing-away of conceptual clutter, something of the true logical structure of the anticipated New Paradigm might be revealed’. Not all participants agreed with Pope’s philosophical stance. In particular, it led to a lengthy and sometimes heated email debate between Pope and Peter Rowlands. The whole debate is recorded on compact disk, but I have attempted to reproduce the essence of the arguments below, since many important points were raised. Not least, these arguments indicate the importance of taking a serious philosophical stance when

attempting to find remedies for problems with Modern Physics.

Peter Rowlands to Viv Pope:

Not everyone at the meeting agreed with narrowing down the terminology, and reducing the approaches. Not everyone believes that this is the problem with physics. Some of us think that it's the narrowing down itself (e.g. to SR or an exclusive field approach), which is the problem. So, my contribution to the proceedings will be extremely positive towards the physics by other contributors — especially to you and Anthony, George, Neal, Peter and Dave (Roscoe) — and I will also stress the value of IAAAD, Mach's Principle, and other main concerns of the group. I will try to bring what insight J can into the fundamental relationship between these ideas and those of QM and particle physics. But I cannot be positive towards a philosophy of exclusiveness.

Viv Pope to Peter Rowlands:

You say you are against any attempt ‘to channel physics into a single picture’. But if a single picture proves to be possible, then why reject that out of hand? How can you say, a priori, that no such single, most conceptually economical and logical, overall picture is possible? You say that one cannot lay down laws as to what nature ought to be like. But by that same token you cannot, yourself, lay down laws as to what nature ought not to be like. . What makes you think that my aim is to restrict the ways of thinking by cutting out things that I consider unnecessary for solving particular problems? What we are talking about are logical and philosophical paradigms, which have to be exclusive, because that is what they essentially are: different conceptual/perceptual ways of organising our thinking, which cannot sensibly be mixed.

You suggest that all those different perspectives on physics, which come with the current philosophy (or, rather, anti-philosophy) of eclecticism, sophistic pragmatism or whatever else you call it, are all somehow ‘inclusive’ or ‘complementary’. I see this as a policy of desperation. As a result, just look at the present state of theoretical physics, with disagreements just about everywhere you look! But how else can it be if alt valid theories are held to be equally true and the choosing between them no more than a matter of arbitrary opinion?

Peter Rowlands to Viv Pope:

I don't disagree in any way that it is essential for physics to be described by a single exclusive picture at its foundational level, but it is only when we are certain that we are at that level that we can safely say that al! other alternatives must be rejected. I don't think for a moment that the Swansea discussion reached that level, and we will only be able to say we have achieved it when we can explain much more fundamental ideas such as

‘ space and time, as well as merely use them, or establish relationships between them which are not purely empirical. You can't establish fundamental principles of how physics ought to be conducted without knowing what the foundations are.

I would also like to point out that my work in physics is as much philosophically based as is yours. In my case, it is based on epistemology rather than on philosophy of science, which, as a practising scientist and historian of science, I have personally found to be of only limited value. The fundamental conviction on which my physics work is based is that nature cannot be characterised, and in very significant ways resists any attempt at characterisation. If we apply a ‘probe’ to nature there is always a ‘response’, which resists the probe and denies its validity. What physics does, very successfully, is to devise a strategy to incorporate both ‘probe’ and ‘response’ into a single ‘systematic’ approach.

So, to me, the question of whether physics (or mathematics) is devised by us or is there in nature in the first place to be ‘discovered’ is irrelevant. It is neither. It is the combination of our ‘probing’ and nature's ‘response’ produced in a very technical way, That combination is manifested in the concept of symmetry, which occurs everywhere in physics at the fundamental level, and is in every case at that level produced, by this process of combining the ‘probe’ and ‘response’. The laws of physics are expressions of this combination.

| believe that there is no contradiction at all at the fundamental level, but at ‘higher’ levels, where we have to use compromises and shortcuts, efc., we are often faced with apparently incompatible choices. Space and time, for- example, are very different concepts but linking them in a physical, as well as mathematical way, will mean that we have to make either space time-like or time space-like, Neither is ‘correct’, and both choices produce a contradicting ‘response’ from nature, but our limited capacity to understand the fundamentally abstract idea involved means that, to get a ‘physical’ picture, we have to choose one or the other.

A ‘physical’ picture means that we can choose alternative ‘paradigms’, such as waves and particles, Schrödinger and Heisenberg, etc., at this less fundamental level, where we are already beginning to compromise. At no time whatsoever can we mix different paradigms without getting philosophical nonsense, but we can recognise that they are equally valid or, indeed, invalid, considered separately. And proving that one is unnecessary doesn't prove anything unless we also prove that the alternative is necessary. In principle, we could say that all such alternative physical interpretations are equally unnecessary. There is nothing eclectic about this position. It is an explanation of how apparently different physical explanations come about.

Another important point to make is that many alternative approaches are not alternative paradigms. We can describe classical mechanics using Newton's Laws, conservation of energy, conservation of momentum, conservation of angular momentum, Lagrangians, or Hamiltonians, and no doubt several other principles, but the physics remains unchanged. This is because we can use more than one type of composite parameter to express the true nature of the foundational ones, and the historical development of physics has shown that it would have been absurd and extremely limiting to restrict our choice to any one of these.

Viv Pope to Peter Rowlands:

Be assured, Peter, that in studying Philosophy of Science, I did not neglect epistemology. However, there were not thought to be any particular epistemological difficulties of the sort you mention with the Newtonian paradigm, so why should there be with some suitably corrected successor? May J also repeat that as far as I am concemed, the question of whether or not some unified picture, or new paradigm, is realisable cannot be decided at the outset but remains to be seen. I cannot see any reason, on logical or epistemological grounds, to be defeatist about it. You say that our Workshop did not produce such an agreed unified picture at its first flush, but failing the once does not necessarily mean failing forever more, And we are getting there, I feel. After all, what are we trying to do if not simply to strip away from the Newtonian paradigm (which, in many ways was otherwise entirely successful) the whole mess of historical accretion that has gathered since Newton and slant it in the direction shown by Mach? I feel

more optimistic than you do, evidently, that such a picture is possible without having to compromise natural logic with desperate ploys like that of ‘complementarity’.

Peter Rowlands to Viv Pope: I don't see the epistemological situation as a problem, but as a way of understanding how physics operates. Newtonian physics worked, in my opinion, because it, for the first time (though unconsciously), took account of the correct epistemology, mainly because of Newton's own ruthless use of Ockham's razor, and his introduction of mass as a fundamental concept on the same basis as space and time.

Viv Pope to Peter Rowlands:

I do see the epistemological situation as a problem. I think it is a central problem presented by our having to choose between a Newtonian and a Machian epistemology, which are the complete reverse of each other. The Newtonian is a rationalist epistemology, in which knowledge starts with a (presumed) ‘God's-eye-view’ of a world-in-itself of self-sufficient matter, space and time — the view sometimes called ‘realist’. The Machian, by contrast, is an empiricist epistemology in which knowledge starts with the observer, and is basically information-based. In other words, in the Newtonian view, matter is a priori and observation a@ posteriori. In the Machian view, observation is a priori and matter a posteriori, which is the complete reverse of the Newtonian view.

Peter Rowlands to Viv Pope:

{t can be argued with some justification that contemporary physics is philosophically mixed up, but the position I am advocating is not. It is technically clear on all aspects, and totally uncompromising. It just doesn't happen to be either Machian (in your sense, meaning an entire philosophy) or realist. Both, in my opinion, are fundamentally wrong, because they are using the wrong epistemology. And, although this at first sight may appear to be a negative stand to take, the very positive aspect is, I believe, that it provides a basis for understanding the common core of real physics, which underlies many apparently opposing positions.

Proposition 2: Holistic conservation laws imply instantaneous correlation of motions (‘action-at-a-distance’) and thereby contradict Einstein’s naive version of Machian relativism that was called ‘Special Relativity’

With hindsight, it may have been better to consider this proposition in two parts.

All participants in the first Swansea Workshop are firm believers in IAAAD, which is an absolute necessity if we are to make any sense of the various interactions, which take place in the observable ‘universe’ as a coherent whole.

For example, if there was no IAAAD, a galaxy could not function as a coherent

whole. IAAAD clearly contradicts Special Relativity (SR), implicit within which is a universal speed limit ¢ for all interaction and information. (See Propositions 6 — 8 below.) Hence it would be very hard to argue against the second part of the proposition. One possible explanation of unmediated IAAAD is the existence of holistic conservation laws and this is the explanation that most of the participants, including myself, support. As Peter Graneau stated, “The strongest point is that momentum cannot be conserved with local action.’ Viv Pope and I would argue that the reason why freely moving bodies behave they way they do, in a spiral galaxy for example, is that the angular momentum of the whole system is naturally holistically balanced and conserved.
