It would be difficult, even for those who did not believe in holistic conservation laws, to argue against this proposition on logical grounds. Hence, it is not surprising that all the participants present at the first meeting agreed with the proposition. Here are the views of some of these

participants.

Neal Graneau:

Universal momentum conservation is not necessarily inconsistent with the time delays inherent in SR. Einstein field theory allows that the momentum is held in escrow by the travelling electromagnetic field. However, energy/momentum calculations involving motors and railguns reveal that the Einstein field cannot exist for reasons of self-consistency and thus | fully agree that universal momentum conservation must imply instantaneous interactions. By interaction, I am referring to ‘vacuum’ UIAAAD forces of gravitational, electrostatic or electrodynamic form.

Peter Rowlands:

SR, naively interpreted, involves a mechanistic (model-dependent) account of the connection between physical objects which cannot be reconciled with the instantaneous correlation required by Quantum Mechanics (QM). My own formulation of QM is based on quaternionic nilpotents of the form (KE + iip + ijm), which are 0 when multiplied by themselves. Non-zero superposition requires instant ‘knowledge’ that no wavefunction may be exactly the same as any other. That is, it requires entanglement of all wavefunctions in the universe.

Jon Blay: Any holistic conservation law implies and logically requires some kind of instantaneous action (or correlation) at a distance (whether mediated or unmediated).

George Galeczki:

In a continuous field theory conservation laws are local, i.e. valid at every ‘field point’ x, y, z, £ In discrete particle dynamics conservation laws for extended systems are global and imply universal time. The meaning of ‘distant correlation’ remains to be clarified; it doesn't imply energy transport. In my view, ‘distant correlation’, just like ‘distant action’, are simply undefined terms, more or less plastic words, but certainly no less strange than ‘force’. However, Newton’s third principle defines force explicitly as inter-action rather than simply action.

Galeczki’s view identified an important point for further discussion, that is, the meaning of distant ‘correlation’, as opposed to distant ‘action’. Here is Pope’s

response.

Viv Pope: I think that a clear distinction ought to be maintained between instantaneous distant action and instantaneous distant correlation. For me, these two are not the same. As I see it, those interrelations between masses, which are traditionally called ‘gravitational’, are correlations and those traditionally called ‘electromagnetic’ are actions. In my thesis, for “gravitation” read ‘angular momentum’, and for electromagnetic’ read ‘change in angular momentum’. Both are proper-time instantaneous, but whereas the former is a static extension (like a single cinematographic frame, or ‘still’), the latter is sequential (as in the running of the film through the projector). While both are proper-time instantaneous, the former is static, in the manner envisaged by Mach, and the latter is propagational in the manner of what we customarily call ‘light-velocity’. Incidentally, here ‘correlation’ is used in the sense of co-relation. For instance, in my view, the correlation of motions in angular momentum is a necessary and continuous concurrence, which cannot be broken without breaking the law of holistic angular momentum conservation.

Peter Rowlands to Viv Pope: Here is something with which I can most definitely agree. The gravitational and electromagnetic/strong/weak interactions are fundamentally different. I don't object either to the words correlation or action, if you mean propagated at light velocity for the latter. Yes, both types of interaction are proper-time instantaneous. Yes, gravity is propagated without time delay. It is interesting that a book I am currently reviewing called Newton's Gift by Berlinski, says almost in passing that gravity is instantaneous, although the book is undoubtedly 100% mainstream — this would not have passed for orthodoxy a few years ago. Yes, there is a c-related time delay in the action involving electromagnetic/strong/weak forces. Yet in QM terms there is an instant correlation. As I would put it, the correlation or non-locality is between the wavefunctions and this is always instantaneous but it doesn't

involve measurement until we ‘collapse the wavefunction’, and it's measurement that involves time-delayed propagation.

Proposition 3: Angular momentum is a likely candidate for consideration as the instantaneously correlating principle in action-at-a-distance

Many of the participants at the July 2001 Workshop believe that UIAAAD is due to holistic conservation laws of one form or another (see Proposition 2 above). Indeed, a particular version of Mach’s principle implies the existence of holistic conservation laws and ail the participants present at that meeting are supporters of this principie (see Proposition 9 below). Recall that the genesis of Proposition 3 was the wish of the July Workshop’s steering committee for discussions to consider angular momentum in particular as a candidate for the instantaneously

correlating principle in action-at-a-distance.

The participants failed to reach a consensus of opinion on this proposition. In fact, this was the only really contentious issue apart from Proposition 13. However, a significant number of participants held the belief that conservation of angular momentum was a likely candidate for consideration as the instantaneously correlating principle in action-at-a-distance, while not believing that it was the

most likely candidate:

Neal Graneau:

I believe that the essence of Newtonian mechanics is momentum conservation and I feel that angular momentum conservation is a possible candidate for the interaction principle in an ULAAAD universe. However, it looks to be very difficult to apply to practical problems and at the moment, there are no experimental results that demonstrate its predictive capabilities. In my view, the concept of linear force cannot be removed from the analysis (even if it is called a ‘force equivalent’).

Peter Rowlands: Conservation of angular momentum is important because it carries the information that the three charges responsible for the electromagnetic, strong and weak interactions are separately conserved. Interactions are essentially a statement of the transformations of space and time coordinates under which these conservation principles apply. Each charge is connected to one of the quaternions in the nilpotent wavefunction and each is involved with a separate aspect of angular momentum conservation. Angular momentum clearly has a fundamental role to play because it is linked to a

fundamental quantity (charge), but it is not intrinsically fundamental and the link may be broken by decoherence in the quantum/classical transition,

Even though I support UIAAAD, I don't believe that it excludes all other options. Also, I don't believe that angular momentum is ‘superior’ to other approaches, such as those using force, linear momentum, Lagrangians, etc.

In contrast to Neal Graneau and Rowlands, Pope’s view is that angular momentum is the most likely candidate for consideration as the instantaneously correlating principle. Viv Pope: I believe this because in angular momentum, the force-free motions of masses are automatically paired and distanced in mutually balanced systems, with no need of any ‘ether’ or ‘field’. As Osborne demonstrated in his talk (at the first Swansea Worksop), angular momentum is sufficient in itself to define the orbital motions of bodies without involving ‘gravitational’ or ‘electrostatic’ forces. Both Galeczki and Peter Graneau were against the proposition. Galeczki’s view is that on thermodynamic grounds, conservation of energy appears to be more general than conservation of angular momentum. Graneau’s view is that conservation of angular momentum is already implicit in Newtonian dynamics, which had not prevented the advent of Relativity with its emphasis on delayed distant action. Hence, his view is that conservation of angular momentum cannot

be sufficient to explain IAAAD.

Personally, I believe that conservation of angular momentum is certainly a prime candidate for the instantaneously correlating principle in action-at-a- distance. Angular momentum contains information about spatial relations, mass, time and orientation. As pointed out by Rowlands, conservation of angular momentum implies that the three charges responsible for the electromagnetic, strong and weak interactions are separately conserved. In my opinion, angular momentum is a more natural measure than linear momentum since all ‘force-free’ motion is ultimately orbital, and linear momentum is included as a special case, Of course, there are other candidates for the instantaneously correlating principle

in action at-a-distance, such as conservation of energy. (As pointed out by Jon

Blay, the only likely candidates for holistically conserved quantities are angular momentum, linear momentum and energy.) I agree with Pope, however, that angular momentum is a more fundamental measure than energy. Energy may be regarded as a component of angular momentum at the fundamental level, but not vice-versa. Besides, angular momentum is a vector quantity, which provides information about orientation, whereas energy is only a scalar quantity and so cannot provide as much information. However, as Galeczki has pointed out, conservation of angular momentum alone cannot provide a complete solution to a dynamical problem. The on-line discussion following the first Workshop focussed on the choice between conservation of angular momentum, linear momentum and

energy as instantaneously correlating principles in action-at-a-distance.

George Galeczki:

I wonder how could we present in a convincing and understandable way to the laymen the thesis that among various conserved ‘system quantities’ such as linear momentum, angular momentum and energy, ‘the chosen one’ is angular momentum? What is wrong with energy? Like angular momentum, energy includes the three fundamental quantities of length, time and mass. Also, there is a technical point here, namely that all angular momenta of discrete bodies (in the universe) have to be evaluated/calculated with respect to the same, unique point, called by C. Neumann in the 19th century, “body alpha’. For me the ‘body alpha’ is the centre-of-mass of the known universe, to which every motion has to be referred.

Viv Pope to George Galeczki:

The principle behind angular momentum can be understood well enough. (What observant layman has not been puzzled by the way a spinning bicycle wheel behaves when, holding it in his hands, he tries to twist it away from its set axis, or in contemplating the manner in which the winds curl around the earth? This kind of motion is everywhere observed, unlike linear momentum, which isn’t.) It is that principle which first and foremost needs to be got across. To quantify angular momentum does, of course, require some mathematics.

There can be no doubt that every finite angular momentum entails a ‘special point’, which is the fulcrum or barycentre of the whole system. However, if the universe tums out to be infinite, then that ‘point’ in it becomes spread all over to form a uniquely preferred reference-frame. Differential time dilations also imply some least-dilated time frame, which I identify as that of the so-called ‘fixed stars’. In short, it is the Machian frame relative to which gyroscopes orientate themselves, the frame you like to call that of the ‘body alpha’. George Galeczki to Viv Pope:

You and I agree on the holistic conservation of angular momentum, which, in tum, is in full agreement with Newtonian mechanics and in full disagreement with SR. However, I agree with Peter Rowlands that angular momentum is just one candidate for consideration as the instantaneously correlating principle in action-at-a-distance, among several others, and no convincing arguments have been presented for preferring it. That ‘angular momentum is more easy to visualize than linear momentum’ is no convincing argument. Among the three conserved quantities in a closed system, i.e., linear momentum, angular momentum and (internal) energy, I consider energy as the most general and fundamental one. The unique role of energy is best displayed in thermodynamics, the most general, empirically supported compartment of physics. However, the three conserved quantities are independent of each other, in other words neither is a particular case of another. In the two-body problem, for example, the angular momentum and the total energy are two distinct integrals of motion, while the conservation of the total linear momentum means that the centre of mass is moving with uniform velocity. Except in uniform, circular motion, the conservation of the angular momentum never provides a complete solution of a dynamical problem.

Viv Pope to George Galeczki:

I do not see how energy can be more fundamental than angular momentum. At the fundamental level, energy can be regarded as a component of angular momentum, but angular momentum is certainty not a component of energy. Also, conservation of angular momentum does not imply conservation of kinetic energy. In an elliptical free-orbit of constant angular momentum, the orbital speed of the body typically varies and so does the kinetic energy. Hence, conservation of angular momentum appears to be more fundamental than conservation of energy. Moreover, all the motions that come under the rule of entropy are, ultimately, angular momentum systems of particles about one another like the stars and planets, hence are subject to the same overall rule of angular momentum conservation.

Angular momentum and linear momentum may be regarded as the same fundamental quantised phenomenon at the micro-level, but [ do not accept that each may be regarded as a special case of the other. In the first place, there is no way in which linear momentum can be regarded per se, as quantised. In the second place, since linear momentum can be expressed as a special (i.e., limiting) case of angular momentum but not vice-versa, angular momentum is the more fundamental of the two.

George Galeczki: I felt attracted by the angular momentum approach. I have always contended that since everything in the Universe, from electrons to galaxies, rotates, linear motion is the exception, rather than the rule. However, just as geometry needs the concept of straight line in order to define curvature, so

does any description of nature. Newton’s first law is built upon the concepts of straight line, uniform time and uniform velocity, even if all are idealizations. The beauty of Newton’s dynamics is that it can be improved by successive approximation. Also, it gives a description of any motion as a combined roto-translational motion. ‘Force’ is responsible for translatory (linear) motion, while ‘momentum of force’ accounts for rotational motion. Force is the time-derivative of linear momentum, while momentum of force is the time-derivative of angular momentum. The two kinds of motion, as well as the two ‘causes’ are qualitatively different and irreducible to each other. Energy has both translational and rotational (spin and orbital) contributions. I confess that I have difficulties in ‘freeing myself from Newtonian dynamics.

Proposition 4: Angular momentum is fundamentally discrete (i.e., quantised

at

I believe that any physical measure, in contrast to certain mathematical measures, has to be fundamentally discrete. Angular momentum contains information about mass, distance, time and orientation and so has to be fundamentally discrete. Most physicists would agree that angular momentum is ultimately quantised. None of the participants disagreed with this proposition, but two were unsure. There was some disagreement as to whether energy is ultimately quantised or not. Here are

some of the views expressed. (This proposition did not generate further on-line

the level of elementary particles)

discussion following the meeting.)

George Galeczki:

Angular momentum, rather than energy, is quantised at the elementary particle level. I maintain that the physical meaning of Planck’s constant is angular momentum (rather than ‘action’, which nobody knows the meaning of) and that, like in the Bohr model of the atom, quantisation of angular momentum has priority over quantisation of energy.

Viv Pope:

Angular momentum has to be fundamentally discrete because otherwise, by conservation of angular momentum, for anything to move, every other thing would have to move in that self-same instant, entailing an Eleatic stasis, or ‘cosmic gridlock’. However, in my view, energy is not a quantised phenomenon at the fundamental microphysical level. What is quantised, as I see it, is not energy as such, which is a continuous variable, but action, which is the product of energy and time. This is quantised in units of Plank’s constant A.

Peter Rowlands:

Conservation of angular momentum carries the information that the three charges responsible for the electromagnetic, strong and weak interactions are separately conserved. In the case of these interactions, angular momentum is fundamentally discrete because it is linked with charges that are fundamentally discrete. More generally, quantisation applies to energy, linear momentum, angular momentum and rest mass of discrete states because charge is quantised.

(See also Rowland’s response to Proposition 5.) Neal Graneau remains

unconvinced of the need for Planck’s constant, which he feels is simply a ‘fudge

factor’, and so remains unconvinced of quantisation of angular momentum.

Neal Graneau: The notion of discrete quantised units of angular momentum arose from a late 19th century attempt to apply the equipartition theorem (developed for a finite number of gas molecules in a bottle) to the problem of radiation in a cavity. The only way this could be done was to ascribe particle-like properties to the radiation. In order to limit the number of photons in the cavity and thus avoid the so-cailed ‘ultraviolet catastrophe’, Planck proposed an energy per particle of ho. In response to Proposition 4, I believe that radiation does not exist in a UIAAAD universe, so that the energy stored in the box is contained in the vibrations of the atoms in the walls of the box. There are only a finite number of these (like the gas molecules) and thus there is no potential ‘ultraviolet catastrophe’ and no need to ascribe a

quantised energy per interaction; thus the need to calculate / disappears. I see no reason to hang on to this fundamentally field theory ‘fudge factor’ A.

Proposition 5: Matter (mass) is ultimately discrete (quantised)

This proposition did not generate a lot of comment or discussion, the participants being 100% in agreement. George Galeczki’s response was typical: ‘As a physicist I am a supporter of atomism, i.e. of discreteness. Matter consists fundamentally (pace Mendel Sachs) of discrete quanta, rather than of continuous fields’. However, Galeczki, others and myself believe that any mathematical description of nature (as opposed to physical measurement) is greatly facilitated by the use of continuous variables. Galeczki was also in full agreement with Viv Pope and others when he stated ‘We fully agree that matter is fundamentally

discrete, since a physical continuum would really be a “Parmenidean universe” or

“Eleatic still”, in which no motion would be possible.’ Here is Neal Graneau’s

view.

Neal Graneau: Matter always appears in particle form and large particles always seem to be composed of smaller particles; thus Proposition 5 seems quite reasonable. If matter were not quantised then it becomes philosophically difficult to apply zero force between two particles of zero mass.

Interestingly, Peter Rowlands agreed with the proposition for matter, which he equates with charge, but not for mass, which he equates with energy. His reasons

are reproduced below, and follow on from his views on Proposition 2.

Peter Rowlands:

There is a c-related time delay in the action involving the electromagnetic/ strong/weak forces and yet, in QM terms, there is an instant correlation. The explanation is that the correlation or non-locality is between the wavefunctions and this is always instantaneous but it does not involve measurement until we ‘collapse the wavefunction’, and it is measurement that involves time-delayed propagation. On the other hand, ‘gravity’ is propagated without time delay and so I have always argued that there must be a ‘physical’? mechanism for the instantaneous correlations, because gravity requires a universal linking of all energy. This, to me, is the same thing as both QM and particle physics requiring a ‘filled vacuum’ (in effect, the Higgs field), and is what I mean when I say that mass-energy is continuous. The Higgs field or whatever we might mean by a ‘filled vacuum’ is nothing to do with ‘matter’, or, as I would put it, whatever arises from the presence of ‘charge’ (electromagnetic, strong or weak). Measurement always involves discretisation and this, to me, is the same as saying the action is propagated with time delay — that's how we know things are discrete. (Dan Kurth, the philosopher, assured me of the validity, philosophically, of a fact I have always believed, that the concept of repeated division cannot be applied to a continuum; in other words, space, or anything else involved in measurement, cannot be made continuous.) So charge is discrete, and space is always structured in this way.

Hence, for me, matter is discrete because it is essentially another word for charge, whereas mass is continuous since it is really energy. However, the stationary energy state associated with discrete charge is certainly discrete.

Proposition 6: The experimentally established constant c remains a significant part of any future Physics

Though there are, of course, many different physical interpretations of the

constant c, there was unanimous agreement that c will remain a significant part of

any future Physics. One traditional interpretation is that it is the ‘speed of light in vacuo’, simply because it was first determined from Roemer’s observations relating to one of Jupiter’s moons. It is also the constant relating the ratio of electromagnetic charge to electrostatic charge, which appears in Maxwells equations and first determined in the Weber-Kohlrausch experiment. There are other interpretations also. For Pope and myself, it is the fundamental conversion factor, which converts a time measure into a distance measure, in the form of converting light-seconds into metres (see Proposition 7 below). We can always change our system of units to ensure that c = 1. Indeed, many General Relativists adopt this convention. This simply implies that a choice of very impractical units

produces a very easy conversion factor. Here are two other views.

Peter Graneau: I agree with this proposition because of the perfect agreement with the propagation of voltage and current disturbances along transmission lines. In my view, the Weber-Kohlrausch experiment is wrong because galvanometer torques are not produced by the motion of charge in the wire of the galvanometer coil. .

Peter Rowlands: The constant ¢ is implicit within the nilpotent wavefunction, which is a square root of the relativistic energy-momentum-mass relation. What it means, in effect, is that measurement, being always between discrete objects, always involves a time-delay.

(See Rowland’s views on the previous proposition.) In Neil Graneau’s view, c is

physically significant in physics, but not necessarily a constant.

Neal Graneau: I can see no evidence that ¢ is a constant. In fact measurements involving radar interactions with mirrors placed on the surface of the moon reveal that c is not constant and depends on the local mass distribution of the earth- moon-solar system. Further, signals from satellites to GPS receivers on the earth’s surface seem to have different delay constants depending on whether they are to the east or west. There are also the well-known laser range experiments where a Mars orbiting spacecraft reflected radiation pulses, and the timing delay, which is always consistent with the observed motion of Mars and the satellite, was dramatically increased as the line of sight approached the sun. The extra delay was easily measured and implied that the ‘delay of radiative effects’ (1/c) was affected by the influence of the

massive and also electromagnetically active sun. This result was naturally twisted by the relativists who claimed that c remained constant while time was distorted. However in the uncurved vacuum that I am discussing, the only interpretation is that c is variable. Obviously if the path of a spacecraft is through empty space, then we can expect c to have roughly the value that is also measured on earth, which is a very smal] mass compared to the sun.

As I said previously, however, whether c is variable or not, if we don't ascribe its physical value to some physical cause, then it necessarily becomes a property of the vacuum. I presume therefore that we are all in agreement with the suggestion that c is caused by matter distribution. This is a pure analogy with the fact that the delay in a transmission line or wave guide is affected by the position of the conductors. ,

However, Neal Graneau is very much in the minority with his view that c is not a

constant. Here is Pope’s response.

Viv Pope: It is well known that the ratio of distance to time is less in a refractive medium than it is in vacuo. Also well known is that the atmosphere of the sun does not end abruptly at what we see as its surface but extends well beyond the earth, as far as the outermost planets.

Now an atmosphere is, of course, a refracting medium, as we are well aware from the effect of our own atmosphere on the sun's rays and so on. So I do not find it surprising that the ratio of distance to time decreases, the nearer the line-of-sight gets to the denser regions of the sun's atmosphere in the ranging experiments Neal describes.

In any case, as I've already argued, the fact that one can measure either c or those variations in it that Neal writes about does not entail that this is what happens to that ratio in the space between the observer and the object. The whole thing can be explained kinematically — that is, in the Machian way, as a factor, not of a ‘space’ that we may imagine to exist in and of itself in between bits of matter but of observation itself. In this kinematic alternative, neither the measuring of c nor of variations in this conversion factor entails anything happening ‘in space’ to what we classically imagine as ‘light itself’.

Proposition 7: Bondi’s interpretation of c as no more than a dimensional constant, or distance-time conversion factor, is a logically viable alternative to Einstein’s interpretation of c as a ‘velocity in vacuo’ |

Following on from Proposition 6, this proposition examines just two interpretations of c. Hence it is not surprising that Neal Graneau was a dissenter in

this case. In fact, he was the only participant who disagreed with the proposition.

In my experience, it is hard to convince students studying SR for the first time that light ‘travels’ at the same finite ‘speed’ ¢, relative to all inertial observers. However, a conversion factor is naturally universal and this approach gives a far more logical explanation of the second postulate of SR. A conversion factor for converting (light-) seconds into metres is no more mysterious than a conversion factor for converting pounds into kilograms. I agree with Martin Miller when he states that c is not physically attainable or observable as a speed, although it can be thought of as a universal speed limit. Such a limit implies a time-delay in our observations of distant events. Just because ¢ has the dimensions of a speed, this does not imply that it represents a physical speed. Of course, there are other ways in which c can manifest itself as a conversion factor, as Peter

Graneau implies.

Peter Graneau: I agree with the proposition in so far as ¢ is not an energy flight velocity. But in transmission line theory we find that c = 1/V(LC), where L is the inductance per unit length and C the capacitance per unit length of the line. Inductance and capacitance are physical parameters and not merely distance-time relations.

Here are the views of some of the other participants.

Peter Rowlands: ; At a fundamental level, space and time must have a linked system of units. This is prior to any mechanistic interpretation, being derived purely by symmetry from the properties of quaternions.

Viv Pope: Just because c has the dimensions of a speed, does not necessarily imply that it is a speed. To have a speed is one thing. Merely to register a time- delay (that is, to have no more than the dimensions of a speed, as c does), is something else. In any case, most contemporary physicists would have to concede that for light to be anything of real substance, travelling at the speed c its mass would have to be larger than that of the whole universe, which it plainly is not.

George Galeczki: So long x, y, z, t are independent Eulerian variables — as in fluid dynamics, or in field theory ~ ¢ could well be a ‘conversion factor’ between space and

time variables and Bondi's interpretation is superior to Einstein's. The problem is that in our world, particles, rather than fields are fundamental! In

particle dynamics, however, x, y, z, ¢ are not independent: x(£), y(t), z(t) are dependent Lagrangean variables.

Herman Bondi was not the only one who maintained that c was no more than a universal proportionality constant relating distance and space. Burniston-Brown, while writing about ‘delayed action at a distance’ (which work he never finished) thought the same way. In the 70’s, Mendel Sachs of SUNY — at Buffalo — argued that ‘the very four-dimensionality of our ‘space-time language’ requires the existence of a universal conversion factor with the dimensions of a velocity’, thus making Einstein’s second postulate superfluous. Sachs also rejects the photon as a ‘bona fide’ particle.

Viv Pope to George Galeczki:

I am delighted, George, with your mention that Bondi was not the only one to maintain that ¢ is no more than a constant of proportionality. For the record, Bondi wrote to me on July 19th, 1985 to say that he claimed precedence (over me) for this idea, which he published in 1965 (Bondi, H.: Assumption and Myth in Physical Theory, CUP, page 28). Mary (Pope) thinks, however, that he may have got this idea from some correspondence he had with me, a few years previously. I don't wish seriously to contest this claim of Bondi's, but it is certain that in unpublished papers I expressed this idea independently, as can be seen from the record of correspondence entitled Philosophical Glimpses in the Swansea County Archives (ref D/D NVP/1-17, vol. 8 page 2970). Anyway, what really matters is not who thought of the idea first but whether or not the idea of c as a conversion is, or is not, more efficacious than thinking of it as a velocity — with all that the latter entails concerning what we imagine is ‘travelling’ and how it does so, whether in the form of ‘waves’, ‘photons’ or whatever.

Certainly, as you say, this non-mechanistic interpretation of c, by Burniston-Brown and Mendel Sachs — and no doubt others if we looked for them — is the only kind of interpretation which is congruent with a consistently Machian approach to physics.

Finally, here is Neal Graneau’s point of view as the only dissenter.

Neal Graneau: Following my response to Proposition 6, if ¢ is not a constant for all observers then the Bondi/Pope interpretation of c as a conversion factor between space and time makes no sense to me since ¢ is only measurable in electromagnetic interactions, and yet space and time are concepts for all interactions. This proposition certainly requires clearer definitions of space and time before it can be fully discussed.

Proposition 8: The constant c, whether in Einstein’s or Bondi’s interpret- ation, entails time-delay in our observations of distant events

As far as 1 am concerned, this proposition is unarguable. Whether c is interpreted

as a speed or as a distance-time conversion factor, it represents a universal finite

speed limit for observational information of any kind. This would still be true if ¢ were not a constant. So it is not surprising that none of the participants disagreed with this proposition. Only one participant who remained unsure gave any sort of reason for his view. This was Peter Graneau who stated that, “Since the flight of energy is the opposite of IAAAD, the time delays between emission and reception

of radiation are not necessarily always given by c.’

Peter Rowlands: At a fundamental level, space and time must have a linked system of units. This entails time-delay in observations of discrete events, and a collapsed wavefunction, but, where there is no wavefunction collapse, as in quantum non-locality, there is instantaneous correlation. The two possibilities are incorporated directly in the nilpotent wavefunction.

Neal Graneau:

It is sufficient to note that experimentally we experience c-related delays in our observation of distant events through our electromagnetic sensors (eyes, light detectors, etc.) As Tom Phipps once wrote, ‘If Mach was right, the seen universe and the felt universe are two quite different places. We see because of causal (velocity of light) retardation of electromagnetic radiation (light) an old universe — a thoroughly obsolete relic. But the inertially felt universe, free of such radiation, is strictly up to date.’ So, in my opinion, ¢ is nothing more than the delay of electromagnetic effects.

I believe that delays happen between events separated by empty space. The delays however occur as a result of interactions with other matter also separated by empty space. I am not happy with the idea that the delay per distance or ‘speed of light’ can be a property of a vacuum, and this does seem to be inherent in Viv (Pope’s) conception. The truth is that the evidence that we have about light and radiation is insufficient to decide which of these models is correct which is why I am happy to leave it as a philosophical problem until related issues in electromagnetism and gtavitation/inertia are ironed out and only then we may have a new outlook which will possibly reveal to us whether the vacuum has physical properties or not,

Neal Graneau’s view gave rise to a number of interesting questions, particularly since he gave a different explanation from Pope and Rowlands as to why UIAAAD is not inconsistent with time-delayed observations. His view also opens up the question of whether the vacuum can have associated physical properties or not. Discussion of this question developed through replies by Galeczki and then by Graneau and Pope.

George Galeczki to Neal Graneau:

I appreciate your openness in acknowledging your dissatisfaction with the idea that delay per distance, or the ‘speed of light’ could be a property of a vacuum. However, after stating your belief that ‘delays happen between events separated by empty space’ and that ‘the delays, however, occur as a result of interaction with other matter also separated by empty space’, you ask, whether, after all, ‘the vacuum has physical properties, or not’. These doubts show clearly the limits of your Machian philosophy, according to which (see J. Barbour, for example) ‘space is no more than the totality of instantaneous distances between discrete matter’ (in a vacuum, I assume). I wonder what kind of new experimental facts, about electromagnetic radiation in general and light in particular, you are expecting in the undefined future. Are telecommunications, astrophysics, illumination engineering and the like not already enough? I’m well aware that it is not easy to make choices, but to postpone a decision ‘until related issues in electromagnetism and gravitation are ironed out’ (to see whether vacuum has physical properties, or not) seems to me a too easy subterfuge.

Neal Graneau to George Galeczki:

I do firmly believe that all of the circumstantial evidence points to an IAAAD universe in which the vacuum is not required to have physical properties.

By overstating the quality of the physical evidence, we will never be able to enter a dialogue with the conventional physics community. A more concrete form of experimentation would require us to be able to alter the vacuum ‘distance related delay of EM effects’ in a controlled laboratory experiment. Already, this delay can be controlled by introducing transparent media (which are mostly vacuum anyway) and we know, for instance, that the ‘distance related delay’ is not constant between all points between the earth and moon. Once we can control this delay in a laboratory vacuum at will, then we will be able to say more definitely what is the true cause of it.

Viv Pope to Neal Graneau:

You say that ‘the idea that light could be a property of the vacuum seems to be inherent in Viv's conception’. You could not be more wrong. For me, as for Mach, space (void, vacuum or whatever) has no properties whatsoever. A void, as such, can have no size, no duration nor motion in and of itself. All lengths, times and whatever are measures relating to matter and material events.

One of the most difficult problems we have to deal with is that even when the most conclusive experiments have been carried out, there may still be trouble. For instance, the most conclusive experiments that have been performed have affirmed that a light signal takes time to travel from A to B. There are also other experiments which are claimed to prove, no less conclusively, that there is instantaneous action-at-a-distance. Now just suppose that these evidences for delayed and instantaneous action turn out

to be equal. Surely, if one believes in IAAAD, one must anticipate this eventuality. Then one must have some theory as to how these evidences can be reconciled. The question of apparently contradictory evidence is a logical question, which cannot be answered by just performing more and more experiments.

The first paragraph of the above reply of Pope to Graneau is developed under Propositions 11 and 12. The second paragraph of his reply gives rise to an

additional Proposition 16 below.

Neal Graneau:

I have implied that Viv's conception implies that light is a property of the vacuum, so I should explain my reasoning. From what I have read, Viv assumes that while the interactions may be instantaneous, the observer delays are caused by a constant c, which relates distances to times. I would argue that the fact that c has a measurable value dictates that it must have a physical cause. | attribute this value to the distribution of matter, but Viv has proposed that it has no physical cause, in other words that it is a definition and thus a property of nothing. To me, this is the same as saying that it is a property of the vacuum.

Viv Pope to Neal Graneau:

‘From what I have read’, you say, ‘Viv assumes that while the interactions may be instantaneous, the observer delays are caused by a constant ¢ which relates distances and times.’ But where does c come in? In my view, c comes in as a pure, unembroidered fact of nature, first discovered empirically by Rémer, that afl observational distances are times in that particular ratio of units. To comprehend this, one has to ditch the traditional Democritan or mechanist conception of light, as something travelling, in favour of the Machian conception of light as what you see (the opposite of dark). In that way, the optical elements of which objects consist and of which our observations of them consist become one and the same, like the pixel-events which make up both the video scenario and the viewer’s perception of it. In other words, the paradigm switch is from a classical, mechanist to a Machian phenomenalist (information-based) approach to physics.

You argue that ‘the fact that c has a measurable value dictates that it has a physical cause’, and you attribute this to ‘the distribution of matter’. By contrast, you say, ‘Viv attributes this value to nothing, which is the same as attributing it to a vacuum.’ But why, Neal, does the fact that you can measure something mean that it has to have a physical cause? Interpreted in my/Bondi's way, c can be made to disappear simply by choosing different units, which makes its ‘cause’ a formality of mensuration, not something

physical. Moreover, how can it be that attributing a value to nothing is the same as attributing it to a vacuum?

Proposition 9: Some version or another of Mach’s principle applies

Although there was unanimous support for this proposition at the first Swansea Workshop, it would have been helpful for us to agree on specific versions of Mach’s principle first. Indeed, on-line discussions soon focussed on this very point. I have always thought that Mach’s principle essentially states that it is the ‘fixed stars’, by means of their masses, distribution and motion, which determine a local inertial frame. To me, this is the only practical way of determining an

inertial frame. Other participants had different ideas about Mach’s principle.

Peter Rowlands: The universal correlation between all quantum states can be thought of as a version of Mach’s principle and there is, in my opinion, only one way that it can be achieved in physical terms, and this is through an instantaneous gravitational action conveyed through an energy continuum such as is required by quantum mechanics and the weak interaction (the Higgs field). Neal Graneau: It seems that there are as many interpretations of Mach’s principle as there are physicists in the world and everyone (including the GR theorists!) seems to agree with it in principle. My version implies that the inertial force on particles and all bodies is due to their acceleration relative to all of the other particles and bodies in the universe.

Peter Graneau ; Mach suggested that the inertial interaction is, like Newtonian gravitation, a simultaneous matter interaction with particles in the distant universe.

Viv Pope As far as ] am concerned, Mach’s principle is a holistic principle according to which the existence, positions, and motions of all masses in the universe are inter-dependent.

Pope’s version of Mach’s principle is a more general version than that of the Graneaus and it was this version that gave rise to the evolved Proposition 9a below.

Proposition 9a: Mach’s principle (of universal connexivity of masses and their motions) applies

It can be seen from this amended form just why the proposition was of direct

relevance to previous discussions concerning holistic conservation laws. Indeed, it

can be argued that acceptance of holistic conservation laws implies agreement with this version of Mach’s principle. (See Propositions 2 and 3.) During later on- line discussions, Pope elaborated further on his position regarding Mach’s

principle.

Viv Pope:

For me, there is only one version of Mach’s principle on offer, which is that of Mach’s own basic philosophical stance towards physics. This is that physics should be based on what is sometimes called/miscalled the ‘Positivist Principle’, that only things and events that are capable of providing us with observational or instrumental data should signify in scientific explanations. Things, which are inexorably invisible, or doomed to remain purely theoretical, should be ruled out of account. The only spin- off from this, that all too few physicists can contemplate, is just the truncated view that all masses in the universe are connected together in some inscrutable way.

For most participants however, Mach’s general philosophy is not to be confused

with Mach’s principle, which is much more specific.

Neal Graneau to Viv Pope:

I think you are confusing Mach’s principle with your obviously very thorough understanding of Mach’s overall empirical philosophy. Mach’s principle as discussed by most scientists, is a more defined statement. Mach was directly repudiating Newton’s absolute space and therefore claimed that no acceleration was absolute. Therefore all accelerations were relative and thus every pair of objects in the universe had a relative acceleration. If two objects are separated and their relative acceleration is non-zero, which it always is, then there is by definition an equation that describes that acceleration and that is the definition of a vacuum force. So while Mach’s philosophy can be used to describe holistic and empirical concepts, Mach’s principle is precise and is the definition of relative accelerations and thus vacuum forces.

George Galeczki: I would like to circumscribe more clearly ‘Mach’s principle’ (a name which was in fact coined by Einstein). To my mind, one of the important statements of Mach was that physical laws are Jormulated in terms of relative positions and their time derivatives only. The IAAAD theories of Newton and Weber are in full accord with Mach’s requirement. It suffices to write the Newtonian equations of motion for two masses and to subtract one from the other, in order to obtain an equation of motion in terms of relative quantities only. This component of Mach’s principle deserves to be called kinematical relativity, since it contains only kinematically reciprocal

quantities, and has to be contrasted with ‘special’ relativity in which positions, velocities and accelerations are referred to some coordinate system. Thus, empiricist epistemology notwithstanding, the Machian formulation of Newton’s dynamics eliminates the ‘observer’ (which doesn’t interact with the real masses) and leaves only the interacting, real masses,

The other component of Mach’s principle refers to the origin of mass/inertia. According to Mach inertial mass is not an intrinsic property of matter, but is the result of the long-range, instantaneous interaction with all the masses in the (closed) Universe. ,

Viv Pope to George Galeczki:

I very much like your supportive account of the historical precedents for thinking in the way you and I do. Certainly, Mach's view was that physical laws are ail formulated in terms of relative positions and their time- derivatives. I also agree that this is true relativity (as opposed to Einstein's classically-compromised version) and that it is essentially kinematical. (My own distinctive version of this is called ‘cinematic relativity’ to point its neo-Machian flavour.)

You say that the Machian formulation of Newton's dynamics ‘eliminates the observer’. I would rather say that it eliminates the self as a unique informational focus. That is to say, whilst retaining an un-compromised scientific empiricism, Machian relationism spreads ‘observation’ into a Cartesian grid-space consisting of other observers and other observational instruments. Every piece of matter is an actual or potential ‘rudimentary observer’ in that sense.

This way, you retain empiricism without any danger of solipsism and you dispense with the sort of Rationalism which presumes to see, behind and between all the various bits of matter, a space with an existence and endurance of its own — with light travelling in it, of course, at an absolute and constant speed c.

I agree, also, that Mach's dynamics represents, as you say, the only true relativity theory, and that this explains Mach's hostility towards his errant philosophy pupil, Einstein. 1 can also concede that for the ordinary practical purposes of physics, the paradigm switch it represents from a matter-based to an information-based approach to the subject can largely be ignored. However, insofar as in our search for a new paradigm involves philosophy and lay commonsense as well as physics, all these inputs, I would say, need to be mobilised in seeking to articulate the anticipated New Paradigm.

The question of the origin of mass/inertia is still diagnostic. It is something to be argued at length, in later discussion. Peter Graneau and 1 will definitely have much to dispute about it.

The remaining discussion by email, which was mainly between Pope and

Galeczki, focused on whether or not Mach’s principle implies a finite and closed

universe. Galeczki’s view is that it does while Pope’s view is that this is not

necessarily the case.

George Galeczki to Viv Pope:

To close this issue I would ask your opinion about Mach’s principle implying a finite, closed-in-itself-Universe. According to some authorities — for example H. Hénl, J.A. Wheeler and Mendel Sachs — the answer is yes. For these people the main task of Mach’s principle is to make boundary conditions unnecessary.

To me, the self-closure is a most basic feature of a Machian Universe. In the spirit of the component of Mach’s principle related to the origin of inertia, there cannot be matter free from interaction with other matter ~ whether in the sense of gravitational interaction, or of ‘angular momentum correlation’ — which is tantamount to saying that Mach’s principle implies a closed-in-itself Universe.

However, experimental astrophysics provides no evidence of a closed and finite universe and for several reasons, I personally opt for an endless, boundless Universe, although this, in my opinion, is not faithfully Machian.

Viv Pope to George Galeczki:

I cannot see why Mach’s fundamental frame should be ‘closed-in’. I fancy there might be some very good arguments for thinking otherwise, and until these counter-arguments are exhaustively explored I can’t see what justification there can be for this claim. I cannot think of the universe as either closed or infinite since, in my approach, there is no *God’s-eye-view’.

Jon Blay:

Is it not possible that if the universe is fractal (not necessarily Dave Roscoe’s model) then at some scale the cluster of our particular known bit of the universe could be so distant (isolated) from all other clusters that Mach’s principle (apparently) requiring a closed universe nonetheless approximates well to the actual effects observed? Even if we identified the boundaries of the universe, it could presumably look like a vast cluster, and we could not be sure that there were not more clusters beyond the range of our detectors. In any case, I would have thought that if the universe was closed, you would get strange conditions at or near the boundary where a mass would inertiaily interact more strongly with the matter in one direction than with the ‘nothing’ in the other. Hence, I can’t help thinking that the apparent Machian need for a closed universe is erroneous.

Proposition 10: There is a universally preferred reference-frame for the

positions and motions of physical objects

The majority of participants supported this proposition since it is viewed as the

essence of Mach’s theory. However, there was no full agreement on what form

this preferred reference-frame should take, as the following responses reveal.

Martin Miller: Yes, of course there is a universally preferred frame of reference — the absolute one, being the centre of the point of gravity of all matter in the universe, although the orientation is uncertain and so left to the observer.

Peter Rowlands:

An energy continuum such as is required by quantum mechanics and the weak interaction would, indeed, constitute a universally preferred frame of reference for the positions and motions of objects.

(See Rowland’s response to Propositions 5 and 9.) Neal Graneau was the one participant who could see no necessity or even convenience in the concept of a

preferred reference frame.

Personally, | have never been happy with the idea of a universal, absolute reference frame, since there is no physical way of detecting the difference between this frame and other inertial frames. However, Mach’s principle seems to imply that such a frame exists and would be associated with the centre of mass of a supposedly closed (bounded and finite) universe. But I am yet to be convinced of the existence of such a universe. I am in agreement with Pope and Blay that Mach’s principle does not necessarily imply a bounded and finite universe (see

their views on Proposition 9a above).

Proposition 11: There is no distance, or space, except in relation to material objects

Some of the participants felt this proposition was obvious and required no further elaboration. In my opinion, it only makes physical sense to talk of spatial relations and distances in relation to material objects, especially in any approach to physics which is essentially observer-centred. In contrast, in mathematics, there is no problem in constructing spatial relations and distances that are only related to a fixed origin. Hence, we have to be careful to distinguish between mathematical structures in general and any mathematics, which in our opinion gives an adequate description of a particular area of physics. Two responses received are as follows. Neal Graneau:

In my vision of Machian dynamics, there is a measurable distance between every pair of particles. There are no fundamental units, but I can say that A

is twice as far from me as B and thereby establish an arbitrary scale. This is all I need to know about space. Consequently I agree with Proposition 11.

Peter Rowlands: Following my response to Proposition 10, it is energy that constitutes the universally preferred frame of reference, not space, and space is a discretely structured concept that only has meaning in relation to discrete objects with discrete charges.

The one dissenter was Martin Miiller, who could not agree if ‘material’ was confined to meaning ‘having a mass at rest’. In hindsight, we should have been careful to clarify what ‘material’ meant in this context. For example, Miiller would have agreed with the proposition if the term ‘material’ were general

enough to include electromagnetic waves.

Proposition 12: There is no time except in relation to processes involving material objects

Almost all the participants agreed with this proposition and, as in the case of the previous proposition, Miiller might have agreed if the meaning of ‘material’ was general enough. I believe that it only makes physical sense to think of the passage of time in relation to processes involving material objects, since otherwise we have no temporal frame of reference. Time, in a sense that is difficult to qualify, is a more physical measure than space. It is clear that spatial relations can be constructed mathematically. On the other hand, temporal relations cause problems for mathematics. The best we can do, it seems, is to represent such relations by other spatial relations, in a way that is entirely unsatisfactory. Here is Neal Graneau’s view.

Neal Graneau: Time appears to me to be nothing more than a man-made creation, invented to more easily discuss the relative frequencies of a collection of periodic events, such as orbits, pendulum swings, tides or oscillations.

During discussions, it emerged that we have to be very careful when talking of ‘time’, since the term can have more than one meaning. Time can be interpreted as observational time, i.e. duration, meaning a succession of states, which is

ultimately discrete. Time can also be taken to mean a continuous parameter, as in

the revolutions of the hands of a clock for example. Here are the views of

Galeczki, Rowlands and Pope.

George Galeczki:

When speaking about ‘time’ one has to differentiate between the time parameter which enters all dynamic laws of physics (such as Newton’s equation of motion, Maxwell’s equations and the law of radioactive decay) and, in the spirit of Mach and Aristotle, time as an epiphenomenon. The latter one belongs to the domain of time measurement — by means of time standards relying upon cyclic phenomena — and implies a discretisation process. The former one (the time parameter) implies an abstraction, namely a conceptual transition from discrete measurements to a continuous variable, which allows the application of calculus to physics. The same happens while extrapolating from discrete, empirical distances, to the concept of length and continuous space. The bottom line is that your discrete unit of time can change, but the continuous time parameter can neither dilate, nor contract. Most important, ‘time’ in the sense of continuous time, is unidimensional, extending indefinitely forward and backward,

Peter Rowlands:

I agree with Proposition 12, for very specific reasons. The proposition is ambiguously worded and needs careful elaboration. I believe that irreversible time, unlike space, is a continuum, which is why Zeno’s paradoxes occur, and why we have wave-particle duality. It is, in effect, the preserver of causality, despite delayed action mechanisms, and its continuity can be linked to that of the vacuum. J take the proposition to be true to the extent that the four fundamental parameters (space, time, mass and charge) are meaningless except in relation to each other, but I do not take it to mean that we should define time in an operational sense, and make it discrete like space. We should realise that time, unlike space, can never be directly measured.

Viv Pope:

Light, according to my thesis, is both instantaneous and delayed. Its instantaneous component has no time but only length (i.e., the macroscopic distance between interacting atoms in metres) and its delay component has no length but only time in seconds, or rather, nanoseconds 1/v, where v is the light-quantum’s spectral frequency, e/h. I say ‘delay’ here, but that is not quite apt, since the quantum time 1/v is irreducible; that is, it is not like the time measured by the revolutions of the moon or the hands of a clock, of which there are always discernible subdivisions. Nevertheless, it is those irreducible measures e/h, which in macro-statistical amounts, constitutes time as we know it. And (pace Mach) there is no time other than what is reducible to those irreducible events.

Proposition 13: Instantaneity/simultaneity is universal (i.e. transitive)

This was the second contentious issue, with only half of the participants agreeing. Explicitly, the proposal is that if an event A is instantaneous/simultaneous with an event B and 8B is instantaneous/simultaneous with an event C, then A is instantaneous/simultaneous with event C. Here are the views of two of the supporters of the statement. In Neal Graneau’s view, it is inertia that is the

mechanism which prevents cosmic gridlock under universal instantaneity.

Neal Graneau: Time appears to me to be nothing more than a man made creation, invented to more easily discuss the relative frequencies of a collection of periodic events. With such a simple view of time, and a belief in literal IAAAD, then instantaneity is indeed universal and inertia is the mechanism which prevents the entire trajectory of the universe from happening all at once. My vision of inertia allows motion to happen smoothly as predicted by Newtonian dynamics, not in the quantised stills that have been suggested by Viv Pope.

George Galeczki All three holistic conservation laws — those for linear and angular momentum and for energy — require in my view transitive, rather than non- transitive distant simultaneity. In other words conservation laws for extended systems require universal time (i.e. position and velocity independent time).

Peter Graneau was also a supporter of the proposition. He stated that if it were not transitive, we would once more have to revert to Einsteinian local action. However, I do not think that this is necessarily so. I agree with Viv Pope in believing that action can be both instantaneous and delayed (see Proposition 16

below). I disagree with Proposition 13 for much the same reasons as Pope does.

Viv Pope: From my agreement with Proposition 12, it follows that in any instance where an atom B absorbs (directly) a quantum of action nh from an atom A, atom B cannot, in that self-same event, also emit that same quantum to another atom C. (Receiving and giving away are two events, not one.) This means that the instantaneous linkages between atoms into the sequences of which time analyses, cannot be transitive. That is, there cannot be — at least, at the ultimate microphysical level ~ any universal, or non-local, single present-moment (if there were, then it would be a case of ‘cosmic gridlock’.) Between one instantaneous connection of A with B and the next

instantaneous connection between B and C, there is an indeterminate non- connection involving an indeterminate time-delay at B, which is taken by that mediating particle in passing the ‘quantum baton’, as it were. It follows then that all time, as we know it on the macro-level, consists of statistical numbers of these basic, one-for-one, unsharable and therefore intransitive quantum-interactions.

Jon Blay also supports Pope’s reasoning. "

Jon Blay:

It seems reasonable to assume that all observed phenomena must, ultimately, be wholly explainable by fundamental, irreducible quantised phenomena. If all quantum interactions were wholly time-delayed, then there could be no holistic conservation laws of any kind. If all quantum interactions were wholly instantaneous, then all possible sequences of quantum interactions would presumably happen in an instant, and therefore there could be no such thing as time or a universe. Therefore, if all quantum phenomena share the same characteristics, then all quantum interactions must have both a time-delayed and an instantaneous component, as Viv proposes.

However, I do believe that this is a difficult proposition to contemplate simply because there is more than one aspect (the instantaneous and the time-delayed) to action-at-a-distance. Hence, I can appreciate how Peter Rowlands can both agree

and disagree with the proposition.

Peter Rowlands:

This proposition is another tricky one. There are two meanings to this idea. For one, my answer is yes and for the other one, my answer is no. The proposition has to be true for the non-local situation involved in the uncollapsed wavefunction, but it cannot be true where we are concerned with a collapsed wavefunction or delayed action measurement. I think that this is a case where the naive model-dependent view of an interaction as a transfer of a physical object, such as a quantum, causes problems. To my way of looking at it, it depends on which aspect of the nilpotent we are looking at.

As with most of the other propositions, discussion continued through email after

the July 2001 meeting.

Viv Pope to George Galeczki: About the only thing I can find to disagree with you on George, is when you claim that holistic conservation demands that instantaneous energy/angular momentum connections are transitive. As I see it, in a situation where everything is instantly connected, not only would any adjustment at any

place instantly effect everything else throughout, but also the converse would be the case, that no adjustment could be made, because in having to move anything one would have to move everything, in which case, everything would be jammed solid — the theoretical situation of an Eleatic stasis. Only by breaking the continuity of connection, at the atomic quantum level, into discrete units in the way I have described, can this stasis, in my view, be avoided.

By the way, Mary (Pope) asked me how the distant angular momentum connections between galaxies, say, can be instantaneous if the atomic ‘baton’ is intransitive, which must imply a delay. My answer, which Mary was happy with, was that the atomic interaction is not necessarily between atoms that are arranged in series, smal! distances apart. It may be between atoms that are separated, on the macro-phenomenal level, by intergalactic distances. In that instance, the galaxies are as instantly connected as are their respective atoms — remembering that in Machian philosophy, distance is a macro-phenomenon, which does not exist at the micro-phenomenal level.

Neal Graneau:

Viv states ‘If everything did consist of just one single, universally transitive event, there could be no succession of instants of the sort that constitutes time. All there would be would be a single gigantic ‘flash’ of existence with no before or after, which is totally inconceivable.’ Peter Graneau presented the Machian calculation of instantaneous Newtonian gravitational interactions leading to the observed force of inertia at the first Swansea Workshop. Inertia is precisely the concept that separates force from the infinite accelerations that so worry Viv. Similarly, I discussed how the inertia of current elements leads to the delays related to the concept of c. Thus the argument is that the forces are transitive in time but the accelerations are affected by distances, masses and charges relating every pair of particles in the universe.

Proposition 14: Critical arguments and hypotheses should be stated clearly and ‘ unambiguously, so as to be unequivocally refutable by logical/ mathematical tests of consistency and/or experimental observation (Popper’s criterion)

This was another proposition for which the participants were 100% in agreement. Indeed, it would be difficult to argue against it, at least in principle. Any truly scientific approach should ideally be conducted in this way. Clearly, any scientific hypothesis must stand or fall by logical and/or mathematical tests of consistency and experimental observation. We can never know whether a proposed theory in

physics is a true ‘description’ of nature in any sense. But we can certainly reject

any theory which is logically inconsistent or which does not agree with known

experimental evidence. My view is echoed in Pope’s response.

Viv Pope:

A true scientific dialectic cannot be conducted in any other way. As Popper was well aware, no thesis can be refuted simply by stating another thesis (the classic fallacy called ponendo tollens). To reduce a surplus of theses, some of them need to be refuted, so that Physics does not become what the logician W. V. O Quine called a ‘conceptual slum’. This dialectical clearing-away of bogus concepts, is, by the use of ‘Ockham’s razor’, to shave off ali the hoary theoretical accretion which logical argument reveals to be either unnecessary, equivocal, internally contradictory, or at odds with observation and experiment.

Although it was agreed that this Popperian methodology is the procedure that should always be adopted, some of the participants recognised that, in practice, it is sometimes difficult to adhere to certain aspects of it, as illustrated by the

following two responses.

Peter Rowlands: One cannot argue, in principle, with Proposition 14, but we need to be careful when making a distinction with two apparently opposing theories, that they are truly A and Not A. We can only decide that at the fundamental level, and many of the cases put forward (for example, waves versus particles, Schrödinger versus Heisenberg) are no such thing. We must also avoid confusing the richness of using different formalisms with the production of mutually opposed theories. J have developed an overall theory based on a group symmetry between four parameters which is absolutely exact and uncompromising, but this mathematical structure also implies a ‘dual’ set, in which some of the properties are reversed. Mathematically, this is possible, and often convenient — as in the traditional Dirac formalism — but we are always forced back eventually into the ‘canonical’ form of the group. Only an accepted fundamental theory will lead to ‘final closure’ so we have to keep discussing the issues. Neal Graneau:

I agree that the Popperian methodology represents a desirable procedure, but as we discovered in the July Workshop, it is not always easy, nor is it necessarily how the human brain works best, so I feel we must allow a bit of flexibility in our procedures.

Proposition 15: Another conference/workshop needs to be convened to iron out the remaining differences

Most participants of the first Swansea Workshop were of the opinion that convening another workshop in order to continue with discussions and to give others a chance to attend would be fruitful. Some were interested to see how the debate would evolve. I was of the opinion that a second workshop should not be held too soon in order for participants to reflect on the first workshop and to have sufficient time to prepare for the next. I was, and still remain, pessimistic concerning the possibility of any workshop reaching anything more than a general consensus of views on UIAAAD. However, I was optimistic that another workshop could, with some re-wording, lead to almost unanimous agreement on at least 13 of the propositions (excluding the more contentious Propositions 3 and 13) and reach agreement on further propositions. The following are two typical views of participants. George Galeczki:

I have serious doubts that the first Swansea Workshop achieved its goal,

namely to be a round-table discussion in which all participants actively take

part and are ready to adjust their initial beliefs according to the better

understanding resulting from the open discussions. Therefore I’m for a second workshop, perhaps in Spring 2002.

Peter Rowlands:

Only an accepted fundamental theory will lead to ‘final closure’ so we have to keep discussing the issues. We have a fair body of agreement, and some of the areas of apparent disagreement may be artefacts of interpretation and verbal issues rather than truly fundamental ones.

The discussions at Swansea were intense and sometimes a bit fierce, but it was worth it to get a productive consensus, and I think we should definitely continue.

In view of all the responses received in support of a second workshop, the organisers, Viv and Mary Pope, agreed to arrange another one. This workshop was again held at Swansea, in April 2002. Here is a summary of Viv Pope’s own response. Viv Pope: I agree that, despite the undoubted advances made, at the July Workshop, in the direction of a consensus, there was, as Peter Graneau put it, ‘no final

closure’, But there was, by all accounts, sufficient intellectual momentum gained to warrant a least one more meeting. This follow-up workshop

should be convened in the not-too-distant future for the purpose of settling, in particular, the most contentious issue surrounding Proposition 13.

Peter Graneau was the one participant who was uncertain about a follow-up

Workshop. His reasons are reproduced below.

Peter Graneau: The differences you wish to iron out are not worth the effort of arranging another meeting in Swansea. What we should address is a new physics paradigm based on IAAAD. To me the two most important questions are: (1) How do we explain radiation time delays without the flight of energy? (2) How can we eliminate the electromagnetic field from quantum mechanics?

As I see it, the object of these workshops is to produce a book in which some twelve authors explain why they believe the next physics paradigm, to supersede field theory, will have to be based on IAAAD. In the course of this they will describe what areas of physics are already under the AAAD umbrella and what other areas have to be filled in with new theories.

We have IAAAD theories of gravitation and inertia, including universal angular momentum conservation, and the electrodynamics of metals. What are missing are appropriate theories of electromagnetic radiation, particle- accelerator experiments, and a substantial section of magnetism. This covers, perhaps, ninety percent of all physics. It seems to me we cannot address the physics community at large without such a book. Nor can we ask for their help in the filling-in process.

The last proposition

One of the most fundamental questions discussed at the July 2001 (i.e., the first) Workshop was that of whether actions at a distance are instantaneous or time- delayed. (See Proposition 13 above.) All the participants were believers in IAAAD, so did this imply that all participants did not believe that actions-at-a- distance were also time-delayed? Remember, as indicated above, 67% were in agreement with Proposition 8, that the constant ¢ entails time-delay of our observations of distant events. The debate on this issue continued at the April 2002 Workshop. The organiser, Viv Pope and others, including myself, support the premise that action can be both instantaneous and time-delayed. It is all a matter of perspective. Accordingly, Pope added one further proposition to the

original 15 at the second meeting, as follows.

Proposition 16: Instantaneous and time-delayed action together is not a logical contradiction

The majority of the participants at the second workshop supported this view, Neal Graneau being the main dissenter. As Pope pointed out, instantaneous and time- delayed action can be conceived without contradiction as components of the same interaction. This is made explicit in papers by Pope and myself, which were published in Physics Essays.” Here is Pope’s view, as disseminated by email after the April 2002 Workshop.

Viv Pope:

There has been a misunderstanding which, from the very start of these workshop discussions has kept the group split into two seemingly irreconcilable factions. This vitally concerns whether actions at a distance are instantaneous or time-delayed. The root of the misunderstanding lies in an assumption that these two alternatives are logically exclusive. This makes it seem as though the aim of the debate is to ascertain, decisively, whether action-at-a-distance is either instantaneous or time-delayed. Any suggestion that a distant interaction may be both instantaneous and time-delayed therefore continues to be neglected by a whole section of the group on the mistaken assumption that this has to be a complete contradiction. That this is not so, however, is easily demonstrated. Take the statement: ‘Things that are apart can also be together’. This /ooks like a contradiction but it is not. A set of objects that are together in one dimension may, in that same instance, be separate in another. For example, although the characters in this sentence are separated in the lateral dimension of the page, they are ail collapsed together for anyone who views the page from its edge, with the sentence seen end-on.

By that same logic, a set of events, which occur together in a purely spatial dimension, may be separate in time. For example, in what movie directors call ‘action’, an archer, his bow, the target and the arrow are all present together, simultaneously, in each photographic frame or ‘still’; yet in the action of the movie the arrow takes a finite time to fly from the bow to the target. In this case it is plainly no contradiction to say that the action has both instantaneous and time-delayed components. Every item in the Scenario is presented at the same instant in the dimensions of the stills and yet every movement in the action itself takes place at a finite speed.

After the second workshop, George Galeczki, who was unable to be present at that meeting, and who does not support SR or time-delayed action, made the

following reply by email.

George Galeczki: In my view, there is no ‘misunderstanding’ to be reconciled here; one . alternative (SR) is simply non-existent.

Conclusion

All participants agreed that all the discussions had been thought provoking and very fruitful. It was felt that a consensus had been reached on all but two of the propositions, i.e., Propositions 3 and 13. Primarily, agreement was reached on the premise that UIAAAD was due to holistic conservations laws, the existence of which is implied by Mach’s principle of universal connexivity of masses and their motions. The chairman of the first Swansea Workshop, Jon Blay, gave this summary some time after the first meeting. “As we all know, the basis on which this Workshop was convened was a hundred percent agreement as to the existence of UIAAAD. Agreed also was that UIAAAD implies a holism in which the positions and motions of material particles are dependent on one another, regardless of distance. The only differences that have arisen are those concerning

how, or by what means, these holistic influences are conveyed.’

The discussions at both workshops were clearly split on the issue of whether it is angular momentum in itself that accounts for the observed correlated orbital motions of bodies, that is, without Newtonian in vacuo ‘gravitational force’, or whether Newtonian rectilinear momentum (inertia) together with Newtonian ‘gravitational force’ is responsible for orbital motion and action-at-a- distance. (In the former case, it is conservation of angular momentum that is responsible for UIAAAD and in the latter, it is conservation of linear momentum.) Supporters of the first approach are mainly Viv Pope, Jon Blay, Peter Rowlands, Martin Kokus and myself (Anthony Osborne). Supporters of the second approach appear to be mainly George Galeczki, Neal and Peter Graneau, David Roscoe, and

to some extent, Peter Kummel.

The last word in this chapter goes to the discussion coordinator of both Swansea Workshops. After the first workshop had taken place, Pope proposed the

following compromise arising from the discussions. Viv Pope:

Let us start with an ideal Newtonian universe in which all the various bits of mass are almost infinitely far from one another. Applying Mach’s principle to that would mean that all those bits of matter behave more or less in accordance with Newton’s laws of motion (omitting his gravitational law which does not pertain for such far-separated objects), and that they are holistically connected in the way that the Graneaus describe. Mach and Newton are now, to that extent, united. And so are Neal Graneau, Galeczki, Osborne and myself. Things have energy, which is conserved in the way Galeczki would want and they also have angular momentum, which in this case, with the distance between the masses being virtually infinite is also virtually infinite. Such motions as there may be in this situation are also virtually rectilinear in the way Galeczki and Neal Graneau require, and these rectilinear motions are also curved, albeit to an extent that, in the situation described, can virtually be discounted. This, I suggest, combines Galeczki, the Graneaus, Pope and Osborne, at least in something like a single agreed starting-point.

But now I suggest that in view of the overall-conserved nature of Galeczki’s energy and the same for angular momentum, we have the logical consequence that inertia, as the Graneaus conceive it in the Machian mode, and the angular momentum as Pope and Osborne conceive it in that same Machian mode (inertia and angular momentum being virtually indistinguishable in that ideal situation) make ‘gravitation’ redundant. After all, why would we want ‘gravitation’ to hold everything together when that same holistic connection is already assured by inertia/energy/angular- momentum? That holism itself, as Galeczki has concurred, ensures that if the motion (inertia/energy/angular-momentum) of something changes in any one place, then some compensatory change must also occur somewhere else, where the phase-conditions of the motion are just right. And in order to avoid an Eleatic stasis,’ that change cannot take place all over but only between specific in-phase trajectories, which means that the inertia/ energy/ angular momentum has to be ultimately quantised.
