Now let’s suppose that in drifting around in a virtually rectilinear way, two of the masses approach each another to the extent that the distance between them is no longer virtually infinite.* This means that the angular momentum between those two masses is no longer infinite; hence that their motion is no longer virtually rectilinear. This means, in turn, that the inertia is no longer rectilinear either. Notice that there is still no need to talk about any ‘gravitation’ being responsible for this. Nor do we need to ponder as to how this ‘inertial’ influence which the bodies have on each other in adjusting their motions in this sympathetic way is ‘conveyed’, because that ‘influence’ is already assured by the holism of the Machian principle. In any case, consistent with Machian philosophy, there can be no ‘vacuum’ or ‘void’ for any such ‘influence’ to travel through or be conducted by (since, of course, in Mach there is no space, or distance except in relation to

matter), hence no need for any talk of ‘force-fields’, ‘ethers’ or whatever for the conduct of anything between the masses — on which, at least, Neal Graneau, Osborne and myself are agreed.

This, of course, does not give us UIAAAD at a stroke, but it gives us a common basis from which to argue.

' The logical alternative to interpreting ¢ as a ‘velocity’ is as described by Herman Bondi as foliows:

Any attempt to measure the velocity of light is ... not an attempt at measuring the velocity of light but an attempt at ascertaining the length of the standard metre in Paris in terms of time-units.

Bondi, H.: Assumption and Myth in Physical Theory, 28 (Cambridge University Press, 1965).

2 Pope, N.V and Osborne. A.D., ‘Instantaneous Relativistic Action-at-a-Distance’, Physics Essays, 5, 3, 460-488 (1992) and ‘Instantaneous Gravitational and Inertial Action-at-a-Distance’, Phys. Ess., 8, 3, 384-397 (1995).

3 The philosophers of the ancient Greek town of Elea argued that if there is no such thing as separation {in the form of a true void, or vacuum) between bits of matter, then for anything to move it would also have to move everything else. As they reasoned it, therefore, without there being a void or, literally, a nothing separating things, motion would be impossible; everything would be in a state of cosmical gridlock. The leading Eleatic, Parmenides, had argued that since (as he saw it) to say that a nothing exists is a contradiction, it follows that everything that truly exists is one, indivisible and immovable. In this way was created the notion of what was called ‘The Eleatic One’. In opposition to this, Democritus argued that since motion plainly is possible, the void or the ‘nothing’ must really exist. In this way, Democritus ‘shattered the Eleatic One’ into bits, which he called ‘atoms’. These had all the characteristics of the Eleatic One, each being without distinguishable parts, hence internally unchanging and unmoving. All motion was thenceforward conceived as being not internal to the One but between the Many.

4 A reader may ask: ‘If angular momentum changes in this way, then how can it also be conserved?’ The answer is that in a many-body system of the sort here described, the loss of the angular momentum of a mass m with respect to one countermass M is automatically compensated by an equivalent gain in angular momentum with respect to some other countermass (or masses) M,, M; ... etc., so that overall angular momentum of the system is conserved. This answers to the phenomenon of perturbation, as witnessed in the case of the planets of our solar system, where any change in the orbital trajectories of any one body with respect to any other affects, to some extent or other, the trajectories of all the rest. This is the principle Pope uses in his argument to explain

Chapter 5: A Tale of Two Paradigms

Viv Pope

or From Atomism Towards Holism in Modern Physics

Modern physical science is no settled state of understanding. It is a theoretical war-zone, a deep-rooted, unresolved conflict between two historical paradigms. In the one, which we may call the Cartesian, or ‘French’, paradigm, light is an intermediary between material objects and our knowledge of them. In the other — let’s call it the ‘English’ paradigm (from the so-called ‘English Empiricism’ of Locke, Berkeley and Hume) — all time and space are in the light, in the sense of the word ‘light’ meaning the opposite of dark, the light being, in that sense, the source of all scientific knowledge of physical reality. We maintain that the long- standing conflict between these two radical alternatives, fuelled by Einstein’s ambivalent rendering of relativity in terms of the ‘speed of light in vacuo’, is the root cause of the current confusion in Modern Physics and that with the removal of this conceptual stumbling-block, progress towards true resolution may be

resumed.

5.1 Light in space or space in light?

Radical ideas always generate reactions. Sooner or later, however, if an idea is sound, resistance to it is overcome and the new idea becomes generally

accepted.

In some cases, however, the conflict remains unresolved, leaving both the new idea and the old in an uncomfortable and prolonged state of un-peaceful co- existence. When this happens, it suggests that there may be some fundamental flaw in the new idea which inhibits the normal process of rational, commonsense

resolution.

We propose that such may well be the case with Einstein’s ingenious Relativistic theory, which is supposed to have replaced the equally ingenious Mechanistic theory of Galileo and Newton. The fact that, for now nigh on a century, so many scientists still have reservations about this transition, also that commonsense has steadfastly refused to accommodate it, makes us think that there must be some deep flaw in Einstein’s theory which impedes natural progress

in our understanding.

It is here argued that this flaw lies in Einstein’s classical interpretation of the constant ¢ as ‘the velocity of light in vacuo’, which over-complicates the theory and, with its vestiges of classical ‘God’s-eye-view’ absolutism, generates paradoxes. We show that setting aside this Einsteinian interpretation of ¢ as a basis of relativity, in favour of Bondi’s non-velocity interpretation of the constant as simply a dimensional conversion factor unblocks the route to a natural acceptance of the revolutionary relativism of Einstein’s philosophical mentor, Mach. The implications of this ‘Copernican flip-over’ from thinking of light as something travelling invisibly in space to thinking of it as, first and foremost, what we see (that is, as epistemologically a priori), are here shown to be crucial to the whole issue of action-at-a-distance. The logical implications of this ‘Copernican revolution’ from mechanistic (‘analog’) to informational (quantum- digital) physics promise to be every bit as socially traumatic as the historical transition from flat-earth to round-earth geography, or from earth-centred to sun- centred planetary theory. Such revolutions may be expected to offend the sensitivities of cautious conservative thinkers. Indeed, it might be said that unless it produces consequences that are capable of causing such offence, the

‘revolutionary’ idea is trivial.

However, a fear of such offence cannot suspend progress forever. Sooner or later, common understanding must move on. This is manifestly what is not happening at present. The impediment is plain. It is that matter cannot sensibly be thought of as existing both self-sufficiently, known to observation only indirectly,

via the agency of light, in the way Descartes imagined,' and relatively to the

observer in the way Einstein’s mentor, Mach, envisaged. Einstein’s ‘compromise’ solution, of bridging the gulf with space-travelling light-corpuscles simply doesn’t work. In Mach’s notorious rejection of Einstein’s theory, in 1921, he warned that the theory could never amount to more than a ‘temporary inspiration’ in the history of science. We disagree. We think that like the Copernican revolution, Relativity is here to stay — but not in the form which separates and isolates masses within a self-sufficient void, united by nothing but chance encounters with wandering waves and photons. Relationism implies holism, and holism implies immediate and instantaneous interconnection. A ‘Relativity’, therefore, in which the bits of matter are separated in the way Einstein imagined simply has to be modified if it is to be logically consistent. This modification, we argue, needs to be in the direction pointed by Bondi and Mach, in which observational interrelations with the physical world are described in direct, information-

theoretical terms, not in terms of a mediating ‘light-velocity’.

5.2 What is a paradigm?

The word ‘paradigm’ has become fashionable nowadays to describe just about any and every new idea that comes along. However, for the purposes of the Swansea Workshop we needed to be reminded of what a ‘paradigm’ means,

behind all the various fashionable misuses of the word.

The true meaning of a scientific paradigm is as follows. In 1962, Thomas Samuel Kuhn, an American historian of science, published a book called The Structure of Scientific Revolutions, regarded as one of the most influential works in the history of scientific philosophy. In his first book, The Copernican Revolution (1957), Kuhn studied the development of the heliocentric theory of the solar system during the Renaissance. This led to his second book, The Structure of Scientific Revolutions, in which he argued that different kinds of scientific research and thought fall into classifications which he called ‘paradigms’. These are conceptual world-views consisting of ‘formal theories, classic experiments, and trusted methods’. Typically, he said, professional scientists accept a

prevailing paradigm and seek to extend its scope “by refining theories, explaining

puzzling data, and establishing more precise measures of standards and phenomena’. However, because human knowledge is essentially open-ended and uncertain, their efforts may, on occasions, generate insoluble theoretical problems or experimental anomalies that expose fundamental inadequacies in a paradigm, or they may even contradict it altogether. This accumulation of difficulties eventually triggers a crisis that can be resolved only by an intellectual revolution that completely replaces an old paradigm with a new one. For example, the overthrow of Ptolemaic cosmology by Copernican heliocentrism, and the displacement of Newtonian mechanics by quantum physics and General Relativity (GR) are examples of major paradigm shifts. This prompted Kuhn to question the traditional conception of scientific progress as a gradual accumulation of knowledge based on rationally chosen experimental frameworks. Instead, he argued that the paradigm determines the way scientists think, the kinds of experiments they perform, the types of questions they ask and the problems they deem important. A radical shift in the paradigm alters the fundamental concepts underlying research and inspires new standards of evidence, new techniques of research and new pathways of theory and experiment that are radically

incommensurate with the old ones.

The prime example of a paradigm change is the heliocentric revolution on which Kuhn based his analysis. An even earlier one was, of course, the change from thinking of the world as flat to thinking of it as round. Now a later and similar paradigm change that Kuhn cited took place from the absolutistic physics of Galileo and Newton to the relativistic or observer-centred physics of Einstein. However, it has to be said that this paradigm change has fallen short of complete consummation. The fundamental sticking-point in this supposed ‘paradigm shift’ is not only its lack of that commonsense-logical appeal which typified the round- earth and sun-centred revolutions; it is also the failure of Einsteinian relativism to encompass that whole section of contemporary science called ‘quantum physics’. All attempts at synthesising these two have so far been unnatural and contrived,

with none of the general, commonsense-logical appeal of the true revolutions

achieved by Columbus and Copernicus. Rather, the ‘paradigm changes’ that are claimed have been confined to sectors of specialised physics which hold out little hope of ever involving commonsense to anything like the extent of the former

revolutions.

This chronic hang-up in the development of modern physics has led to the expectation, among many science commentators, of a new and truly paradigmic, lateral thinking alternative to what is now commonly identified as ‘classical physics’. So although there may be, as yet, little or no idea of what shape the anticipated ‘New Paradigm’ might take, at least we are all too familiar with the shape of the old one. This is the paradigm of Mechanical Materialism, which

Encyclopaedia Britannica describes as follows:-

Mechanical Materialism is the theory that the world consists entirely of hard, massy material objects, which, though perhaps imperceptibly small, are otherwise like such things as stones. (A slight modification is to allow the void — or empty space — to exist also in its own right.) These objects interact in the sort of way that stones do: by impact and possibly also by gravitational attraction. The theory denies that immaterial or apparently immaterial things (such as minds) exist or else explains them away as being material things or motions of material things.

The ultimate expression of this mechanist-materialist paradigm is, of course,

Newtonian mechanics: 3

Newton's general dynamic theory, as expounded in his Philosophia Naturalis Principia Mathematica of 1687, laid down in the form of his laws of motion, together with other axioms and postulates, the rules to follow in analyzing the motion of bodies interacting among themselves.

The key phrase, here, is ‘among themselves’. Matter and its mechanisms were thus conceived by Newton as absolutely real and self-sufficient, existing completely apart from and independently of ‘observation’ as modern relativists understand the word. In this he was simply following his French predecessor, Descartes (1596-1650), who introduced into Western science the mind-matter dichotomy that began with the Greek atomist, Democritus, Thus, in his (Descartes’) imagination, there were two ‘realities’. One, he postulated, was the world of matter ‘as God sees it’, while the other was the completely separate

world of human perception, thought and feeling.

An essential accompaniment of this mechanistic way of thinking is that of ‘light’ as a quasi-mechanical intermediary between these two worlds. One of the first to think of light in this quasi-mechanistic way was the Greek philosopher Empedocles (5 century BC). Based on this Empedociean conception, light became regarded as a sort of ‘streaming substance’ emitted by atoms to strike, by pure chance, other atoms, including those in the eye of the observer. To ask what this ‘streaming substance’ consisted of when travelling on its own in space between its emitter and absorber was to be answered — quite literally, so far as these mechanists were concerned ~ by saying ‘God knows’.

5.3 The ill-fated ‘English Empiricism’

For later philosophers, this two-worlds view of Descartes was nonsense. It seemed to them that the only way to restore the integrity of our knowledge of nature was to dispense entirely with Descartes’ directly unknowable ‘world of matter’ and accept the one we actually perceive as the one and only real world we can ever know and sensibly talk about. Unfortunately, in a classic ‘Catch- Twenty-Two’ situation, the English empiricists, Locke, Berkeley and Hume, in all their efforts to establish such an alternative, had accepted without question the very dichotomy they were trying to remove. It left them with a completely truncated view of knowledge in which nothing exists beyond perception, a view which could not be reconciled with the aim of nascent physical science. This science had no less unquestioningly accepted the Cartesian dichotomy by choosing the opposite course, which was to penetrate the barrier Descartes had placed between us and the world as it exists independently of our perceiving it. Thus was created the long-standing, so-called ‘Problem of Knowledge’ (or Epistemological Problem), which was the completely bogus question of how Mind, the subject of philosophy and psychology, can ever be certain even of the existence of Matter, the subject of studies like physics and mechanics. An almost total preoccupation with this ‘problem’ has stunted Western academic

Philosophy right up to the early part of the twentieth century.

What was, originally, natural philosophy thus became split into the two factions, Physics and Philosophy, as these are academically distinguished today. In the way it was posed, the problem of mending this Cartesian split has proved insoluble. The manner in which the English Empiricists failed in their attempts to solve this problem may be illustrated with reference to the biblical tale of Solomon, who was called upon to judge a case of two women claiming to be mother of the same child. In his renowned wisdom, Solomon declared that the child should be cut in two and divided between the two women. The one who then hastily renounced her claim to the child thereby signified that she was the true mother. In contrast, in the case of Descartes the ‘infant’, Natural Philosophy, was actually cut in two, with both the Materialists, and the Empiricists revealing the falsity of their claims to true maternity by each of them settling for half a ‘dead child’.

This continued failure to solve the problem of dualism clearly falls under the heading of Kuhn’s fundamental inadequacies in an existing paradigm. For instance, vestiges of that bogus Mind-Matter dichotomy can still be seen in the ongoing conflict among modern physicists between the self-styled ‘realists’ (the mechanistic traditionalists) and those they disparagingly refer to as ‘mentalists’, or ‘subjective idealists’. Some physicists of the hard-line ‘realist’ persuasion, calling themselves ‘Dissidents’, reject even standard relativism and quantum

mechanics as belonging in the ‘idealist’ category.

This continuing conflict, however, takes no account of the fact that the bogus Cartesian dichotomy no longer exists. It was quietly despatched, in the 1920s, by a group of philosophers who came more or less independently to the same conclusion, that Cartesian dualism had been a fallacy. This meant that there was no such thing as the ‘Problem of Knowledge’ that Descartes had created. All the scholarly attempts to solve that ‘Problem’ had been so much wasted effort.

The philosophers so convinced were, mainly, G. E. Moore, the later L. Wittgenstein and the later A. J. Ayer, plus G. Ryle and J. L. Austin. So far,

however, the artificial but now socially established division between Arts and

Science (itself a relic of Cartesian dualism), has prevented this ‘Eureka!’ of philosophical discovery from percolating through into contemporary physics, where the ‘God’s-Eye-View’ Realism of the traditional Mechanists and the equally truncated ‘Logical Positivist’ reaction to it still compete with each other for monopoly in the name of ‘Science’. Briefly, this ‘Eureka’ of philosophical realisation was that so far as human knowledge is concerned, our ideas of what a thing is and what that thing is in itself, ‘behind those ideas’, are never two things but one. So, what had been classically conceived as “The Problem’ of fitting our ideas of physical reality to that physical reality itself had never been anything more than the problem of fitting one set of ideas to another, which is a different

problem entirely from what had been imagined since Descartes.

There is, therefore, no question of there being some sort of ‘Mental’ paradigm to oppose the traditional ‘Materialist’? one. The whole dichotomy between ‘matter’ and ‘mind’ is now (in Philosophy, at any rate) no more than an archaism, so that physicists who continue to squabble over ‘Positivism versus Realism’ are a dying breed. In any event, in the search for the anticipated ‘New Paradigm’ there can be no question of either the ‘Realist’ or the ‘Idealist’ approaches to physics prevailing one against the other. This means that the way forward, from now on, has to be by means of some completely different way of

thinking which avoids this bogus dichotomy altogether.

5.4 An ahistorical triple bypass

Like many organisms, as a society ages, its ‘arteries’ harden, leading to the typical loss of efficiency that precedes a decline and fall. The same goes for any system of ideas that fails to adapt to changing circumstances. We should be thankful, therefore, that throughout our social history, there have been revolutions in thinking, of the kind Kuhn mentions, which, despite the traumas they created at the time, if they had not been eventually incorporated into our ways of thinking would have signalled the end of our evolutionary line. (Imagine, for instance, how we would have competed with some modern space-age society if we had

remained adhered to a traditional flat-earth geography or geocentric cosmology!)

Think, then, of how it would be if, from now on, there were no new, radical ideas beyond those of contemporary theoretical physics as the current media describe it. ‘What a dreary prospect!’ many might say — reflecting on how little interest the common man has in the subject and how few students, nowadays

are inspired to choose Theoretical Physics as a vocation.

There can be little doubt, then, that our physical science has aged. All the signs are there that it is in imminent danger of collapse, right at the very conceptual heart of the current paradigm where the main arteries of thought are all but completely blocked with the plaque of an age-hardening tradition. What seems to be needed, then, is what might be called a logical bypass of those conceptual traditions, the ones that have created our present confusions. There are three logical ways in which such a bypass might be facilitated. These are as

follows.

5.4.1 The first bypass: Pythagoras to Einstein

The answer to those problems in theoretical physics that have been created by Cartesian dualism should, logically, have been provided by Einsteinian relativity in linking the observer and physical reality directly together. Unfortunately for that transition, in the judgement of Einstein’s philosophy teacher, Mach, Einstein ‘blew it’ In relativity as Mach taught it, the absolute Cartesian dichotomy between the world of matter and the world of the observer breaks down completely and is replaced by an interpretation of matter (mass) as being indescribable other than in direct relation to a specified point of direct and/or instrumental observation. Why, then, has relativity not consummated that philosophical revolution? Why has it so signally failed to gain general acceptance as a clear-cut alternative to the classical paradigm? The answer is that in Einsteinian relativity the world of matter and the world of the observer remain inexorably separated by what Einstein conceived as ‘the velocity of light in vacuo’. This notorious ‘Einstein separation’ leaves us wondering how the mass of an object can be said to be relative to the observer when the observer can know

nothing about it till the light from it reaches him, which may, in some cases, be

zons after it left the object. This mystifies us by seeking to ‘relativise’ physical reality whilst isolating it in the old Cartesian way. It confounds commonsense logic as surely as if we were asked to think of the earth as both round and flat in the same instance.

If only this ‘Einstein separation’ (light-separation) could be removed, then nothing could stand in the way of Mach’s anticipated paradigm change from absolutism to relativism. In that same stroke, the conflict between the time- delayed interaction of relativity theory and the instantaneous action-at-a-distance of quantum physics would be resolved. But how might that be achieved? The

answer is surprisingly simple. In 1954, Herman Bondi wrote: *

Any attempt to measure the velocity of light is ... not an attempt at measuring the velocity of light but an attempt at ascertaining the length of the standard metre in Paris in terms of time-units.

In other words, the constant c, customarily called ‘the velocity of light’, becomes, instead, what Bondi calls a ‘conversion factor’, which has the dimensions of motion but none of motion’s mechanical characteristics and commonsense implications.

With distances in metres cashed-out in this Bondian way, as times in seconds (i.e. with every metre being equivalent to 3.3 nanoseconds), all physical motion involves, basically, three time-dimensions.® One of these time-dimensions is that of the distance-time, measured in seconds, between where the motion starts and where it ends, as measured by the observer of the motion; another is the time the object takes to travel that distance as measured by that same observer's clock, and the third is the time that same observer reads (in his telescope, say) from the object’s clocks between the start and finish of that same motion. These three time- dimensions are all part of the same package of observational information that defines the phenomenon of motion relative to the observer. The constant ¢ relating the different units involved (i.e. metres and seconds) is the same for all observers regardless of their states of motion or rest with regard to one another — in the same way that relative motion or rest is irrelevant to the fact that for all observers there

are always a 39,37 inches to the metre and 2.2 pounds in a kilogram.

PYTHAGOREAN TIME-DILATION As dependent variables, these three time-dimensions are orthogonal (i.e.

rectangular) geometro-temporal projections out of the same package of information that defines the motion of any object relatively to an observer. (See also Rowlands in this volume). We may now see how very simply and easily — and commonsensically ~ this produces relativistic time-dilation without any reference to travelling ‘light-rays’. For example, let the distance-time travelled in uniform motion by an object relatively to an observer be s/c, and let the object’s time (i.e., the proper time as registered by that object in the telescope of that same observer) be fp. The observational resultant of these orthogonal projections is then, automatically, the relative time, tp of the motion, by the Pythagorean

$$t_a = \left[\frac{s}{c} + t_p\right] \tag{1}$$

where c is now a dimensional conversion-factor, not the ‘speed of light’. Since the relative velocity v is s/p, we have s = vtz, Substituting this equivalent expression

Vtr for s in (1) produces

te=V [(vtr/e)? + tp°] , (2) from which it follows, by ordinary transposition and simplification, that

$$t_p = t_R\sqrt{1 - (v/c)^2} \tag{3}$$

Plainly, then, Einstein’s paradox-making Second Postulate, that ‘The

speed of light is a constant, c, in vacuo for all observers regardless of their states of motion or rest with respect to one another’ is surplus to requirements, since the

same result of relativistic time-dilation can be deduced much more simply without either that postulate or its perplexing implications.

THE TWINS NON-PARADOX

Speaking of paradoxes, many people (i.e., the ‘Dissidents’ already mentioned)

believe that the idea of time-dilation itself is paradoxical. For how, they reason,

can a pair of twins, just by the action of moving away from each other and back, end up with one of them being older than the other.

‘The basis of this so-called ‘paradox’ is the unwarranted classical assumption — which amounts, with many people, to a dogma — that ‘time is the same everywhere’. To reveal the fallacy of that assumption we only have to ask how we can possibly know that. Without presuming to be an all-seeing ‘God’, the only way in which these twins can know what time it is relatively to each other is by each of them, in his telescope, reading the other’s clock and comparing those readings with the readings of his own clock throughout the out-and-back motion. The ‘clock’, in this case, need not be a mechanical clock. It may be any physical process, such as heart-beats, cell-divisions, bodily ageing, or — to take the most exacting case — nuclear clocks of the sort used in the Mössbauer method of counting the periods of gamma-ray quanta by means of spectroscopes. These methods of counting, the ultimate means of which is by spectroscopic analysis, reveal — what is commonsense in any case — that whichever of the twins undertakes a reversing action to bring the two back together switches immediately at that point from counting red-shifted to blue-shifted periods in his spectroscopic readings of the other’s clocks, while for the other that reversing-action (and the associated switch from red- to blue-shifted periods) will not be observed immediately but only after an interval which is the distance-time s/c of that distant motion-reversal. This asymmetry in the period-counting, between the two observers, disposes of the ‘paradox’ by determining precisely which of the two times is dilated and which is contracted relatively to which.’

THE NON-PARADOX OF TIME-DELAYED INSTANTANEITY

Like Bondi, many people including Michel Grossman and the present writer have found it much easier and simpler to understand and to teach Special Relativity in this purely dimensional or phenomenological way, without having to follow the same historical wild-goose chase on which people like Michelson and Morley were led in trying to square the observational constancy of c with the mechanistic

‘streaming-substance’ of Empedocles. It seems, then, that this Bondian interpret-

ation of the constant c, freed of all the historic rigmarole involved with the mechanistic interpretation of light, is the conceptual hinge pin on which the long- overdue paradigm change from the traditional Separatist to a truly Relativist physics must eventually turn.

Those purely practical consequences of SR regarding the speed-limit, c, of mechanical motion, the consequent relativising of mass and so on,® follow from this Pythagorean presentation in the same way as in the conventional theory. However, presented in this Pythagorean way, there are some interesting implications which are masked by Einstein’s ‘light in vacuo” rendering of relativity. For instance, squaring the Pythagorean equation (1) and plotting the three variables in tum at | right angles to one another describes a rectangular conic surface, a model of which is shown in Fig. 1. On this surface, the three time-dimensions of motion are accommodated in a way they cannot be in the conventional two-dimensional flat graph. This can be seen

in the two aspects of the cone depicted in |

a-distance debate is that the conic represent- Fig. 1 ation clearly shows how the Newtonian The Pythagorean time-cone speed-unlimited graph of motion (the end-

elevation of the cone shown on the left of Fig. 2) and the Einsteinian speed- limited aspect of physical motion (the side-elevation shown on the right of Fig. 2.) can be equally represented with no conflict whatsoever on the same conic surface. It also shows how what is represented in the left-hand aspect of the combined graph as a theoretically infinite Newtonian speed appears in the right-

hand aspect as the Einsteinian ‘speed-limit’ (asymptote) c.?'”

On the left of the figure (end-clevation of the cone) we see a series of regularly increasing velocities plotted as the distance-times travelled by bodies in some standard time tp registered by those bodies themselves as viewed in the telescope of the observer. On the right (side-elevation) we see those same motions as the same distance-times travelled by the bodies in the relative time tg of the observatory (i.e., the observed time of the motion minus the distance-time s/c). In the first figure, the limit of the velocities s/t is infinity; in the second, the limit of the velocities (s/tg) of those same motions is the ‘finite speed’ c.

Clearly, then, there is no sensible question of there being any ‘superluminal’ action-at-a-distance. In order to travel ‘faster than light’ the proper-time time ¢p of any object or influence would have to be less than zero. In other words, its time would have to run backwards! While this might present an exciting prospect for speculative metaphysicians, it is hardly something to be

accepted by commonsense as part of any anticipated new paradigm.

To the question, then, of whether action-at-a-distance is instantaneous or time-delayed the answer, so far as light is concerned, is plainly that it is both. In its Newtonian aspect (left-hand, Fig. 2) the limit is instantaneity, whereas in its Einsteinian (right-hand) aspect it is c. This can be seen if, in equation (1), we make the proper-time fp of a light interaction zero (i.e., instantaneous) and then solve for the relative time. tg of that same interaction, which is the delayed time

s/c ascertained by observation and experiment.

5.4.2 The second bypass: Einstein to Balmer

So much, then, for what is generally regarded as the transition time of a light- signal from A to B as measured in experiments to determine what is traditionally called ‘the ‘speed of light’. As we have seen, this is, without contradiction, both intrinsically infinite (fp = 0) and observationally time-delayed (fp = s/c). Let us now look at the time which is the period of a light quantum itself, in its ‘standing state’, as a store of energy at the pole of a light interaction conventionally regarded as the emitting atom. This is not instantaneous but has the intrinsic period 1/v, where v is the frequency of the quantum as manifest in the spectral

line viewed at the absorbing pole of the interaction.

THE PYTHAGOREAN QUANTUM ACCUMULATOR !! The generalised Balmer-Rydberg formula for these atomic spectra may be written in the form

$$\nu = N'cR\left[\frac{1}{n_1^2} - \frac{1}{n_2^2}\right] \tag{4}$$

Here, v signifies the frequencies of the classically conceived electromagnetic fieid-vibrations as manifest in the spectral bar-codes, as it were, of chemical elements; c is the usual conversion-factor for converting distances into times; R is the empirical Rydberg constant and ny, Nz and N are natural numbers, with ,, 12 denoting the spectral tine number (the fixed and running terms, respectively), V being the ordinal number in the periodic table of the element in question. As an example, for N = 1, the formula fits those frequencies of the lines in the optical spectrum of hydrogen which were originally classified as series. Each series was found to correspond to transitions from various energy-levels down to a particular value of n. For instance, 1; = 1 gives the Lyman series, 7 = 2 gives the Balmer Series, and so on. This relationship for hydrogen was discovered on the basis of purely empirical evidence, by Balmer in 1885, Equation (4) is a rewritten, more

general form due to Rydberg, |

With this in mind, let us now look again at our time-formula (3). The

lower limit of this formula is, of course, fp. Taking this as some unknown basic

unit of the series of values tp, the formula produces an interesting result. Here is

that formula again:

$$t_p = t_R\sqrt{1 - (v/c)^2} \tag{3}$$

Let us suppose that in accordance with our neo-Machian, holistic, top-down approach to physical analysis, there is some natural level of reduction, to be determined, at which ali measures fp are integer multiples N of some natural indivisible unit fo, and that all measures fg are also integer multiples n of that same

$$\frac{n_1}{N} = \frac{t_1}{N}\sqrt{1 - (v/c)^2} = \sqrt{1 - (N'/n)^2} \tag{5}$$

from which it follows that v’/c? forms a sequence of discrete values. It must be stressed, however, that at this fundamental quantum level (v/c)* is no more than a terminal abstraction of the process of reduction, so that it makes no sense to think of v, here, as an ordinary speed. The same applies at this level to the mass mo that is expressed in terms of v in the standard energy-equation e = mov’, where e is potential energy, measured in joules. For heuristic reasons (to be justified in due course) we may, without contradiction, substitute e/mo for ¥ in equation (5), which produces e=moc{1—(N'in*)] . which is equation (4), the Balmer-Rydberg formula. This completes our Second ahistorical Bypass from the Pythagorean time-formula to the frequency formula of Balmer and Rydberg. This is by pure logic without involving any of the usual theoretical infrastructure.

5.4.3 The third bypass: Balmer to Bohr

This process also produces the mass of the so-called ‘electron’. The natural optimum unit of mass in this cumulative sequence is the mass mg in equations (7) and (8). The value of this mass may now be calculated, from (8) and (9), as AR/c. With the known values of h ~ 6.6256196 x 10° Js, R = 1.097373 x 107m", and ¢ = 2.997925 x 10° m s', we have mo © 2.42527 x 10°75 kg, which, classically, is the mass of the ‘photon’ (a redundant conception, of course, in the new paradigm

we are exploring).

Looking again at Fig. 2 we see how the regular increments in the velocities shown in the left-hand, classical aspect of the conical graph become smaller in their right-hand, relativistic aspect as they approach the asymptotic

limit c. This means that in the observational frame, as a body approaches the

relative velocity limit c, there are diminishing returms on regular investments of force and energy in accelerating it. Since the mass of a body is defined as the proportion between the force applied to the acceleration produced, then from that definition one might reasonably assume that as the velocity of a body increases

towards the limit c, so its mass increases relatively to the observer of the motion.

In other words, classically speaking, in the reference frame of an observer, applying a force to a body of rest-mass m, for a standard period f, produces a (classical) velocity v,. However, if, at the end of that same period /, the same force produces in that same body a smaller velocity v, such that v = s/tp (where ¢ is the relative or dilated time according to equation (3)), then one could take it as

implicit that the mass m of the body has relativistically increased in the proportion MR= (v,/v)m, (11) Since, by (3), the relativistic velocity v satisfies v= sltp=Vl-W/2)] y, (12) we may conclude that ma = mN(1~-(/e*)), (13) which is the standard formula for relativistic mass in SR. It is interesting to note that in SR this formula is a direct consequence of conservation of four- momentum, and so (11) is essentially our replacement for this conservation principle.

Now since, in standard physics, losses and gains in energy, as in our quantum accumulator, must be produced by the movement of some mass which, at rest, is m, then from (13) we have

$$(m_a - m)c^2 = \left[(1 - v^2/c^2)^{-1/2} - 1\right]mc^2 = m_0c^2 \tag{14}$$

where v is the relativistic ‘speed’ of the mass mp, and mo is our natural unit of mass.'? For v «¢, the approximate kinetic energy of the mass, given exactly by the

left hand side of equation (14) is 4 mv’. Thus, let
