Starting with ¢= 0, equation (17) provides a value of m. Hence values of mp and v can be calculated from equation (14). Equation (15) then provides a better approximation of ¢. With this new «, the quadratic equation (17) can be solved in the usual way to obtain a better approximation of m, and so on. This iterative procedure converges and provides mr = m+ mo ~ 9.109558 x 107! ke whence, from (14) v = 2.1875525 x 10°ms"

These are the standard textbook values for the mass of the so-called ‘electron’ and the velocity which Bohr deduced for the orbiting electron on the basis of classical electrodynamics. Moreover, in the standing configuration we have named the Pythagorean quantum accumulator, the mass mr that has been called the ‘electron’ and to which vy is attributed doesn’t go anywhere beyond the configuration. So, to borrow an argument from Aristotle, its velocity v must be orbital.'* In other words, what might otherwise be its momentum mv is an angular momentum r x my. Empirically, the smallest measure of this quantity is Planck’s constant h divided by 2x, which physicists know as fi (= 1.05457266 x 10% kg m/s). From this we may deduce the value of r, which is (for ideally circular

motion):

$$r = A/m_e \approx 5.29177 \times 10^{-11}\;\text{m}$$

(where r= |[r|| and v= || v||). This is the same as the textbook value of the Bohr

radius.

Readers might like to compare this result with that of Anthony Osborme, in the Example he presents in section 5.1 of his Chapter on ‘The Pope-Osborne Angular Momentum Synthesis’ (Chapter 9). The aim of this example, he says,

...is to demonstrate ... how those parameters, calculated by Bohr in terms of a Newtonian dynamics laced with the electrodynamics of Faraday, Maxwell, Coulomb and others, could logically have been derived from Newtonian-type dynamics alone, simply by altering the value of the ‘gravitational constant’ G. ;

In that same chapter, in replacing the conservation principle (11) by the principle of conservation of angular momentum, Osborne obtains a deductive method for calculating the parameters of the hydrogen atom. This, he holds, is a better method, requiring fewer ad hoc assumptions than the above, which he regards as being — in this Third Bypass, at any rate~ more of a ‘feasibility study’ than a true

deduction.

5.4.4 Interim Conclusion

It is significant that these parameters of Bohr’s historical model of the atom can be reproduced in this way, by-passing all historical references to ‘electric charges’ and their associated ‘field’ and ‘force’ postulates — indeed, bypassing practically the whole of traditional electrodynamics.'> The logical implications of all this are that, in principle, the discoveries made by Einstein, Balmer and Bohr, in that order, could have been deduced by the Pythagoreans, if only it could have been

known at the time that distances are times in the ratio of conventional units c.

But, of course, like the Bohr model, our Pythagorean quantum accum-~ ulator is, as it stands, somewhat primitive insofar as its cycles are conceived as ideally circular. It needs to be stressed, however, that being purely schematic at this stage, these cycles are not to be conceived as well-defined, determinate trajectories of the sort we are conditioned to think of by experience with

macroscopic objects like planets and cannon-balls. In our lateral-thinking

alternative to the current paradigm, the parameters of this quantum configuration, in the measure-categories of mass, length and time, are derived by methods of almost pure dimensional syllogistic, with only the barest inclusion of key bits of information from experimental physics. Nevertheless, for present purposes, this reasoning so far suffices to show how getting rid ‘of the classical ‘light-velocity’ interpretation of c clears the way towards a whole new paradigmic way of thinking about ‘atoms’ and their spatial interactions. However, for any critic to demand, as a criterion for consideration, that at this barely emergent stage from a chrysalid state, our lateral thinking alternative should cope straightaway with the millennial accumulation of eclectic knowledge that is Modern Science, would be no more than a flagrant attempt at a dismissive put-down. Nevertheless, some intimations of how this nascent, lateral paradigm might be expected to cope may be gleaned from what follows.

5.4.5 The Cinematic Model of Quantum Interaction First, however, let us return to the question of distant interaction. As commonly understood, angular momentum is a conserved quantity, which means that for our quantum accumulator (let’s just call it an ‘atom’, why not?) to lose or gain a quantum of angular momentum, some other atom somewhere else has to lose or gain precisely that same amount. That compensatory loss or gain has to be immediate, otherwise there would be a period during which the angular momentum is disconserved, which conservation cannot allow. That interaction also has to be resonant, not only with the periods of the respective atoms but also with the distance-time s/c, between them. That distance-time, we recall, is in the time-delayed dimension ¢p = s/c, not in the dimension tp, where the interaction is instantaneous. In this latter dimension the interaction is what Gilbert Lewis, in 1926, described as a ‘quantum touching’ in accordance with Newton’s third law of instantaneous and reciprocal action-reaction,'® (See also Peter and Neal Graneau in this volume.)

Without this condition of resonance being satisfied, there can be no

transfer of energy. This is, of course, completely foreign to the classical paradigm

according to which quanta are fired out from atoms to travel in an entirely hit-or- miss way, like a spray of buckshot, with no assurance of any particular ‘strike’. Yet the condition of direct, instantaneous synchrony required by our replacement paradigm is confirmed in very precise measurements, not only with gamma radiation in what is called resonance absorption (see the Mössbauer effect, section 1.2 of Chapter 11) but also in the well-known two-slit interference

experiment (see following, section 5.8).

The conceptual ‘flipover’ that is mooted here is therefore away from the standard paradigm, in which atomic interactions take place in the form of time- delayed wave-processes involving mediatory ‘fields’ or ‘ethers’. Instead, it conceives time-delayed distant light-interaction as a purely kinematical process in which the irreducible (i.e., quantised) angular momentum relations, both internal to and between atoms, occur like the still photographs or ‘stills’ in a movie. The basis for this kinematical derivation of quantum accumulation and interaction is,

as we have seen, the theorem of Pythagoras.

In our lateral paradigm, light is not something that is just incidental to matter; it is basic to matter’s very existence. That is to say, it is relative or relational, in Mach’s sense, not absolute and self-sufficient in the sense of Democritus and observationaily remote in the ‘God’s-eye-view’ sense of

Descartes.

5.5 Light as quantum information

How, then, if we relinquish the traditional mechanistic ‘streaming-substance’ notions about light, may we conceive of light in the context of this antithetical “New Paradigm’? The answer has to be to think of it as pure information. Stripped of ail mechanistic conceptions, light-quanta are simply informational bits, like the binary digits of information-technology, out of which, ultimately, our knowledge of the world is constructed. And because they are immediate they are, in any instance, as much a part of the objects we perceive as of our perceptions of those objects, being, in effect, common to both. So there can be no radical distinction or

separation, of the sort conceived by Empedocles, Descartes and Einstein, between

the world of objects and the world of observation. This consequence also fits perfectly the discovery made by the 1920s philosophers, as described earlier (section 5.3), that there is no way of distinguishing any ‘God’s-eye-view’ of physical reality that is completely remote from the reality we ordinarily observe. The default realism, which remains when the bogus Cartesian dualism is removed, has been called Normal Realism, to distinguish it from the presumptuous ‘God’s-

eye-view’ atomic fundamentalism of classical physics.

What sort of world, then, does Normal Realism require us to think of? The. answer is simply to look around us. Our eyes, aided by instruments such as spectacles, binoculars, microscopes, telescopes and all sorts of other, more sophisticated apparatuses, tell us what there is. All this is corroborated by information obtained by our other senses, all processed by means of logical inference, mathematical calculation and so on. Thus we perceive, on a good day and under paradigm conditions,'’ the very essence of what is ‘out there’, in what

everyone whose conceptions are not conditioned by classical physics recognises

as the real world.

But, of course, in that real world we never perceive all there is. Behind and beyond what we perceive in any instance there is always what lies beneath that rock, inside that nutshell or star, beyond that furthest galaxy ... and so on, which no man has ever or may ever perceive. This huge remnant is all part of what is normally called reality. The only difference between this meaning of ‘beyond perception’ and the Cartesian meaning is that it eschews all reference to a radically remote ‘reality’ which we can never, ever perceive because it exists in

some way that we presume only ‘God? sees, behind all the actual and possible

appearances.

Laying aside, then, this classical ‘God’s-eye-view’ presumption, what we perceive or ‘observe’ in any paradigm situation is an informational character- complex, a hologram-like presentation which the Peripatetic followers of Aristotle called a species.'* In this informational manifold is contained all the data of sense

and instrumentation, in al! the various dimensions and measures, or ‘degrees of

freedom’, known to commonsense and practical science. Peculiar to our Westem tradition of natural philosophy, ali sorts of thinkers, from Democritus to Einstein, have sought, like flies trapped in a bottle, to interpret this information in such a way as to find a way ‘out of the bottle’ into the “God’s-eye-view’ world that, in his imagination, Descartes projected beyond ordinary perception. However, as Wittgenstein was one of the first to discern, there is no such ‘bottle’. As philosophical ‘flies’, he said, we are and always have been, ‘outside’ it. Or, more correctly, the limits of the ‘bottle’s’ confinement are at infinity, so that it makes

no sense to think of anything “beyond it’.

5.5.1 Objective Idealism

It turns out, therefore, that the whole syndrome of ‘idealism versus realism’ should never have been a part of Western philosophy. As modern philosophers now judge, it transpires to have been no more than a sickness, a kind of philosophical schizophrenia, which was visited on Western society by Descartes’ purely arbitrary drawing of a voodoo line between what we see and what ‘God’ sees. The cure which Normal Realism offers, in that modern tradition, is no more than to re-introduce the realism that is opened up to us by the complete erasure of that voodoo line between observation and physical reality that had practically disappeared in modern philosophy but was re-drawn by Einstein in his ‘velocity in vacuo’ interpretation of c. The only difference this makes is to present a programme for modern physics based simply on discerning the best and most efficient way of observing the world and ‘telling it as it is’, as succinctly as possible and with an absolute minimum of metaphysical speculation and jargon. This is not quite the same as the ‘Naive Realism’ envisaged by the Linguistic philosophers, such as Austin, Wittgenstein, ef al., whereby they sought to make the touchstone of realistic description the science-rejecting, standard language of Arts scholars like themselves —- which they fancifully described as ‘Ordinary Language’. Normal Realism bases its realism on the much wider experience of ordinary folks, including that of the scientists. True, however, to its Machian

lineage, what Normal Realism seeks to interpret is neither the restricted ‘Ordinary

Language’ of the Oxbridge philosophers nor the cobbled-together jargon of classical Science. It is the language of plainly and logically interpreted observational information, the language that in earlier times was (secularly) called

logos, or ‘the Language of Nature’, as Berkeley called it.

In short, in the New Paradigm the light that was historically regarded as an incidental mediator of information about an inexorably remote ‘world beyond’ becomes that very information itself which is the fount of all our knowledge of space, time, matter and motion. In this new way of thinking, the task for a New- Paradigm physics would be to cease trying to match our human perceptions and conceptions to the world as ‘God’ might be presumed to perceive it, and settle for solving problems arising from logically conflicting interpretations of ordinary and instrumental experience. Perceptions and conceptions that contradict one another naturally excite our curiosity. This prompts us to revise our ideas in the various sectors of observation and conception towards satisfying the need for what is commonly known as understanding. For instance, ideas about ‘atoms’ are not ideas about self-sufficient, non-ideational entities. They are ideas about ideas. There are no atoms we can ever know as ‘things in themselves’ as opposed to our ideas of them. Nor is there any self-sufficient space or time beyond what we can actually measure. So far as Normal Realism is concerned, anything we may identify in thought and language as ‘atoms’ ‘electrons’, ‘charges’, ‘fields’, ‘photons’ or whatever, is revisable in the light of rational reflection on ongoing experience. Unfortunately, the consequence of this, expressed in Wittgenstein’s edict “The World is language’, seems, from the traditional ‘God’s Eye-View’ standpoint to be sheer nonsense. From that old-established, absolutist viewpoint, how can things like material particles, gravitational fields, electric fields and so on, “as God sees them’, be just bits of language? Without what these reactionary ‘realists’ see as the underpinning provided by their traditionally conceived ‘absolute reality’, physics, they fear, would lose its time-honoured ‘objectivity’ and collapse into an anarchy of purely psychological ideas and impressions. That

this fear is unfounded becomes plain when we consider that these ‘ideas and

impressions’ that we gain in practical experience come supplied with their own built-in criterion of objectivity. This criterion is, of course, that of the logical coherence and consistency of those ideas in the context of evolving perception and language. Far from being ‘merely subjective’, this criterion of logical objectivity is common to all who are of sound mind, which fits the description of

what is ordinarily known as ‘commonsense’.

As long, then, as we remain careful in attending to its continued construction, reconstruction and maintenance, this evolving language of commonsense is objective in a way which needs no reference to anything beyond itself. This is precisely the point that was made by Wittgenstein about the World being Language — or Jogos, as the Greeks called it (witness the fact that most of our departments of scientific knowledge still append the word ‘-/ogy’ to their titles, ¢.g., physiology, geology, biology ... etc.). On the other hand, perceptions or conceptions traditionally accorded the status of absolute God-given truths create stumbling-blocks in the path of true rational progress, since it is only in the context of experience as a matured whole that our ideas of what there is, and how

it is, can be properly verified and understood.

There is thus nothing of certainty that can be found in language itself, on which to base our ideas, whether it be in commonsense, in Oxbridge Linguisticism or in science.'? That is to say, there is no algorithm of language, in the categories of logic, mathematics or whatever, that can serve as an ‘oracle of truth’. The development of a// language is completely heuristic, so that its uses, regardless of how familiar they may be, may just as easily mystify as enlighten us. This makes the combined task of physics and philosophy that of filtering out uses of language (Wittgenstein called them ‘misuses’) which achieve nothing so much as to create chimeras — such as, for instance, calling light-quanta ‘photons’ (see above), conflating ‘waves’ and ‘particles’ and calling interactions between

particles ‘particles’, to name no more than three of innumerable such examples.

5.6 The logical redundancy of Newton's in vacuo gravitational force

Our programme of Normal Realism for physics is not that of hard-line Rationalism or Axiomatics. It is that of empiricism, or ‘enlightened commonsense’ as T, H. Huxley called it. This programme, as we see it, is simply to discern the best and most efficient way of interpreting observation. This minimalist approach to scientific observation makes much use of the method known as ‘Ockham’s Razor’ for economising on unnecessary theorising. The

following is an example of how this minimalist method works in practice,

When Newton tells us that all bodies left to themselves travel in straight lines,?° the Normal Realist will say “But where is that straight-line motion ever observed?’ All the free motion we observe, from satellites to galaxies, is curved. Even balls rolling on the flattest of surfaces and pucks on ice would be seen to travel in curves if their tracks were sufficiently elongated. So why did Newton say

that free motion is naturally rectilinear when plainly it is not?

In contrast to Newton, the eminent empiricist, Aristotle, observed what is undeniably true, that all free motion in space is naturally curved — in astronomical cases, typically cyclic or orbital. This, at a stroke, dispenses with the in vacuo “gtavitational force’ that Newton invented to explain why nowhere do bodies in space travel rectilinearly in the way that his ‘First Law’ dictates. Newton gave us two laws for the price of one. The one tells us that bodies left to themselves travel in straight lines, while the other (the so-called ‘Universal Law of Gravitation’)

telis us why the first law is never obeyed.

In Normal Realism, this metaphysically manufactured ‘force of gravity’ is eliminated simply by making Newton’s rectilinear momentum a limiting case of angular momentum, r X mv with the radius r (where r = |[r{]) at theoretical infinity (a cycle of infinite radius and a straight line being one and the same).”! But with r at infinity, so is the magnitude of the angular momentum, which is unreal. It follows, then, that for any real — that is, finite — angular momentum, r is also finite, so that a continuous free (inertial) motion is naturally orbital, or cyclic,

in the way Aristotle observed. This postulate, together with conservation of

angular momentum forms the basis of the Pope-Osborne Angular Momentum Synthesis (POAMS) as described in Chapter 9.

5.6.1 Newton’s Second Law Revisited In Newton’s Second Law of Motion, force is defined as the measure of change in momentum:

$$\mathbf{F} = d(m\mathbf{v})/dt \tag{18}$$

In the alternative paradigm we are exploring, this is replaced by a more general definition of torque as the measure of change in angular momentum:

$$\boldsymbol{\tau} = d(\mathbf{r} \times m\mathbf{v})/dt \tag{19}$$

For freely orbiting bodies, the effect of torque is to change the radial vector

r and the velocity vector v.”” However, in instances where r cannot vary — as, for

example in the case of the motion of a ball on a billiard table where, in being

level, all points are at the same distance r from the centre of the earth — a torque T

has no affect on r, hence no affect on the angular momentum. In such cases, all

that varies is the linear momentum mv. When r is constant, T = (dr/dt) x mv + r= (didi(mv) > T=rxF, (20)

so that the magnitude of the torque is given by T = Fr, where F is the magnitude of the force. That is to say, prevented from orbiting where it should, with an angular momentum insufficient for it to orbit above the earth’s surface, an object presses on that surface, which responds by registering, by reaction, an upward restraining force of 9.81 newtons per kilogram of mass.” This, of course, is no metaphysical in vacuo force. It is a real, felt and measurable force, which can register on a weighing-scale. Take the restraining surface away and the force

disappears, as any sky diver knows.

Consistently, then, with our New Paradigm, in the same way that we dispense with ‘light in vacuo’, so we dispense with ‘gravitation’ as an invisible and un-detectable in vacuo agency — as also, with any question of a ‘field’

associated with it. (See Osborne, Chapter 9, section 3.1). However, in the

Workshop of which this present volume is a result, objections have been raised as to whether the changes in momentum, typical for bodies in elliptical orbits, betoken by definition the presence of an accelerating force. This made it necessary, in the course of the Workshop debate, to do some linguistic revision in the manner legitimate for the Informational Paradigm. Plainly, if there is no force involved in the changes in velocity that take place in elliptical free orbits, then this signifies some fundamental ambiguity in the customary word ‘acceleration’ insofar as it implies a force in accordance with Newton’s Second Law. For the sake of the more general law, therefore, this ambiguity needed to be removed, and for this purpose, a neologism was provisionally agreed. With ‘acceleration’ retained for changes in velocity in those special cases involving bodies in constrained orbits (such as billiard-balls moving on flat surfaces that prevent them from orbiting freely near the earth’s centre) the word ‘flacceleration’ (in view of the lack of any nicer alternative) was proposed to distinguish the forceless changes in velocity that occur in elliptical free orbits of constant angular

momentum.

There have been, of course, many attempts to dispose of Newton’s problematic in vacuo gravitational force. Most notable of these was, of course, Einstein’s, in his General Relativity (GR). Jn that theory, by a so-called ‘Principle of Equivalence’, the force of gravity that holds us to the earth is explained as a reaction, by Newton’s Third Law, of our body-mass against an upwards acceleration of the earth’s surface under our feet. Unfortunately, that suggests, absurdly, that the earth is swelling at the incredible rate of 9.81 metres per second. per second. That absurdity is avoided in the Aristotelian alternative, in which, as already explained, the weight of a body is simply the reaction of the earth’s surface, or a weighbridge, let us say, against the natural tendency of the body to orbit the earth’s centre somewhere way down below our feet. Calculations in accordance with the angular momentum formula reveal that for a mass of one

kilogram, standing at the equator, hence with a diumal angular momentum of

2.95 × 109 kg m2s−1, the depth at which that mass should orbit the earth, if it were free to do so, would be over six thousand kilometres.

5.7 The logical redundancy of in vacuo electric and magnetic forces

it transpires that not only can ‘gravity’ be eliminated in this logical way, but so also can in vacuo ‘electrostatic’ and ‘magnetic’ ‘forces’. The reason is that in an overall-conserved angular momentum system, not only are the orbital angular momenta of masses automatically coupled but so, also are their spins. This means that the orbital parameters of spinning bodies cannot be the same as for non- spinning ones. Again, calculations reveal 5 that a mass equivalent to that of the standard ‘electron’ should orbit the mass of a standard ‘proton’ (assuming, for argument a purely ‘gravitational’ and ideally circular orbit) at a truly extragalactic distance of approximately 1.2 x 10” m, whereas given a purely mechanical spin- energy in joules equivalent to the ionisation potential of the hydrogen atom, that same mass should orbit the central one at a distance approximately equivalent to the Bohr radius.”* (See also Osborne in this volume: ‘The Pope-Osborne Angular

Momentum Synthesis’, Chapter 9, section 5.1.)

So far as magnetostatic force is concerned, the solution arose in the Workshop debate. This was when Martin Kokus, who accepted our angular momentum explanation of electrostatics as a replacement for the esoteric ‘charge’, kindly pointed out what he saw as a flaw in the argument. This was that in talking about particle spin, as a replacement for the ‘charge’, no clear distinction was made between electrostatics and magnetostatics, both of which involve a spin angular momentum of the same particle. The theory, he argued, “can therefore unify either magnetostatics and gravity or electrostatics and gravity, but not all three together’. .

In response we suggested that in a way similar to that in which electrostatic orbits are explained by electron-proton angular momentum relations, magneto- static orbits might be explained by angular momentum relations involving only electrons. In magnetism, the intrinsic spin angular momentum of electrons gives

them vectorial properties whereby their orientations may be added or subtracted in

the same way as in electron-proton arrangements.’ The intrinsic spins of free electrons are therefore always part of the same overall angular momentum equation. For instance, a flow of electrons in a wire induces sympathetic changes in the orientations of neighbouring electrons in ferromagnetic materials such as iron, nickel, cobalt, efe.; and banging a piece of iron aligns the spins of some of its electrons with that of others in the earth. This confirms, in principle at any rate, the same UIAAAD connect ions for electron-electron arrangements as for those of

electrons and protons in ‘electrostatics’.

Unfortunately, so far as any precise formula is concerned, free electrons are not found in neat orbital arrangements like the electrons and protons to which the formula satisfactorily applies. In magnetostatics, the spinning electrons are confined within regions of the material where their freedom to form orbital arrangements is severely restricted by their occupancy of widely separated positions in the material throughout which they are spread. Moreover, while there are crystalline domains within a ferromagnetic material in which the spin- orientations of the electrons are perfectly aligned, the orientations of the domains, overall, in a non-magnetised material are not all aligned but are so orientated as statistically to cancel each other out. This makes it difficult (although not, in

principle, impossible) to obtain empirical values for the variables involved.

However, such calculations as have been made reveal that the difference in strength between the forces required to prevent electrons from ‘orbiting’ electrons is in the right order of magnitude of the recognised difference between ‘magnetostatic’ and ‘electrostatic’ forces. Further researches along the same lines also suggest that in vacuo nuclear ‘forces’ may eventually be dispensed with in the same way. What is thus proffered is a logical solution to the chronic problem of ‘unified field’. This is simply to get rid of in vacuo ‘fields’ and ‘forces’ altogether. (We apologise here to the Graneaus, in this volume, who argue for the physical existence of in vacuo — albeit instantaneous and unmediated — electrical

forces.)

5.8 The mysterious two-slit experiment

Another problem to be solved in similar fashion is one which has puzzled physicists for centuries, namely, the Thomas Young two-slit interference experiment. In that well-known experiment, the puzzle is that light, thought of in the classical mechanistic way as travelling wave-packets or corpuscles, seems to behave in a manner which confounds commonsense. In the context of the classical Mechanistic Paradigm, energetic interaction between the source and screen is facilitated by particles (‘photons’, ‘electrons’, ‘atoms’ and so on) travelling in a bullet-like way, with no connection whatsoever with the target until they actually ‘strike’ it. This makes it a complete mystery that these particles, if thought of as travelling in this way, have the sort of foreknowledge of their destinations that is necessary for them to form the ordered patterns of strikes that the experimenter

observes at the screen.

In our ‘flip-side’ Non-Mechanistic Paradigm this phenomenon of apparent ‘pre-cognition’ on the part of fundamental particles presents no such problem. This is because the source-screen interaction is not conceivable as being of that ballistic kind. Instead, the apparatus is taken as a geometrical whole (an informational ‘hologram’) in which everything is connected with everything else at every quantum instant. This makes it the paths from source to screen that interfere, not ‘waves’, corpuscles’ or whatever that we may think of as travelling along them. This purely geometrical interference is in positive and negative half- lengths of the De Broglie so-called ‘wavelength’ 4/p, where p is the momentum of

the particle emitted by the source.”®

The situation, therefore, is exactly as described in standard ‘wave- interference’ terms but minus any suggestion of there being any real wave travelling in vacuo between the source and screen, The path-lengths, regardless of what may be conducted along those paths, are direct and immediate action-at-a- distance quantum connections of proper-time transition tp = 0 occurring in cinematic sequences, in time ¢p, orthogonally projected in accordance with the

Pythagorean equation (1). Moreover, this immediate interaction has the same

dimensions as a wave velocity, that is, the dimensions ¢ for light-quanta (‘photons’) and v = h/mA for real particles. And because the distance between the source and screen which is geometrically fixed on the macrophysical level is always varying at the microlevel (due to the indeterminate thermal ‘jigglings’ of the atoms), the patterns of interaction at that same microlevel are purely probabilistic. The ‘wave-function’, such as it is, is therefore that of the Schrödinger probability-function rather than of the wave-continuum classically

envisaged by the likes of Huygens and Fresnel.

The same applies to all other sorts of experiments employing light-beams and lasers — such as, for instance the classic experiments of Fizeau and Michelson to determine the ‘velocity of light’. No interactions can take place between the source and a detector that are not inter-resonant with the frequency v= h/e of the energy e exchanged between the atoms involved. All these interactions are direct, reciprocal and consummated, That is, there can be no interaction between an emitting atom and ‘the vacuum itself ~ ie. between an atom and nothing. In terms of action, the quantum connection is instantaneous (i.e., its proper-time tp of transition is zero), whence, from (1), it follows that the observational time of that same connection is fp = s/c. So there is no conflict whatsoever between the Bondian, non-velocity interpretation of c and any experiment designed to determine its value. All experimental implications are the same. The only difference is that the intractable mysteries manufactured by the old paradigm

simply do not appear in the new.

5.9 Cosmological implications of the lateral paradigm

The Pope-Osborne Angular Momentum Synthesis, based as it is on the law of conservation of angular momentum, does not fit with the notion of a continually expanding universe. Nor does it fit with any idea of light and matter disappearing down the inscrutable insides of ‘black holes’. It is incumbent on us, therefore, to provide, for the observational data that have led to these theories, alternative explanations which are consistent with our paradigmatic departure. Such

alternative suggestions are as follows.

5.9.1 The alleged &lsquo;Expanding Universe&rsquo;

For a theory to imply that the whole universe of space, time, matter and motion was crammed, some fifteen billion years ago, into a volume the size of a nut would normally be regarded as a reductio ad absurdum of that theory, hence a very good reason for rejecting it. Not so, however, the theory of the ‘Expanding Universe’, which is as desirable to some modern cosmologists as it derisory to

others and, not least, to ordinary commonsense.

Despite the grandiose claims for this theory that are made in the media, most scientists one meets at conferences say something like ‘Whatever is it that makes apparently intelligent people entertain such a ridiculous theory?’ The reason is that the advocates of that theory have assumed, fallaciously, that because the light from a body travelling away from us is spectrally shifted towards the red, every time we see the spectrum of an object shifted towards the red we are seeing that object travelling away from us. (A paraphrase of this failacy is to assume that

because all ants are insects, all insects are ants.)

However, despite this obvious fallacy, the fact, noted by Edwin Hubble, that the light from the far-off galaxies is shifted towards the red in proportion to their observational distance is reported in many authoritative publications as though it were an observed fact that those galaxies are receding from us and from one another, hence that the universe is expanding. For instance, in Encyclopaedia

Britannica ” we read:-

Hubble constant: in cosmology, (the) constant of proportionality in the relation between the velocities of remote galaxies and their distances. It expresses the rate at which the universe is expanding. It is denoted by the symbol H and named in honour of Edwin Hubble, the American astronomer who attempted in 1929 to measure its value. With redshifts of distant galaxies measured by Vesto Slipher, also of the United States, and with his own distance estimates of these galaxies, Hubble established the cosmological velocity- distance law: velocity = H (times) distance. According to this law, known as the Hubble law, the greater the distance of a galaxy, the faster it recedes.

Note that this statement contains no caution as to the logical possibility of interpreting the redshifts other than as velocities of recession. Regardless of any

other considerations, this establishes the interpretation, logically and by

definition, as a dogma. In fact, Hubble himself avoided this dogma by preferring to talk straightforwardly about ‘redshifts’ rather than ‘velocities’, though this

caution was thrown to the winds by his followers.

But, some might say, what other possible explanation can there be for the observational redshift in the light from the far off galaxies? How else might these redshifts be explained other than as a Doppler effect due to a recession of those galaxies? One suggestion that has so far been neglected is that the Hubble redshift is an effect of time-dilation due to an increasing velocity of motion on the part of the galaxies in proportion to their distance — not of uniform recession but in random directions. What kind of mechanism might account for that? An answer

which our Lateral Paradigm offers is as follows.

Distance, in the angular momentum synthesis, is a function of angular momentum. That is to say, the greater the magnitude of the angular momentum between a pair of free-orbiting bodies, the further they both are from their common centre of moment. Now in general, as the orbital angular momentum of a mass increases, so its orbital velocity decreases. Here, of course, we are speaking, for simplicity, of constant masses in ideally circular obits.*° In that case, the magnitude of the orbital angular momentum L is given by the standard Newtonian formula

$$L = mvr = \frac{GmM}{v} \tag{21}$$

where m is the mass whose orbital speed is v, M is the central mass, and G is the usual ‘gravitational constant’, of value 6.67259 x 107! m3 kg! s", In this equation it can be seen that for constant m, M and G, doubling Z will halve v and quadruple r. However, this applies only to a system involving just one pair of bodies. In angular momentum systems involving large numbers of bodies, the bodies do not behave in that way. In the case of spiral nebulae, for instance, the stars do not

orbit their common centre of rotation in the way single pairs of bodies do.?!

For an angular momentum universe, then, of normally distributed masses,

of stars, galaxies, dark matter or whatever, the common ‘centre of motion’ need

not be centred anywhere but may be indefinitely — perhaps infinitely — distributed throughout that universe in the ubiquitous manner that Mach conceived for his datum of inertia. In that case, the further away a body may get from one local centre of motion the nearer if may approach another, as in the case of a space- probe leaving its orbit around earth to orbit another planet such as, say, Jupiter, where its orbital speed as it nears that planet becomes much faster than it might

ever have been near earth.

In general, then, in a nommally distributed many-body system of angular momentum, the greater we make the local angular momentum magnitude of one pair of masses, the more distant these masses become from each other until their common centre of motion becomes lost among all the other centres that those bodies have in their paired angular momentum relations with other bodies. In other words, if it is true of angular momentum that the nearer a body is to the major mass of a system the greater is its orbital velocity, then it also has to be true that when that major concentration of mass lies beyond any given locality, the motions of bodies become faster the further away they get from that locality. In a sense, the further away a body is from any chosen point in the system, the ‘nearer’ it gets to the greatest (ubiquitous) accumulation of cosmic mass, hence the faster its motion becomes. This logical prediction is, indeed, confirmed by the angular

momentum formula. For instance, it follows from (21) that

v= GMrr. (22) (Here, G is not the traditional ‘gravitational constant’ but an empirical factor of angular momentum for the purely orbital motion of non-spinning bodies).

Let us assume, then, that the speed v increases, statistically, in direct

proportion to the distance r. That is, ex hypothesi, vekr, (23) where & is a constant. Substituting accordingly, in (22) produces:

$$v^2r = GM \tag{24}$$

whence we have

$$M = (K/G)^3 \tag{25}$$

This implies that for this (assumed ideally circular) motion, if the speeds of bodies increase linearly with distance from any point in space, then according to (22), the external, ubiquitously distributed mass accumulates statistically as the cube of that same distance. (Compare and contrast this result with Roscoe’s view, in this volume, Chapter 7, also with the Graneaus’ view in Chapter 6. In a homogenous mass distribution, M is proportional to r’ but this is not so if galaxies have a fractal distribution.)

What remains, then, as a test of our hypothesis is to obtain a value for our

constant & in this ideal case, in terms of time-dilation. Now the current redshift

parameter is: **

2=(Ao- AVAL , (26) where A, is the wavelength of a particular spectral line, as measured in the laboratory, and Ap is the wavelength of that same line from a distant source,

measured in the astronomical observatory. If our hypothesis is correct, then this is

the same as Z=(tr—tp)tp = (tp/tp)—1, (27) where 2g is the relativistically time-dilated period fp according to the standard

time-dilational formula (3).

Now the same value for z, in terms of standard recessional theory (over

moderate galactic distances), is: >? z» HDic, (28)

where H, is Hubble’s constant. This is given as 75425 km/sec for every megaparsec, or 75,000 + 25,000 m/s for every 3 x 10” metres (* 2.430427396 x 10°"? + 8101424652 = 1019 sec'), D is the distance in metres of the object from the observatory, and c is the usual space-time constant ~ 2.997925 x 10° ms"! At D= 1 Mpe, with the larger value, H, = 100,000 m s'/Mpc = 3.240569861 x 1078

z = 3.335640952 x 10%,

Solving for v then gives: vy © 7,741,35ims'. Our value for k, therefore, in equation (23) is: k= 7,741,351 ms” per megaparsec = 2.508639 x 10's,

From (25) this produces, for the value of our ubiquitised 4 in a one-megaparsec

cube:

$$M = 2.771347 \times 10^{30}\;\text{kg}$$

This gives the theoretical density of the universe as 9.4309 x 10 kg m®.

This is as near as about one tenth of the theoretical density quoted in Haliday, Resnick and Walker,** for what is described as ‘the present estimated density of the universe’, namely, 10?° kg m>, Taking the lower value of H, = 50,000 m s! halves this cubic mass, which puts it somewhat nearer to the value given in Dobson, Grace and Lovett,? namely, 1.5 x 1076 kg m~” Given these wide differences in the estimated mass-density of the universe, we may say that our hypothesis places the mass-density in the middle range of these textbook values and may therefore be regarded as quite reasonable. If so, then it supports our hypothesis that galaxies have very large omnidirectional speeds and correspond- ing time-dilations relative to us and to one another in proportion to their distance. Indeed, from the standpoint of our angular momentum paradigm, it would seem

puzzling if they didn’t.

Where, then, shali we locate the centre of angular momentum for a gas-like

distribution of galaxies? It all depends on whether we assume that the extent of

that distribution is finite or infinite. If it is finite, then there has to be some unique centre of moment for the momentum of the whole system, in which case we should find some anisotropy in redshifts measured in different directions. On the other hand, if the universe is infinite, then there can be no such unique centre. In either case, the increase in the orbital speeds of bodies in proportion to distance is implied. At the very least, this alternative interpretation of the Hubble redshift provides logical scope for us not to feel compelled to accept, without question, the current dogma of the ‘expanding universe’ and its metaphysical accompaniment,

the “Big Bang’. (See also Roscoe in this volume.)

5.9.2 &lsquo;Black holes&rsquo;

Something else which does not fit with our Normal Realist paradigm is the current conception of a ‘black hole’. This is alleged to be an astronomical body of extremely intense gravity from which nothing, not even light, can escape. In theory it is formed by the death of a massive star which, as it reaches the end of its life, becomes unstable and implodes, due to the force of gravitation, to a point of zero volume and infinite density, which advocates of the theory call ‘a singularity’. This singularity constitutes the centre of a black hole and is hidden from observation by the object's so-called ‘event horizon’. Inside that horizon — so the theory goes — the velocity required for matter to escape from the object’s “gravitational field’ exceeds the speed of light, so that not even rays of light can escape into space. The radius of the horizon is called the Schwarzschild radius, after the German astronomer Karl Schwarzschild, who in 1916 conceived the existence of collapsed stellar bodies that emit no radiation. Black holes, it is claimed, cannot be directly observed because they emit no light. But they can be ‘observed’ — at least, that is the claim — indirectly (i.e., theoretically), by the effects of what are classically conceived as their ‘enormous gravitational fields’

on nearby matter. Some astronomers believe that in 1994 the Hubble Space Telescope provided, in this way, ‘conclusive evidence’ for the existence of a supermassive

black hole at the centre of the M87 galaxy. They calculate that the ‘hole’ has a

mass equal to two to three billion Suns but is no larger than the solar system. They infer the black hole's existence from its energetic effects on an envelope of gas swirling around it at extremely high velocities. Similar evidence suggests to them that a massive black hole with a mass of about 2.6 million Suns lies at the centre

of our own Milky Way Galaxy.

These phenomena are clearly concerned with angular momentum. However, it is typical of angular momentum that for a pair of masses of comparable size, the centre of orbit for those bodies is located in neither of the masses themselves but in the void between. Although commonly called the ‘centre-of-mass’, this centre of the system, has none of the characteristics normally ascribed to bodies. For instance, unlike the bodies themselves, it is completely invisible. Compared with ordinary luminous masses, it is completely ‘black’, and bodies rotate around it. Is

this, then, a ‘black hole’, and if not, then why not?

What sense would it make to explain the ‘blackness’ of this invisible centre of moment as being due to its ‘gravitational entrapment’ of wandering bodies and of the light it would otherwise emit? The fact is that there are ‘centres-of-mass’ which, as masses, simply aren't there, even though they act as though they were ‘gravitational centres’. If the planets of our solar system were bigger and more numerous, then along with the sun they might ali seem to be orbiting around such a ‘black hole’. And if that system were losing energy, by radiating it away into the galactic environment, then what else should we expect but that with the loss of angular momentum the system would gradually shrink around that invisible centre, with ever-increasing orbital speed until, as its angular momentum approached zero, all the masses would come together in what is customarily called ‘gravitational collapse’ And where does that mass disappear to after that? Does it disappear into a mathematical ‘wormhole’, to emerge in some other dimension or universe? We beg leave to think not. The environment provided by the one-and-only universe that (by definition) there is, should be large enough for that collapsed mass to disperse itself into in some form or other of radiated energy

without there having to be any mysterious worm-like monsters to consume it. 1i1

Once again, angular momentum should be sufficient in itself to explain all the phenomena associated with so-called ‘black holes’. In any case, we should surely {according to the paradigm we are exploring, at any rate) be looking for explanations in terms of things actually observable, not things of pure

mathematical abstraction clothed in the fanciful garb of quasi-materiality.

5.9.3 The cosmological microwave background

In 1610, Johannes Kepler, as an argument against the notion of a limitless universe containing an infinite number of stars, cited the fact that the sky is dark at night. If the universe is endless, he reasoned, and uniformly populated with luminous stars, then every line of sight must eventually terminate at the surface of a star, so that the sky should everywhere be bright. The fact that it is not uniformly bright is therefore a paradox which was raised in 1823 by the German astronomer Heinrich Wilhelm Olbers, and its discovery, since widely attributed to

him, now bears his name.

Kepler’s argument that the darkness of the sky betokens the finiteness of the size of the universe seems reasonable on the basis of the assumptions of his time, which did not include the fact, subsequently noted by Hubble, that the frequency of light from the galaxies of stars diminishes with their distance. In view of that fact, it is equally reasonable to assume that in an infinite universe the sky should be bright in the way Kepler reasoned — though not with accumulated visible light, as he supposed, but with light mostly in the far-infrared.

Conventional solutions of this paradox, such as those based, for instance, on comparisons between the lifetimes of stars and the time taken by light from them to reach us, or that the microwave background is a space-temperature relic of the Big Bang, etc., are not consistent with our alternative paradigm. In this altogether different context, Kepler’s reasonable expectation may be claimed to have been fulfilled in experiments with the first Telstar communication satellite, in which two scientists, Arno Penzias and Robert Wilson, measured excess radio noise that seemed to come from the sky in a completely isotropic fashion. Later, more

precise measurements made by the Cosmic Background Explorer (COBE)

satellite launched in late 1989 confirmed these findings, which were interpreted in conventional cosmogonic terms as characteristic of a blackbody radiation of 2.735°K. The members of the COBE team reported that these measurements reveal a statistical distribution of angular fluctuations that appear different from random noise.*> This, they believe, provides the first evidence for the departure from exact isotropy that theoretical cosmologists predicted on the basis of the way they think galaxies and clusters of galaxies condensed out of an otherwise

structureless universe.

But however that may be, apart from the remnant of smal! fluctuations discussed above (which comes to about one part in 100,000), the observed cosmic

microwave background radiation

... exhibits a high degree of isotropy, a zeroth order fact that presents both satisfaction and difficulty for a comprehensive theory. On the one hand, it provides a strong justification for the assumption of homogeneity and isotropy that is common to most cosmological models. On the other hand, such homogeneity and isotropy are difficult to explain because of the ‘Jight-horizon’ problem.

In any event, it is clear that in measuring galactic redshifts or the frequency of background radiation we are never actually observing either galaxies receding or space cooling with expansion after the “big bang’. These, like all observations, are matters of interpretation and, on this cosmical scale of speculation, eminently

diagnostic. As Fred Hoyle writes, in his Nature of the Universe (1950):

I cannot see any good reason for preferring the big bang idea. Indeed, it seems to me in the philosophical sense to be a distinctly unsatisfactory notion, since it puts the basic assumption out of sight where it can never be challenged by direct appeal to observation.

It seems, then, highly unlikely that this idea of the ‘big bang’ would ever have been seriously entertained other than as a putative explanation of the Hubble redshift, on the false assumption that there can be no satisfactory explanation of the redshift other than as a Doppler effect of recessional motion. Since, as we have shown, observing these redshifts is not logically equivalent to observing the galaxies receding, that particular explanation, with its intrinsically absurd and unprovable implications, cannot be the ‘Hobson’s choice’ that its proponents

claim.

5.10 Summary

To our ancestors, who were the constructors of the language which directs our thinking, it seemed that the world was flat; and then it seemed that our earth was the centre of the universe; that the sun, stars and planets moved around it; that there is some sort of invisible force dragging everything downwards towards the centre of the earth, hence into an infernal volcanic abyss; that length and time are different and unrelated measures; that time is the same everywhere, in space as in

matter, regardless of whether that matter is stationary or moving;

Only gradually, and in an entirely circumstantial, historically meandering sort of way, have we learned that those appearances have so often deceived us, that — in science, at any rate — the dictum ‘seeing is believing’ has proved to be highly flawed. People who first saw a whale swore that it was a fish, and those who were first confronted with the phenomenon of men riding horses believed they were seeing creatures that were half horse and half man. These strange creatures were named ‘centaurs’, and the name stuck for long enough to become established as an item of language that can still be found in dictionaries. Similarly, words like ‘force of gravity’, ‘the speed of light’, ‘photons’, and so on, have largely signified chimeras, which exist nowhere but in the language that created them, the language we still use to think with today. Gradually, and out of necessity, the emphasis in science has shifted away from expanding and enriching the common language towards dissociating itself altogether, in many cases, from

that common language.

Unfortunately, in a no less unsystematic and probing way, science never completely casts off the conceptual restraints of tradition. Indeed it compounds the problem by feeding into the traditional mish-mash, all sorts of exotic concepts which have no more than temporary descriptive value, but which nevertheless, in the form of jargon, become stuck in common and scientific language. Without the occasional conceptual sweeping-up operations that rational philosophy should

provide in its role of what John Locke called ‘under-labourer to science’, the

factory-floor of physics would soon become smothered in the swarf of conceptual

confusion.

However, it is only in hindsight that this logical clearing-up operation is ever really possible. We cannot know, ahead of time, which interpretations of experience, even in the plainest of laboratory observations, will turn out to be useful and which will prove altogether false and inhibiting. The fact, for instance, that light is reflected, that there is a measurable time-interval between sending it and seeing its reflection, that it can be blocked by obstacles placed in its path, and so on, does not necessarily signify that it travels in a vacuum like waves in a pond. Nor does the fact that its energy is quantised necessarily signify that it travels in wave-corpuscles in a bullet-like way. Certainly, these seemed the most obvious and practically efficient interpretations at the time. To have pointed out that they were not necessarily the most logically efficient ways of interpreting the data, or that at their inferential edges, they ran into pure nonsense would have seemed mere philosophical nit-picking. For as long, therefore, as these facile ways of thinking and speaking remained useful, they were retained for practical

purposes, gradually becoming embedded in common language.

However, as our experiences of the physical world in the various sectors of research have progressed, so the adequacy of the language that was originally invented to describe that world has proved, not surprisingly, to become more and more suspect. This is to the extent that there is now, in some areas of science — notably, physics — an almost complete dissociation between the common language and the language of the profession. This does not mean that in itself, the language of the profession makes some superior kind of overall sense. As physicists are the first to admit among themselves — if not in the media — it definitely doesn’t. For instance, as the late Richard Feynman declared ‘No-one understands quantum physics.’ The same might also be said of relativity. Meanwhile, in the conceptual limbo between the two theories, nothing flourishes but the weeds and brambles of

theoretical contrivance.

What has been presented in this chapter is not just another of those intellectual contrivances. Far from being a new theory, it is a non-theory, a methodical stripping-away of traditional theoretical dross to reveal as much as possible of the actual phenomena that the dross has now all but concealed. In this way we have discerned that light as it appears to us, in our eyes and optical instruments, is all we need logically to explain both the relativistic time-delay and the quantum instantaneity of distant interaction. All the theoretical rationale as to what light is and does when shining in nothing, travelling unseen and inscrutably between A to B — enticing researchers like Michelson and Morley to initiate the academic ritual called ‘Relativity’ that is perennially imposed on physics students — was never logically necessary. We have also claimed that behind all the academic juju language of in vacuo ‘fields-forces’, ‘ethers’ and so on, the concept of angular momentum, which is common to al! motions called ‘gravitational’, ‘electrical’, ‘magnetic’, efc., is sufficient in itself for explaining not only the orbital motions of bodies but also the distant correlation of those orbital motions,

with no need whatsoever of the usual jargonistic overlay.

The mathematical details of this digital-physics, angular momentum synthesis are not included here for the reason that they are available in other publications elsewhere.*? The main thrust of. this present argument is to distinguish, as clearly as possible, the basic differences between two historical paradigms. In the one, as envisaged by the followers of Democritus and Descartes, the world we see around us, with its ordinary sights, sounds, colours and so on is unreal, being ‘all in the mind’. In that way of thinking (setting aside the Epistemological Problem it raises, see section 3, above), to find out what is ‘real’ we have to dismantle things right down to what Democritus conceived as their ultimately unsplittable, ‘atomic’ parts, whose mechanical interactions are preconceived as the root-causes of everything, including life and all its concomitants. In this classical way of thinking, all those things which common language identifies in terms of non-material qualities such as beauty, goodness,

justice, and intention (with all our moral judgements upon it) and so on — and

even observation itself — are thus seen as no more than illusions, or ‘conventions’ as Democritus called them. By contrast, in the opposite paradigm, stemming from the work of Moore, et al., on which this present dissertation is based, the search for reality begins and ends on that ordinary common-language level. This is by a systematic, logical and scientific method of processing and analysing, if only in retrospect, all the observational data that are presented to us in physical experience. In physics, it means that we start with phenomena, replete with their fully integrated and inseparable sets of the observational dimensions, mass, length and time. We then find that in reducing these dimensions, a limit is reached at which, instead of ending-up with infinitely hard and unsplittable bail-bearing-like bits of matter, any further reduction of, e.g., mass, increases other measures, such as length and time, and vice versa. This is all in a see-saw-like way in which the integrity of the whole observational package is strictly maintained, such that the product of all the fundamental observational measures remains the same, no matter what. This defines the ultimate datum-level of physical analysis which physicists know as Planck’s constant, 4. This is not the absolute ‘material reality’, or ‘atom’, that Democritus envisaged. It is no more nor less than the absolute limit of reduction at which all reality as we know it vanishes. in that limit, far from finding some ultimately substantial ‘thing-in-itself?, whose mass, position and mechanical motion are absolutely determined, we reach a level at which all determinacy simply disappears. Yet / is as irreducible as anything Democritus could ever have imagined. As Victor Weisskopf admitted to this author, at the ‘Atom-smashing’ laboratory at CERN, in 1975: ‘Certainly, nothing we have ever

done here at CERN or in any other laboratory has ever succeeded in splitting h’.

Hopefully, then, without getting bogged-down in a morass of details, enough has been said in this chapter to distinguish clearly between the two paradigms concerned. They are the complete logical antitheses of each other. Although logically and psychologically incommensurable in accordance with Kuhn’s criterion for true paradigms, they have both, to some extent, co-existed in

an altogether mixed-up, eclectic way throughout the history of scientific thought.

It is our view that the continual seeking to reconcile these irreconcilable thought- systems — the failure clearly to keep them logically distinct and separate — is what so chronically confuses our Western tradition of theoretical physics. In this confused situation, claims to comprehend the incomprehensible have created a veritable priesthood, whose language of abstract mathematics is as unintelligible

to ordinary folk as was ever the Latin of the Medieval Church.

Perhaps it is now too late for our society fully to retract this deeply embedded scholastic tradition around which our language and institutions have evolved. Thankfully, however, for the integrity of natural philosophy, the Democritan Materialists have never had things entirely their own way. Phenomenalists from Socrates and Aristotle to Berkeley, Mach and beyond have been busy, meantime, evolving the logical counter-paradigm which, in its blundering and almost antisocial way, has somehow, by some miracle, produced the relativity of Einstein and the fundamental indeterminacy of Planck, Heisenberg and Bohr. Perhaps, with all vestiges of Democritan, mechanistic ideas of atoms and atomic effluvia stripped from Einsteinian relativity in the manner we have suggested — and which Mach would surely have appreciated — relativity might well come into its own. Thus might the way be cleared for consummating the revolution in physics that these relationist philosophers and physicists had all but completed — and undoubtedly would have done, if only they had grasped and

uprooted the nettle of what was traditionally misconceived as ‘light-velocity’.
