Chapter 7: A Perspective on Mach's Principle and the Consequent Discovery of Major New Phenomenology in Spiral Discs

David F. Roscoe

David F Roscoe

This chapter begins with speculation on the realisation of Mach's Principle, and we came to the details of the present analysis via the formulation of two questions: (a) Can a globally inertial space and time be associated with a non- trivial global matter distribution? (b) If so, what are the general properties of such

a global distribution?

The analysis ' led us to conclude that a globally inertial space and time can be associated with a non-trivial global matter distribution, and that this

distribution is necessarily fractal, with $D = 2$.

Gravitational processes are then understood in terms of perturbations of this equilibrium space-matter-time structure. We give a very brief overview of these gravitational processes, specifically applied to spiral galaxies. These considerations have led directly to the discovery of a completely new phenomenology in spiral discs — which is now in the main-stream astrophysical literature.? Here we review this phenomenology, and give a brief account of how

this work is currently progressing.

7.1 Introduction

The ideas underlying what is now known as ‘Mach's Principle’ can be traced to Berkeley (1710, 1721) for which a good contemporary discussion can be found in Popper (1953). Berkeley's essential insight, formulated as a rejection of Newton's ideas of absolute space, was that the motion of any object had no meaning except insofar as that motion was referred to some other object, or set of objects. Mach

went much further than Berkeley, when he said: °

t Editors’ footnote: see previous chapter, section 6.8.

I have remained to the present day the only one who insists upon referring the law of inertia to the earth and, in the case of motions of great spatial and temporal extent, to the fixed stars.

In this way, Mach formulated the idea that, ultimately, inertial frames should be

defined with respect to the average rest frame of the visible universe.

It is a matter of history that Einstein was greatly influenced by Mach's ideas as expressed in the latter's The Science of Mechanics (see for example Pais 1982) and believed that they were incorporated in his field equations so long as space was closed (Einstein 1950). The modern general relativistic analysis gives detailed quantitative support to this latter view, showing how Mach's Principle can be considered as a consequence of the field equations when appropriate conditions are specified on an initial hyper-surface in a closed evolving universe. In fact, in answer to Mach's question as to what would happen to inertia if mass was progressively removed from the universe, Lynden-Bell, Katz & Bicak (1995) point out that in a closed Friedmann universe the maximum radius and the duration of its existence both shrink to zero as mass is progressively removed. Thus, it is a matter of record that a satisfactory incorporation of Mach's Principle within General Relativity (GR) can be attained when the constraint of closure is

imposed.

However, there is a hard line point of view: in practice, when we talk of physical space (and the space composed of the set of all inertial frames in particular), we mean a space in which distances and displacements can be determined — but these concepts have meaning only insofar as they refer to relationships within material systems. Likewise, when we refer to elapsed physical time, we mean a measurable degree of ordered change (process) occurring within a given physical system. Thus, all our concepts of measurable ‘space and time’ are irreducibly connected to the existence of material systems and to process within such systems — which is why the closed Friedmann solutions are so attractive. However, from this, we can also choose to conclude

that any theory (for example, GR notwithstanding its closed Friedmann solutions)

that allows an internally consistent discussion of an empty inertial space-time

must be non-fundamental at even the classical level.

To progress, we take the point of view that, since all our concepts of measurable ‘space and time’ are irreducibly connected to the existence of material systems and to process within such systems, then these concepts are, in essence, metaphors for the relationships that exist between the individual particles (whatever these might be) within these material systems. Since the most simple conception of physical space and time is that provided by inertial space and time,

we are then led to two simple questions:

Is it possible to associate a globally inertial space and time with a non-trivial global matter distribution and, if so, what are the fundamental properties of this

distribution?

In the context of the simple model analysed, the present chapter finds definitive answers to these questions so that: e A globally inertial space and time can be associated with a non-trivial global distribution of matter; ¢ This global distribution is necessarily fractal with D=2

7.2 General overview

We start from the position that conceptions of an empty inertial spatio-temporal continuum are essentially non-physical and are incapable of providing sound foundations for fundamental theory. To put this another way:

There is no such thing as space, except insofar as it refers to the relations between material objects, and there is no such thing as time, except insofar as it refers to process within material systems.* According to this view, the fact that GR admits an empty inertial spatio-temporal continuum as a special case (and was

originally derived as a generalisation of such a construct) implies that it is not

* Editors’ footnote: see propositions 11 and 12 in “Towards a Consensus’, Chapter 4.

sufficiently primitive to act as a basis from which fundamental theories of

cosmology can be constructed.

Starting with a model universe consisting of objects which have no other properties except identity (and hence enumerability) existing in a formless continuum, we show how it is possible to project spatio-temporal metric properties from the objects onto the continuum. By considering idealised dynamical equilibrium conditions (which arise as a limiting case of a particular free parameter going to zero), we are then able to show how a globally inertial spatio-temporal continuum is necessarily identified with a material distribution which has a fractal dimension D=2 in this projected space. This is a striking result since it bears a very close resemblance to the cosmic reality for the low-to-

medium redshift regime.

However, this idealised limiting-case material distribution is distinguished from an ordinary material distribution in the sense that the individual particles of which it is comprised are each in a state of arbitrarily directed motion, but with equal-magnitude velocities for all particles — and in this sense is more like a quasi-photon gas distribution. For this reason, we interpret the distribution as a rudimentary representation of an inertial material vacuum, and present it as the appropriate physical background within which gravitational processes (as conventionally understood) can be described as point-source perturbations of an

inertial spatio-temporal-material background.

7.2.1 Overview of the non-relativistic formalism

In order to clarify the central arguments and to minimise conceptual problems in this initial development, we assume that the model universe is stationary in the sense that the overall statistical properties of the material distribution do not evolve in any way. Whilst this was intended merely as a simplifying assumption, it has the fundamental effect of making the development inherently non- relativistic (in the sense that the system evolves within a curved metric three-

space, rather than being a geodesic structure within a spacetime continuum).

The fatter consequence arises in the following way: since the model universe is assumed to be stationary, then there is no requirement to import a pre- determined concept of ‘time’ into the discussion at the beginning — although the qualitative notion of a generalised ‘temporal ordering’ is assumed. The arguments used then lead to a formal model which allows the natural introduction of a generalised temporal ordering parameter, and this formal model is invariant with respect to any transformation of this latter parameter which leaves the absolute ordering of events unchanged. This arbitrariness implies that the formal model is incomplete, and can be completed only by the imposition of an additional condition which constrains the temporal ordering parameter to be identifiable with some model of physical time. It is then found that such a model of physical time, defined in terms of ‘system process’, arises automatically from the assumed

isotropies within the system.

In summary, the assumption that the system is stationary leads to the emergent concept of a physical ‘spatio-temporal continuum’ which partitions into a metric three-space together with a distinct model of physical time defined in terms of ordered material process in the metric three-space. The fractal D=2

inertial universe then arises as an idealised limiting case.

7.3 The starting point

If it is impossible to conceive of a physical spatio-temporal continuum in the absence of matter, then it follows that we need a theory of the world according to which (roughly speaking) notions of metrical space and time are somehow projected out of primary relationships between objects. Our starting point is to

consider the calibration of a radial measure which conforms to these ideas.

Consider the following perfectly conventional procedure which assumes that we ‘know’ what is meant by a given radial displacement, R say. On a large enough scale ( > 10° light years, say), we can reasonably assume it is possible to write down a relationship describing the amount of mass contained within a given

spherical volume: say,

$$M = U(R) \tag{1}$$

where U is, in principle, determinable. Because Mf obviously increases as R increases, then U is said to be monotonic, with the consequence that the above

relationship can be inverted to give R=G(M) (2) which, because (1) is unremarkable, is also unremarkable.

In the conventional view, (1) is logically prior to (2); however, it is perfectly possible to reverse the logical priority of (1) and (2) so that, in effect, we can choose to define the radial measure in terms of (2) rather than assume that it is known by some independent means. If this is done then, immediately, we have made it impossible to conceive of radial measure in the absence of matter. With this as a starting point, we are able to construct a completely Machian Cosmology

in a way outlined in the following section.

7.4 The end point

Invariant ideas of spatial and temporal measure arise naturally within the course of the analysis which led, finally, to the conclusion that it is possible to have a non-trivial matter distribution irreducibly associated with an equilibrium (inertial)

universe — but only if mass is distributed with fractal dimension two.*

7.5 A quasi-fractal mass distribution law, A/ ~ R*: the evidence

A basic assumption of the Standard Model of modern cosmology is that, on some scale, the universe is homogeneous; however, in early responses to suspicions that the accruing data was more consistent with Charlier's conceptions of an hierarchical universe (Charlier, 1908, 1922, 1924) than with the requirements of the Standard Model, de Vaucouleurs (1970) showed that, within wide limits, the available data satisfied a mass distribution law M = r '*, whilst Peebles (1980) found M~ r'*3, The situation, from the point of view of the Standard Model, has continued to deteriorate with the growth of the data-base to the point that, (Baryshev et al. (1995))

«the scale of the largest inhomogeneities (discovered to date) is comparable with the extent of the surveys, so that the largest known structures are limited by the boundaries of the survey in which they are detected,

For example, several recent redshift surveys, such as those performed by Huchra et al. (1983), Giovanelli and Haynes (1986), De Lapparent et al. (1988), Broadhurst et al. (1990), Da Costa et al. (1994) and Vettolani et al. (1994) etc have discovered massive structures such as sheets, filaments, superclusters and voids, and show that large structures are common features of the observable universe. The most significant conclusion to be drawn from all of these surveys is that the scale of the largest inhomogeneities observed is comparable with the

Spatial extent of the surveys themselves.

In recent years, several quantitative analyses of both pencil-beam and wide-angle surveys of galaxy distributions have been performed: three recent examples are given by Joyce, Montuori & Labini (1999) who analysed the CfA2- South catalogue to find fractal behaviour with D=1.9 40.1; Labini & Montuori (1998) analysed the APM-Stromlo survey to find fractal behaviour with D=2.120.1 whiist Labini, Montuori & Pietronero (1998) analysed the Perseus- Pisces survey to find fractal behaviour with D=2.020.1. There are many other papers of this nature in the literature all supporting the view that, out to medium depth at least, galaxy distributions appear to be fractal with D =2.

This latter view is now widely accepted (for example, see Wu, Lahav & Rees (1999)), and the open question has become whether or not there is a transition to homogeneity on some sufficiently large scale. For example, Scaramella et ai. (1998) analyse the ESO Slice Project redshift survey, whilst Martinez et al. (1998) analyse the Perseus-Pisces, the APM-Stromlo and the 1.2- Jy IRAS redshift surveys, with both groups finding evidence for a cross-over to homogeneity at large scales. In response, the Scaramella ef a/. analysis has been criticised on various grounds by Joyce et al. (1999).

So, to date, evidence that galaxy distributions are fractal with D ~ 2 on small to medium scales is widely accepted, but there is a lively open debate over

the existence, or otherwise, of a cross-over to homogeneity on large scales.

To summarise, there is considerable debate centred around the question of whether or not the matter in the universe is distributed fractally or not, with supporters of the big-bang picture arguing that, basically, it is not, whilst the supporters of the fractal picture argue that it is, with the weight of evidence supporting D ~ 2. This latter position corresponds exactly with the picture predicted by the present approach.

7.6 Gravitating Systems

Theories of gravitation are derived by assuming some conception of an inertial background (for example, Newton's absolute space, or the flat space-time of Einstein's Special Relativity) and then perturbing it in some way to generate the required theory. Clearly, the assumed nature of the inertial background has a fundamenta! influence on the structure of the final theory. So, we can expect that perturbations of an inertial background which is irreducibly associated with a fractal D = 2 matter distribution will produce a gravitation theory that is quite

distinct from existing theories.

We proceeded as follows:

e Perform a point-source perturbation of the inertial background to obtain a point-source theory, and confirm that it reproduces Newtonian gravitation in the point-source case;

e Extend to a two point-source perturbation, and confirm that the Newtonian two-body theory is reproduced;

* Use the insights gained to write down the N-body theory — which is generally intractable;

e Note that in conditions of very high symmetry, and in the limit from an N- body theory to a continuum theory, there exists the possibility of a tractable form emerging;

e As an example, apply conditions of cylindrical symmetry and use the resulting equations as a model of an idealised spiral galaxy (i.e., a perfect disc with no lumps and bumps).

7.7 Spiral galaxies When applied to a mass distribution with perfect disc symmetry, the theory leads

to a set of highly non-linear equations of motion. The application of additional

constraints conceming the nature of the idealised disc leads to a power-law class of solutions for the circular velocities, given by

V = AR? where V is the circular velocity, R is the radial displacement and A,@ are parameters which vary between distributions.

We use the power law as a model of the circular velocities in idealised spiral galaxies, and demonstrate how well it works by reference to the data from a sample of 305 spiral galaxies published by Courteau 1997. Figure 1 gives the

basic scatter plot of In A, @ computed for all galaxies in the sample.

Detailed investigation shows that the variation is this plot can be virtualiy all accounted for by variations in the luminosity properties of the galaxies used in

the sample. In particular, we find that the model In A = -2.870 — 0.3804 + 07.648 + 0.503M + 0.0128)

accounts for about 98% of the variation in Fig. 1.

Here M represents the absolute magnitude and S represents the surface brightness of each object in the sample. Fig. 2 plots In A against the model, and we see an

Similar results can be obtained from any of the four samples we have analysed. Thus, we can consider the power-law description of circular velocities

in idealised spiral discs to be virtually perfect when applied to large ensembles.

7.8 The discrete states phenomenology: hypothesis

The work which considered the goodness of the power-law model (above) was preceded by a pilot study of a very small sample of 12 galaxies — used as a means of getting a ‘feel’ for how the analysis of large samples might progress. Apart from noting a strong (In A, %) correlation, we also noted that In 4 computed for this small sample clustered, in a remarkably tight way, around the values (3.5, 4.0, 4.5, 5.0). We initially considered this to be almost certainly coincidental, but worth investigating at a later date. Note that these In A values are dependent on the distance scaling used. In this case, the distance scaling assumed H = 50 km/sec/Mpe.

ra

Subsequently, we obtained our first very large sample of data on 900 spirals measured by Mathewson, Ford & Buchhorn 1992 and folded (a necessary

data reduction process) by Persic & Salucci 1995. Most of the basic power-law

fitting work was originally done on this sample (Roscoe 1999). The pilot study of the original 12 objects had raised the idea that In 4 ~ (3.5,4.0,4.5,5.0) for H = 50 km/sec/Mpc.

The current large sample was scaled using H = 85 km/sec/Mpc for which the corresponding In A values are given by In 4 { (3.85, 4.24, 4.72, 5.06). Thus, we had the strong prior hypothesis that, for new samples calibrated with H = 85 km/sec/Mpe, we should find In A = (3.85, 4.24, 4.72, 5.06).

7.10 Comments

¢ With the exception of the Mathewson ef a/. 1992 sample (Figs. 3,4) the 4- peak is generally weak. This is because this peak corresponds to the low luminosity end of the samples. Such objects are very much under- represented in the samples, except for Mathewson ef al. 1992 data - hence weak A-peaks.

e The discrete-state signal, in the form of the A, B, C and D peaks, is confirmed at vanishingly small odds of it being a chance effect -formally, at the level of 10° over the four samples considered as independent given the prior hypothesis raised on the sample of twelve objects.

7.11 What does it mean? We know, from Section 7, that In. A = F(M,S,a). Thus, if In A is constrained to

have discrete values, (k,, ky, k3,k4) say, then we find F(M,S,a) = k, i = 1,2,3,4. Consequently, at a phenomenological level, we can say that spiral

galaxies are constrained to evolve over one of four distinct surfaces in (M, 5, a)

space.

But what deeper meaning can we infer? A straightforward way of possibly understanding the meaning of this phenomenology goes as follows: in the wider world of non-linear dynamical systems, it is quite common to come across systems of non-linear differential equations that can be consistently solved only when a certain algebraic condition between system parameters is satisfied. If this algebraic condition happened to be a quartic equation in three parameters, then we would have a situation very similar to the discrete states phenomenology outlined

above.

In fact, subsequent work has shown that this is exactly the situation in the theory which suggested the power-law formulation in the first place. That is, the theory not only provides the power-law structures which led to the discovery of major new phenomenology in the first place, but it also provides a straightforward

explanation of it.

In other words, we have powerful circumstantial evidence supporting the original Machian viewpoint — that there is no such thing as space, except insofar as it is defined in terms of relations between objects, and there is no such thing as

time except insofar as it is defined in terms of process in material systems.

7.12 Conclusions By taking a particular interpretation of Mach's Principle seriously (see section 2 and other writers in this volume), we have arrived at a conception of inertial space

and time which:

1. is irreducibly associated with a fractal D=2 distribution of material which corresponds very closely with what is observed on medium scales at least;

2. leads to a theory of gravitation which, when applied to model spiral galaxies gives a description of circular velocities in idealised discs which, taken statistically over a large number of spirals, performs with a very high precision and:

® led to the discovery of major new phenomenology in spiral discs;

e provides a detailed explanation of this phenomenology.

Chapter 8: A Short Essay on Closed Systems, Hierarchy and Radiation

George Galeczki

8.1 Matter: continuous or discrete?

Man’s first awareness of matter led him to wonder whether it is continuous or

discrete. In mathematics, discretum and continuum are reflected in the concepts of natural numbers and real numbers, the former being associated with counting, and the latter with the appearance of bodies that are actually observable. It is worth mentioning that, although atomism was accepted early on by chemists, it was rejected in the latter years of the 19th century by physicists and philosophers such as Ostwald and Mach and that this certainly contributed to the suicide of Ludwig Boltzmann. However, champions of the continuity of matter — the most prominent being Prof. Sachs of SUNY Buffalo, USA — have kept the view going

up till now.

Some twenty-five years after reading, for the first time, Schrödinger’s gem Science and Humanism: Physics in our time (Cambridge, 1951) I still find his argument for discreteness/atomism -- taken over from Anaximenes — the most

convincing. Therefore I quote the text:

If you try to assimilate Anaximenes’ idea, you naturally come to think that change of properties of matter, say on rarefaction, must be caused by its parts receding at greater distance from each other. But it is extremely difficult to accomplish this in your imagination, if you think of matter as gapless continuum. What should recede from what? The mathematicians of the same epoch considered a geometrical line as consisting of points. That is perhaps all right if you leave it alone. But if it is a material line and you begin to stretch it - would not its points recede from each other and leave gaps between them? For the stretching cannot produce new points and the same set of points cannot go to cover a greater interval. From these difficulties, which reside in the mysterious character of the continuum, the easiest escape is the one taken by the atomists, namely to regard matter as consisting from the outset of isolated ‘points’ or rather of small particles, which recede from each other on rarefaction and approach to closer distances on condensation, while remaining themselves unchanged ...The particles themselves remain unchanged; only their geometrical constellation changes,

After awakening from the fascination of Maxwellian field theory, I

became a convert towards atomism and all my experience as a physicist reinforces

my conviction. Without atomism all important concepts like structure, configuration, entropy, statistical mechanics and so on would be meaningless. If matter were a continuous, even motion would be impossible, as pointed out by Parmenides. Variable density is conceivable only as different numbers of discrete particles in a constant unit-volume. Here it is pertinent to point out that Special Relativity (SR) is compatible only with (continuous) field theory —~ like that of Maxwell — in which the four independent coordinates x, y, z, ¢ and their partial derivatives are on equal footing. It is incompatible with particle dynamics, where

the time-dependent coordinates x(2), y(#), z(2) move with the particle.

Having said that, have to point out that the mathematical description of fluids and of wave-like phenomena is enormously facilitated by the so called hydrodynamic approximation, which allows the use of continuous and derivable functions, as well as of equations with partial derivatives. We must, however, always keep in mind that this is no more than an approximation. To realise that waves are multi-particle ensembles is to dispose of the (in)famous ‘wave-particle dualism’ according to which a single particle is a wave, so that quantum

mechanics need no longer be plagued by that absurd notion.

8.2 Systems, closed systems, self-confined systems

If matter were a continuum, then it would fill the whole Universe and all space. However, once the discreteness of matter is accepted, we can meaningfully discuss the concept of system. A collection of free and non-interacting particles cannot sensibly be called a system. In order to conceive of a system of particles, some kind of interaction between them is essential. If the interaction is strong enough, as in solid materials, then the system will occupy a finite region/volume, with a reasonably constant shape and could be called self-confined. If the interaction between the particles is negligible, as in many gases, the particles will scatter in all directions, filling the Universe, and they will not constitute a system. Liquids, in which the interaction between particles is considerably stronger than in gases, do not preserve even an approximately constant shape, although their

volumes remain more or less constant. One usually encloses fluids in containers,

which means that one adds to the original collection of particles solid walls, thus calling them closed systems. The necessary presence of walls introduces a clear heterogeneity and therefore complications. For instance, there are strong interactions within the walls and also inelastic collisions (implying energy transfer) between the particles of the fluid and the walls, Since, in the following, I mostly consider se/f-confined systems, the meaning of interaction has to be made explicit. In the spirit of Newton’s second and third laws of dynamics — the only coherent dynamics we have — all interactions are forces, that have the ability to

change the magnitude and direction of the linear momentum of moving particles.

8.3 Closed systems and conservation principles

Newton’s third principle, of action = reaction :

$$\mathbf{F}_{12}(r; v; t) = -\mathbf{F}_{21}(r; v; t) \tag{1}$$

where r and v denote scalar relative distance and relative velocity, respectively, expresses the fact that the sum of every pair of inter-particle forces is zero. This amounts to the definition of a closed system. In conjunction with the law of motion:

$$d(m_1\mathbf{v}_1)/dt = \mathbf{F}_{12}\;;\quad d(m_2\mathbf{v}_2)/dt = \mathbf{F}_{21} \tag{2}$$

equation (1) leads after time integration to the conservation of linear momentum: m,¥, + m2v2 = constant, (3) and after vector multiplication r x , to the conservation of angular momentum: ry X (mV) + 2 X (move) = constant, (4)

provided that the forces are central, i.e., oriented along the instantaneous direction $\mathbf{r}_{12} = \mathbf{r}_1 - \mathbf{r}_2$.

Following Eddington (1920), Van Flanderm (1995) argued forcefully that a delay in gravitational interaction — destroying this condition — would have

“disastrous consequences because conservation of angular momentum would be

destroyed’.

Scalar multiplication of both terms in (2) with v, and v2 leads to the

$$0.5(m_1v_1^2 + m_2v_2^2) = \int \mathbf{F}_{12}\cdot d\mathbf{r} \tag{5}$$

If the inter-particle force is derivable from a potential $U$: $\mathbf{F}_{12} = -\text{grad}(U_{12})$,

then (5) expresses the conservation of the total mechanical energy: 0.5(m)v)? + mv?) + U2 = 0. (6)

It is surely remarkable that the equality of action and reaction leads to three conservation laws in a closed mechanical system for the three distinct but inter- dependent dynamical quantities, linear momentum, angular momentum and energy. It is also, surely, significant that (1) holds for any relative distance r and any relative velocity v at every moment 4. Moreover, Newton’s third law stresses the reciprocity of the interaction between any two pairs of particles, as signified by the label inter-action. Equations (1) — (6), which are valid for any pairs of particles, can be extended to a system of n particles and — holding for the same moment, ¢ — require a unique, global time, which can be shown to be associated

with a unique, centre-of-mass frame of reference.

The concepts of linear and angular momentum remain valid for any system in general. Closed systems are not allowed to translate, or to rotate by themselves, i.e., under the action of purely internal forces. In the presence of external forces, however, they are no longer closed and could therefore be translated, or rotated ad lib, Over time, the concept of the conservation of mechanical energy has gained such popularity that it has been extended to systems in general and the definition of energy has been generalised to include all kinds of non-mechanical energy (electric, magnetic, chemical, nuclear, etc.), in

order to conform with the conservation of the total energy.

One might remark that closed, self-confined systems are, generally, bound systems, in which motion is either periodical and recurrent or quasi-periodical.

Particle orbits are seldom closed (circular, elliptical), since pure 1/r? interactions

of the Newton-Coulomb type — together with Hooke’s law -kr — being the only allowing closed orbits, are the exception rather than the rule.' There are also bound systems with open orbits (e.g., spirals, rosettes etc.) to be considered, confined as these always are to finite volumes. Nevertheless, both linear and angular momenta are separately conserved. There are also, of course, many other

sorts of open systems, with open orbits.

If some perturbation due to a body that is external to a bound system is detected, one has to enlarge the initial system by including that external body. For example, the two-body Sun-Earth system could be seen as an approximation of the Sun-Earth-Jupiter system which, in tur, is an approximation of the Solar System. In every approximation the problem is solved in the corresponding centre-of-mass frame of reference. It is instructive to consider, in this connection, the (in)famous perihelion precession of the planet Mercury that was claimed to be one of the experimental tests of General Relativity (GR). In fact, the total precession of the Mercury perihelion is about 5600"/century. The ‘lion’s share’ of about 5000” is due to the so-called equinoctial precession, followed by the perturbation of planet Jupiter, leaving only a remnant of about 43”, is of unknown origin. A tiny mistake in the approximately two-century-old classical calculations could well change the magnitude and even the sign of the residual ‘anomaly’. It seems that Rudolph Nedved of Brno, Czechoslovakia, has found that the error of 46.3” is due to using the heliocentric, instead of the barycentric, solar system

centre-of-mass frame of reference (Toth-Maatian review, 5, 2459, 1986).4

' Editors’ footnote: This is essentially Bertrand’s Theorem ~ see following chapter by Osborne.

* Note by D F Roscoe:

Some year ago, Tom Phipps sent me a copy of Nedved’s paper, asking for my opinion of his analysis. Whilst I found it difficult to follow his arguments, I also found it almost impossible to believe his implied assertion that the 43” anomaly arose because astronomers had performed the orbital calculation in the heliocentric frame, rather than in the barycentric frame which is the proper one for such calculations. Accordingly, I set out to make the numerical calculations myself, using a Jupiter-Saturn-Sun-Mercury solar system. I found no evidence to support Nedved’s assertions. However, what I did find was that the predicted perihelion advance was highly dependent on the epoch assumed for the calculation — that is, upon the initial configuration of the major planets (essentially Saturn plus Jupiter). I have forgotten the exact figures, but as a ball-park estimate, the perihelion advance varied by as much as + 50"/century. It follows that the present

As a final remark I mention that Newtonian dynamics can be easily expressed in accord with Mach’s principle by writing equation (2) for masses ‘i’ and ‘j’, multiplying the first equation by m; and the second by m; and subtracting them. The resulting equations will contain, in accordance with Mach’s principle, only the differences rj; — rj, provided the interaction force is a function of relative

distances and their time derivatives and inertial mass don’t change with velocity.

The uniqueness of the time-parameter 1 is, of course, of vital importance, otherwise the relative velocity would not equal d(1j— r;)/dt = vj — y; . Ina Machian theory, distances are instantaneous distances and time is independent of position

and velocity.

8.4 Radiation and open systems

The conserved quantities of a closed system could change only if the system exchanges energy with its surroundings. Energy transport from or to the system can be accompanied by matter transport in the form of particles that are with or without ‘rest mass’. A system which exchanges no energy and no particles with the surrounding world is called — to use the thermodynamic terminology — a microcanonical ensemble. We shall shortly enquire whether these microcanonical ensembles could or could not exist in principle. Among the very few candidates are the simplest atoms and molecules and the ideal superconducting loops in which no Joule-heating due to collisions among charge carriers among themselves and with crystal lattices occurs. However, the tendency of atomic hydrogen to form bi-atomic molecules points toward an instability of atomic hydrogen, which means that even the simplest atomic system is not an ideal microcanonical ensemble. Large systems consisting of many particles, atoms or molecules, for which the concept of temperature can be defined, always lose energy by radiation, provided that the temperature is greater than 0° K. This so-called thermal

radiation is emitted with no regard to the existence of potential absorbers, as the

agreement between the observed value and GR’s prediction is almost certainly coincidental and will change significantly with time. I must stress that these calculations should, ideally, be repeated independently.

cosmological microwave background radiation attests. Moreover, all kinds of electromagnetic radiation, including lasers, are emitted without a@ priori ‘knowledge’ of future absorbers — notwithstanding the contrary opinions of the supporters of delayed action-at-a-distance without photons (G. Burniston-Brown, M. Sachs) and of action-at-a-distance in zero ‘proper time’ — also without photons (by P. Marmet, V. Pope, ef al.). As long the interaction between two masses, or charges, is static, it could equally weil be described in terms of mathematically isomorphic point-like particles or static fields. For rapidly moving or oscillating ensembles of charges the situation is qualitatively different. The coherently emitted radiation (photons) self-organizes in waves, which then gain independence from the source and propagate with respect to the fundamental centre-of-mass reference-frame of the known universe. Using the hydrodynamic approximation, free radiation ~- consisting of large numbers of coherent photons — can be described mathematically by propagating fields. As a matter of fact there is an important difference between electromagnetically and gravitationally bound systems. In the former one could distinguish between near field, where no net energy-transport occurs and far field region, where the fields become detached from the source system and transport energy, linear and angular momentum. In the latter case the whole known part of the universe seems to represent a gravitational near field region, in which interaction is instantaneous and no net (gravitational) energy and momentum transport occurs.’ I emphasize again that continuous fields are just a convenient mathematical description of a coherent ensemble of propagating photons in the hydrodynamic approximation, each photon transporting a quantum of energy, of linear and of angular momentum. Both light pressure and light-induced angular momentum are experimentally

secured facts.

In conclusion, due to radiation and to the long range gravitational interaction, there are no isolated systems in nature, therefore — rigorously speaking — microcanonical ensembles do not exist. (Even the walls of a closed

system radiate thermally into deep space, provided their temperature is higher

than 0°K.) The beauty of Newtonian dynamics is that it allows treatment by successive approximations, a treatment which fits, perfectly, the observed, ubiquitously hierarchical structure of the Universe. The galaxies and the clusters of galaxies move along straight lines, more inertially than the neutral molecules of a quasi-perfect gas, since the ‘free path’ between galactic collisions is much greater than in gases. As shown by Roscoe (2001), the corresponding mass distribution — supported by astronomical observations ~ is a fractal structure with dimension 2. This model implements at least one aspect of Mach’s principle in that both the metric and the inertial frame are determined by the distribution of

matter. It is a cosmological model for both (matter determined) space and time.

8.5 Straight line and inertial motion

Since the Pope-Osborne Angular Momentum Synthesis (POAMS)' purports to be a.non-linear, ‘circular’ alternative to the Newtonian ‘linear’ dynamics, a short revision of the concept ‘straight line’ is in order. Euclid’s definition reads: ‘A straight line is a line which lies evenly with the points on itself’. Ernst Mach tried

to remove the vagueness of this definition:

Ifa wire of an arbitrary shape can be laid on a board in contact with two upright nails, and slide along so as to be always in contact with the nails, the form and position of the parts of the wire between the nails will be constantly changing. The straighter the wire is, the slighter the alteration will be. A straight line submitted to the same operation slides in itself. Rotated around two of its own fixed points, a crooked wire will keep constantly changing its position, but a straight wire will maintain its position, it will rotate within itself. When we define, now, a straight line as the line which is completely determined by two of its points, there is nothing in this concept except the idealisation of the empirical notion derived from the physical experience mentioned — a notion by no means directly furnished by the physiological act of visualisation. 2

Further:

We can list four classes of physical phenomena that exemplify what we call straight lines: The paths, or rather loci, of taut cords, chains and wires, the path of (a ray of) electromagnetic radiation, the path of inertially moving bodies or rather their centroids, and the paths of gravitating objects (except the Coriolis effect) 3

Riemannian neo-geometry replaced the concept of ‘straight line’ by that of

geodesic, i.e. the shortest path between two points. This, however, is to resort to a

§ Editors’ footnote: The fundamental postulates of POAMS are as introduced in Pope, Chapter 5 in this volume.

mere property rather than to essence. Anyway, there is no way to conceive ‘non~

linearity’ without the concept of ‘linear’.

Henri Poincaré opted in 1903 for Euclidean geometry as the most convenient, as against the non-Euclidean geometries.* He could well have added that because Euclidean geometry is so very convenient, physics at all levels utterly

depends on it and could not conceivably have developed without it.

In the same way that the concept of straight line is an idealisation making geometry possible, inertial straight line motion is an idealisation, extrapolated from experience, which makes kinematics and dynamics possible. The further away a body is from other bodies, the more isolated it is and therefore the more inertial its motion. Even if, strictly, there are no isolated bodies in the Universe {nor any plane surfaces), the concept of inertial motion in straight line is a very useful idealisation. Newton’s first principle, the definition of the inertial frame,

ties together the three concepts, of straight line, uniform time and uniform motion.

8.6 Linear momentum and angular momentum

Linear momentum, the first dynamical quantity, is defined as: p = mv , where v denotes the velocity with regard to an inertial frame of reference (IFR). The definition of angular momentum: L, =r, x p (sather than ‘mvr’ , valid only in circular motion) always implies the specification of a pole with position vector ro in an IFR.”*

For a force-free particle v = constant, therefore the conservation of linear momentum automatically implies the conservation of angular momentum, too. This is, however, a non-physical scenario, since the simplest mechanical system is the two-body system. Only in this situation (ie. one free particle) is linear momentum the /arge distance — r-limit — of angular momentum. In all other cases linear and angular momenta are physically distinct magnitudes. Most important is

the following statement:

* Editors’ footnote: See also the following chapter by Osborne.

For every mechanical system there is a highly preferred, unique,

fundamental reference frame, called centre of mass (CM) frame of

reference, relative to which the angular momenta of the system’s

components have to be defined. The concept of CM is indissolubly tied to

the concept of force and to Newton's third principle, which is fundamental

to all physics.

8.7 The inverse square law, instantaneous gravitational force

Newton’s insight that there is a universal attraction between all bodies in the Universe, which amounts to saying that in principle there are no free bodies, is in full accord with the principle of Mach. Since Newton’s inverse square law depends on the instantaneous distance between any two bodies, one can pair-wise subtract every equation of motion of the masses belonging to a system and — provided there is a unique, global time parameter and the masses are independent on position and velocity — obtain dynamical equations depending on relative quantities only. This means that, in spite of Mach’s harsh criticism, Newtonian dynamics is relativistic in the Mach’s sense, i.e. truly relativistic. Machian dynamics, contrary to Special Relativity (SR) — and to POAMS - can well define a centre of mass and refer every motion within a system to the CM of that system. The separate conservation of both angular momentum and total energy then allow us to investigate the dynamics of the system under investigation. For both the Newtonian and the Machian dynamics, equations of motion, supplemented by initial conditions, are fundamental, enabling us to calculate the paths of the bodies in the system with regard to the CM. For low velocities the Newtonian/Machian dynamics works excellently within the solar system. The study of exotic physical systems, on the scale of super massive bodies, quasars, super novas, neutron stars and the universe as a whole, may require a gravitational theory beyond Newton. However, despite the great interest in gravitation after Newton, there is not a single known case in which direct experimental or observational evidence conclusively and unequivocally shows that Newton’s inverse square law for

gravitational attraction fails.° The solar system, being a many body system,

presents a mathematical problem far too difficult to solve analytically. This is the main reason why the (in)famous precession of Mercury’s perihelion cannot be

considered as a limitation of the 1/7 law and a test for GR.

For accurate calculations, the orbit corrections due to self-rotation (spin) can be estimated using the energy and angular momentum prime integrals and the equation of motion for the CM of each body. As far as mechanics is considered, these ideas are in full accord with those of Peter Graneau, Neal Graneau, David

Roscoe, Peter Rowlands and André Assis.

8.8 Non-instantaneous, retarded electromagnetic radiation

Despite the formal analogy between Newton’s and Coulomb’s timeless, inverse square force laws, gravitation and electromagnetism are very different. As pointed out by Tom Van Flandern, gravitational interaction is transmitted with at least 10'° ¢ , where c = 3 x10° m/s, which hints at instantaneity. Although Newton assumed instantaneous universal attraction, people like Gauss, Tisserand, Gerber, Poincaré, Einstein and others, entertained the idea of gravitational action being transmitted with the velocity c well known from studies in electromagnetism. Although Weber, in whose work ‘c’ first appears, as velocity depending on force, never suggested, in print, that gravitational action is transmitted with that velocity. The gravitational force could be velocity and acceleration-dependant, without the gravitational action being transmitted with velocity c. These days, instantaneous transmission of ‘signals’ is considered prohibited, since it would contradict the second postulate of SR. Actually it is not the Lorentzian kinematics — the backbone of SR ~ which prohibits ‘superluminal’ energy transmission but, rather, Newtonian dynamics generalized for mass increasing with absolute velocity referred to the CM of the relevant system (e.g., that of the Earth, Sun and the Milky Way), provided that energy posses mass/inertia. Indeed, within an IFR the equality dE = c*dm = v.dp entails m = mo(1 — v/c?)°%, thus forbidding v = c on grounds purely of energy considerations. Peter and Neal Graneau attribute inertia to ordinary matter only, not to propagating radiation, therefore invoking the

oxymoron ‘instantaneous delay’. They reject the idea of electromagnetic waves

conveying energy and momentum, whether by means of discrete photons, or by some continuous mechanism. Although this seems to be in accord with the view professed by some painters that ‘light is not seen but it is the illuminated objects which are visible’, the experimental evidence for electromagnetic waves as transporting energy (Lebedev), linear momentum (Maxwell, Bartolli, Poincaré)

and angular momentum (Beth) > remains with us.

Viv Pope’s argument for ‘instantaneous delays’ relies upon three

assumptions:

A. There are neither ‘photons’, nor ‘light’: Nothing propagates;

B. Since according to Bondi, Milne, Burniston-Brown, Sachs, et al., distance and time are equivalent up to a conversion factor (mysteriously) equal to 3 10° m/s, this necessarily introduces a ‘delayed action’ between source and detector at a distance. (Eddington, the SR-missionary, used an even more amazing language: ‘Some man’s space is other man’s time.’),

C. He (Pope) introduces the ‘proper time’ of SR via the ‘Pythagorean formula’:

$$t_P^2 = t_R^2 - s^2/c^2 = t_R^2(1 - v^2/c^2);\quad s = vt_R \tag{7}$$

in addition to the observer time ép.

This is an eminently practical method to argue. For the instantaneousness of electromagnetic ‘action’ one uses the non-measurable ‘proper time’, while for the delay in action at a distance one employs the usual ‘observer time. In my opinion

delay is due to the inertia of both matter and energy.

Now, energy can be conveyed at a distance either by particles or by ‘waves’ consisting of large numbers of particles. One recalls that it was Einstein who introduced the particles involved in the transmission of electromagnetic energy, later christened ‘photons’ by Lewis. Einstein, who was awarded the Nobel prize for using explicitly the photon concept in explaining the photoelectric effect,

was a ‘Zeno of the twentieth century’, a champion creator of paradox.

Einstein came to SR, in 1905, through Maxwell’s continuous field theory. Then in that same year he published his work on the photoelectric effect relying

upon discrete photons. In the twenties he wrote to Pauli that for the rest of his life he would wonder what light is. To my knowledge he never reached a satisfactory solution, but I doubt that as a physicist he could ever have concluded that ‘light is nothing’, or that ‘there is no light’! Einstein’s confused state of mind served as inspiration for the young Duke de Broglie in formulating the (in)famous particle- wave duality, in which every particle moving with velocity v supposedly has an associated wave accompanying it with a velocity c’/v. This has been established as a central dogma of the ‘Copenhagen quantum mechanics’, till some original thinkers realized that every wave consists of large ensembles of coherent

particles, thus dispersing the fog of ‘particle-wave dualism’.

8.9 Mach’s principle, geometry and time

Most participants to the first Swansea Workshop seem to agree about the meaning of Mach’s principle, i.e.: a) The local inertia of every body is determined by the global distribution of matter:

b) The fundamental laws of physics -if any — depend on relative distances between masses and their time derivatives only;

c) As implied by b), there is a unique, global time (parameter) for every physical system;

d) In spite of b), Mach’s principle combined with Newton’s dynamics reintroduces the unique, global, privileged, fundamental frame of reference determined by the centre of mass of the system under study, from atom to galaxy clusters, relative to which velocities, linear and angular momenta has to be defined and relative to which the approximate, local conservation principles hold.

Not all participants agree, however, that inertial mass increases with velocity. However, if it does, then:

e) Mach’s principle cannot hold rigorously and

f} An absolute effect can be induced by absolute velocities only, i.e. velocities with regard to the centre of mass of the system under study.

Most of the participants did not express their opinion concerning a possible connection between the principle of Mach and the geometry of space. Pope and Osborne believe that there is such a connection. Bertrand Russell wrote: ‘To make Geometry dependent for its possibility on the laws of motion is a gross

hysteron proteron. To make Geometry await the perfection of Physics is to make

1 Editors’ footnote: see Proposition 9 in ‘Towards a Consensus’ (Chapter 4).

Physics, which depends throughout on Geometry, forever impossible’.’ As for Mach himself, let me put it in his words: ‘Physicists have as yet found no reason for departing from the assumption that & = 0 (where & is the ‘curvature of space’) of the Euclidean geometry.” About the N-dimensional geometries entertained by Clifford, Klein, Lie and others, Mach wrote:

Seldom have thinkers become so absorbed in reverie, or so far estranged from reality, as to imagine for our space a number of dimensions exceeding the three of the given space of sense, or to conceive of representing that space by any geometry that departs appreciably from the Euclidean. Gauss, Lobachevsky, Bolyai and Riemann were perfectly clear on this point, and cannot certainly be held responsible for the grotesque fictions which were subsequently constructed in this domain.*

Chapter 9: The Pope-Osborne Angular Momentum Synthesis

Anthony D. Osborne

This chapter gives an account of an angular momentum synthesis, based on Pope’s Normal Realist philosophy. It is postulated that all motion is naturally orbital, and that angular momentum is holistically conserved. In particular, an isolated two-body system is considered, and it is shown how conservation of angular momentum is sufficient in itself to explain orbital motion in such a system, without the need for assuming the existence of an in vacuo ‘gravitational’ force of attraction. This approach naturally incorporates any effects due to spin and leads us to predict certain measurable physical effects associated with spinning bodies. It is shown how this approach, in incorporating effects due to spin, is able to deduce the parameters of the hydrogen atom from considerations of angular momentum alone, without the need for introducing an in vacuo ‘electrostatic’ force. Accordingly, it is suggested that the postulated in vacuo ‘forces’ of static interaction held responsible for the ‘orbital’ motion of ‘particles’ on the micro- as well as the macro-physical levels may usefully be replaced by a common factor, angular momentum. The chapter concludes with a discussion of the advantages and drawbacks of this angular momentum synthesis at the present

time.

9.1 Introduction

As participants in the Swansea Workshops, Viv Pope and I are believers in unmediated instantaneous action-at-a-distance. However, as explained under Proposition 2 in Chapter 4: Towards a Consensus, interrelations between masses, which are traditionally called ‘gravitational’, should be interpreted as correlations, since an action-at-a-distance implies some mystical kind of instantaneous force having to pass between the masses involved. One of the aims of the Pope-Osborne Angular Momentum Synthesis (POAMS), first introduced in

Chapter 5 of this volume, is to provide an explanation of unmediated

instantaneous correlation-at-a-distance (UICAAD) in terms of holistic conservation of angular momentum. The arguments for and against taking holistic angular momentum conservation specifically as the instantaneously correlating principle in action-at-a-distance are well rehearsed in Towards a Consensus. Incidentally, it was Jon Blay, the chairman of the first Swansea Workshop, who

gave our angular momentum synthesis its current name and the acronym POAMS.

Although this chapter concentrates on particular aspects of POAMS concerning orbital motion, it is essential to have an appreciation of its underlying philosophy. POAMS represents a new approach to physical science, which seeks to avoid fallacies in a traditional approach to the subject, and to re-establish it on the more positivistic lines introduced by Ernst Mach. Mach’s programme for physics was to base it on what we actually observe, as opposed to theoretical speculation. Moreover, for Mach, there was also the criterion of conceptual efficiency and economy. Pope’s underlying realist philosophy of POAMS, which has been termed ‘Normal Realism’, is an updated approach to physics of the observer-centred sort pioneered by Mach. However, whereas Mach's philosophy emphasized the dependence of science on ‘sense-data’, Normal Realism emphasizes the part played by language in the organizing of all sense experience. In other words, this philosophy seeks to describe nature using ‘ordinary language’, a term which in this context, includes the all-embracing ‘language of nature’, now called ‘information’. Thus, Normal Realism firmly rejects the extreme positivistic view that only what is directly perceived at an instant can be real. Normal Realism is then essentially the realism that remains when all the traditional ‘God’s-eye-view’ presumptions of classical physics have been removed. Its origins lie in radical developments in linguistic analysis, following the later Wittgenstein, which went largely unnoticed by physicists.

In Normal Realism then, we refer to ‘objects’, our ‘perceptions’ or ‘ideas’ of those objects, the ‘spaces’ between them and even ‘vacua’ all in a straightforward, logically evolving ordinary language sense. Also, since

perception is not self-supporting, we may sensibly refer to a residual class, or

substratum, of unperceived and perhaps unknown things. A fuller discussion of Normal Realism is given by Pope in A Tale of Two Paradigms.

It follows that in POAMS, as in Mach’s approach, there is no space other than that defined by the distances between bodies, and there is no time other than what is measured in motion and other physical changes (see Propositions 11 and 12 in Towards a Consensus). Also in POAMS, in accordance with Mach’s principle of universal connectivity of masses and their motions (see Proposition 9 in Towards a Consensus), the substance of the ‘universe’ does not consist of bits of pure matter or mass floating about, independently of one another, in a self- sufficient void. Rather, the substance of the ‘universe’ is seen as an inseparable

combination of mass, length and time.

It must be emphasised from the outset that POAMS is a synthesis of apparently disparate physical theories. It is not a new physical theory intended to wipe out and replace Newtonian dynamics,! Special and General Relativity (SR and GR) or Quantum Mechanics, for example. Indeed, POAMS ultimately seeks to modify these theories to an extent that is no more than necessary to remove the logical contradictions between them. It retains some of the mathematical elements of existing theories, whilst dispensing with other elements and, in some cases, giving a very different interpretation based on a fundamentally different philosophy.

9.2 Holistic Conservation of Angular Momentum

A fundamental question to which POAMS seeks an answer is what mechanism causes “freely moving’ bodies in the ‘universe’, such as stars and the planets, to move in the way that they do, such that a holistic balance is maintained. In Newton’s theory, all inertial, or force-free motion is naturally rectilinear, so that orbital motion, precisely the motion which is generally observed, is unnatural, caused by an invisible ‘gravitational force’ acting in vacuo. What are traditionally called Newton’s three laws, together with his inverse square law for gravitational attraction, provides an equation of motion for orbiting bodies. In this way,

Newton’s theory deduces Kepler’s laws for planetary motion. However, his

mechanistic philosophy prevented him from seriously contemplating any holistic or non-local approach to ‘gravitational’ interaction. In GR, orbiting bodies move in the way that they do, not because of the existence of in vacuo forces, but because they follow the geodesics, i.e. the curves of ‘optimal length’ in the underlying space-time manifold. In other words, the mechanism for orbital motion is the curvature of the space-time manifold. It must be remembered here however, that this manifold is a convenient mathematical structure, and is not intended to be a model of physical reality. A large number of space-time manifolds that are used in practice provide local models, such as the Schwarzschild space-time, which is a mathematical model that predicts the motion of the planets in the solar system. However, a space-time manifold which gives a global mathematical model, which predicts properties of the ‘universe’, can be contemplated in GR, and this represents a type of holism. But there is certainly no instantaneous unmediated action-at-a-distance in GR, which is bound by SR, in which all physical interaction and information are delayed due to the universal ‘speed limit’ of c.

In contrast to both Newtonian theory and GR, POAMS begins with the holistic balance of the ‘universe’, rather than contemplating the motion of individual bodies first. Such a balance can only be maintained if each body in the system is instantly correlated with each and every other, with no question of invisible ‘gravitational forces’ being involved. With its underlying Normal Realist philosophy, POAMS accepts, as it stands, the empirical and observational © evidences of UICAAD as simply revealing the natural tendency of objects to move and distribute (balance) themselves in instant conformity with the various holistic conservation laws. In this way, Pope and I concur with Phipps, Graneau and Assis,”"* that there is an urgent need to rethink our ideas of space, time and motion along more holistic lines proposed by Mach. Indeed, in his book, The

Science of Mechanics, Mach states,

...every single body of the universe stands in some definite relation with every other body in the universe; ...any one body, and consequently also any several bodies, cannot be regarded as wholly isolated. ... We cannot regard it as impossible that integral laws, to use an expression of C. Neumann, will some day take the place of the laws of mathematical elements or differential laws, that now make up the science of mechanics, and that we shall

have direct knowledge of the dependence on one another of the positions of bodies. In such an event, the concept of force will have become superfluous.

In particular, POAMS postulates that all ‘force-free’ motion (used here in the same sense as in GR) is naturally orbital, with no need to postulate any in vacuo ‘gravitational forces’ being responsible for these curvilinear motions. In other words, for classical inertial or ‘force-free’ motion, all bodies seek to move, if allowed, in orbits with respect to one another, trajectories of the sort that we observe in astronomical space. It must be remembered that in vacuo forces are not observed in practice. Newtonian theory construed what we label ‘in vacuo forces’ satisfactorily to explain observed trajectories in space and so implied that these actually exist. POAMS provides an explanation for what these apparent in vacuo forces actually are, simply by switching from a rectilinear to a curvilinear force- free motion assumption. This approach also removes any necessity of having to contemplate the existence of any mediatory devices for the transport of these

apparent ‘forces’, such as the ‘ethers’ and fields of classical imagining.

It should be noted here that POAMS does not imply that vacuum forces do not exist, only that they are superfluous, in the same way as SR does not imply that the luminiferous ether does not exist, only that it becomes redundant. In contrast, ‘real’ forces, i.e., forces which are actually experienced and measurable

in some way, are very necessary in POAMS.

The natural orbit of any particle may therefore be thought of as the path described by the particle when all restrictions on its motion are removed, that is, when the particle moves freely under the influence of nothing but its own angular momentum. In POAMS, it is a consequence of Mach’s principle of universal connectivity of masses and their motions that all bodies in motion in the “universe” at all times follow their natural orbit, except those in artificially constrained orbits such as a body in contact with the surface of a planet, or an

artificial satellite equipped with a booster rocket.

According to POAMS, the holistic balance of masses in the ‘universe’ is

maintained by the holistic conservation of angular momentum, in which every

body is automatically paired and balanced with every other. This holistic approach to gravitation was first proposed in a 1992 Physics Essays paper by Pope and myself ® and then developed and encapsulated in our 1995 Physics Essays paper.’ Our approach takes the force of gravitation out of metaphysical inscrutability and places it where it belongs, in actual physical reality, as the felt and measurable force required to prevent bodies from travelling in their otherwise naturally force-free (inertial) orbits. The POAMS premise is that it is angular momentum which keeps bodies apart, and if angular momentum disappears, then bodies collapse together with no question of ‘vacuum forces’ having anything to do with it. Just how UICAAD occurs in POAMS under holistic conservation of angular momentum is discussed elsewhere in this volume by Pope, in A Tale of Two Paradigms. However, it should be noted that according to the Normal Realist philosophy, it is as useless trying to find the ‘mechanical’ reason for how a change in angular momentum in one orbit-system produces a change in angular momentum in some other system somewhere else, as trying to find ‘mechanical’ reasons for the fact that in thermal motion, the energies of the particles distribute themselves inexorably in the direction of maximum entropy. So far as POAMS is concemed, the whole of physics is based on fundamental indeterminacy, governed by purely statistical laws like that of the second law of thermodynamics instead of

classical mechanical causal-determinism.

In keeping with the underlying philosophy of POAMS, the fact that it postulates that all motion is naturally orbital and that angular momentum is holistically conserved is based firmly on observational and empirical evidence. POAMS chooses conservation of angular momentum as the instantaneously correlating principle in action-at-a-distance since angular momentum is clearly the natural measure to use in orbital motion and contains information about the fundamental empirical measures of spatial relations, mass, time and orientation. In Towards a Consensus, it is pointed out that the other likely candidate for the instantaneously correlating principle in action-at-a-distance is conservation of

energy. However, angular momentum provides more information than energy,

being a vector rather than a scalar quantity. Also, conservation of angular momentum does not imply conservation of kinetic energy. For example, in an elliptical natural orbit of constant angular momentum, the kinetic energy of the

orbiting particle varies.

In complete contrast to both Newtonian theory and GR, POAMS makes no distinction between the ‘universe’ on the macro- and micro-levels. Whereas both Newtonian theory and GR have an underlying continuum, in POAMS, the structure of the ‘universe’, at the microphysical level, is ultimately discrete, with angular momentum quantised in discrete units of 4/2x. None of the participants of the Swansea Workshops disagreed with this proposition (see Proposition 4 in Towards a Consensus). As pointed out by Peter Rowlands in that chapter, at this level, conservation of angular momentum implies that the three charges responsible for the electromagnetic, strong and weak interactions are separately conserved, so that angular momentum is essentially a representation of charge. It is indicated {ater in this chapter that extrapolating our angular momentum approach down to the quantum level may lead to the possible redundancy of the traditional field forces not only of Newton, but also of Faraday and Maxwell. Energy may be regarded as a constituent of angular momentum at the quantum level, but not vice-versa and this is another reason why POAMS considers conservation of angular momentum more fundamental than conservation of energy. Other recent work, notably by Kanarev, also emphasises the central role of conservation of angular momentum, both in the effect of spin on macroscopic orbital trajectories, as is also indicated later in this chapter, and its quantisation in

atomic structure.®

Of course, the fact that angular momentum is universally conserved cannot be expected to provide, in itself, the reason why bodies move in the way they do. It is quite clear that any conservation principle does not automatically provide an equation of motion. However, it must be remembered that POAMS is a synthesis of Newtonian dynamics and other theories. Hence, for example, since

observational and empirical evidence tells us that the planets follow nearly

elliptical orbits, a (mathematically) Newtonian approach can be taken in POAMS when studying such orbits, so that the equation of motion is mathematically the

same as the Newtonian equation of motion in this case.

9.3 The POAMS Approach to Orbital Motion

There was general agreement amongst the participants of the Swansea Workshops that all bodies in motion must be considered as part of a paired system. In order to illustrate the basic POAMS approach to orbital motion, for the rest of this chapter, we restrict ourselves to the simplest case of an isolated paired system consisting of two bodies, in which a particle P of mass m orbits a larger body B of mass M, where M » m. Of course, we readily acknowledge that such a pure, isolated system is not observable in practice. For initial simplicity we shall assume that P and B either do not spin or that they spin very slowly, so that there is no or negligible contribution from the spin angular momenta of P and B. In this case, P can be considered as orbiting an origin O, which is the centre of mass of B. This is a special case of a more general situation, in which simply a particle P in orbit about a point O is considered. For the present, we shall not assume that P is freely

moving, i.e., the orbit of P could be constrained in some way.

As in Newtonian dynamics, the orbital angular momentum, L, of the

$$\mathbf{L} = \mathbf{r} \times m\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{r} \times \mathbf{p} \tag{3.1}$$

where r is the position vector of P, v = dr/Adt is the velocity of P and p is the linear momentum of P, all relative to O, at any instant of time ¢. Then the orbital angular momentum of P is a vector that lies perpendicular to the plane containing r and v. Here, f is used in its usual sense of the time parameter, which enters all the dynamical laws of physics, as opposed to time in the sense of observational time, or duration, which is discrete in POAMS (see the views expressed on Proposition 12 in Towards a Consensus). According to POAMS, since this system is not subjected to external effects, the angular momentum of P relative to O is

always constant.

Since the angular momentum of P is constant in time, it follows that dL/dt = 0. Let the magnitude of L be denoted by L, ie., L = |[L]]. Since L is a constant vector, it follows that the orbit of P lies in a plane and Z is a constant. Also, since dL/dt = 0, it follows from (3.1) that

dL/dt = dr/dt x my +r x mdv/dt =v x my +r ma=r x ma=0,

where a = dv/dt is the acceleration of P relative to O. Since m+ 0,r #0 and az 0, (a cannot be 0 since v cannot be a constant in orbital motion), this implies that a

must be parallel to r and so, clearly, the acceleration of P is directed towards O.

Since the orbit of the particle P lies in a plane, it is convenient to describe it using plane polar coordinates r and 8, where r = lirll and @ is the angle between the radial vector r and some fixed radial axis. The angular speed of P is then denoted and defined by

$$\omega = d\theta/dt = \dot{\theta}$$

where a dot denotes differentiation with respect to ¢. Exactly as in Newtonian

theory, it then follows that the orbital speed, v = lvl, of P is given by v=(ry+ Po. (3.2) The magnitude of the acceleration, a = llall, of P is then given by a= |r"-ro’| (3.3) and the magnitude of the orbital angular momentum, L, of P is given by L=mre. (3.4)

The fact that Z is constant implies that r’@ is a constant, i.e. 7°@" is a constant, so that equal areas are swept out in equal times by the orbit of P. In other words, this law due to Kepler does not depend on the existence of a Newtonian inverse square

law.

So far, we have not considered the particular shape of the orbit of P about O. In this chapter we shall consider only the case in which the orbit of P is closed,

although in general, POAMS does not require orbits to be closed (See Chapter 11,

section 2; Anthony D. Osborne: Further developments in the Pope-Osborne angular momentum synthesis.) The simplest closed orbit is a circle with centre O. Of course, circular orbits are readily observed. For example, if P is any fixed point on the surface of a rigid body that is rotated about a fixed point O, then P will have a circular orbit relative to O. In the case of a circular orbit, r is a constant, so that r =r" =0, and it then follows from (3.2) and (3.3) that

$$a = r\omega^2 = v\omega \tag{3.5}$$
$$L = mvr \tag{3.6}$$

in this case, v is a constant and from (3.1), v is perpendicular to r, hence to a.

In general, in order to determine all possible closed orbits of P about O, we require, of course, an equation of motion. Since the acceleration of P is

directed towards O, it must take the form $\mathbf{a} = -h(r)\mathbf{n}$

for some function A(r}, where n is a vector of unit length in the direction of r, i.e., n = rir. (It is possible that # is a function of both r and 6, but then in the special case of a circle, this would give a = hA(r, 0). Since in this case, r and v are constants so that a is a constant by (3.5), we obtain a contradiction.) This provides the general equation of motion

$$\mathbf{a} = d^2\mathbf{r}/dt^2 = -(h(r)/r)\mathbf{r} \tag{3.7}$$

for the orbit of P. Then, noting that 7°@" = L/m, a constant, (3.3), (3.4) and (3.7)

$$a = |\ddot{r} - r\dot{\theta}^2| = |\ddot{r} - L^2/(m^2r^3)| = h(r) \tag{3.8}$$

Knowing h(r), this equation can be solved for r(#) in the usual way,’ and together with 0° = L/(mr’), this gives r = r(@) as the equation of the orbit of P.

As communicated to me by George Galeczki, since we require the orbit of P about O to be closed, it follows by Bertrand’s theorem,’ that either A(r) = a/r*

or A(r) = ar, for some positive constant a. It is interesting to note that in the case

$h(r) = ar$, (3.8) can be integrated using the standard procedure to give

$$1/r^2 = E/L^2 - \sqrt{(E^2 + \alpha L^2)}\sin(2\theta - 2\theta_0)$$

as the equation of the orbit of P, where 0 and £ are constants, with E related to the total energy of the orbit. With appropriate scaling we can consider the equation

$$1/r^2 = K - \sin 2\theta,\quad K > 1$$

It is easily seen that this equation represents a closed curve that is ellipse-like in character in the sense that it is symmetrical about a major and a minor axis, but it does not represent an ellipse. Note that in this case, since A(r) = ar, it follows that || d’x/d? || = ar, so that || ax/a?? || takes its maximum value at the point on the orbit of P furthest from O, which makes no physical sense and does not agree

with empirical evidence.

Hence, bearing in mind POAMS underlying Normal Realist philosophy, and the fact that the only choice left for A(r) that agrees with observational and empirical evidence is h(r) = a/r*, it follows from (3.7) that in POAMS, the

equation of motion for P in a closed orbit about O is rid? =-(a/r)r . (3.9)

This equation gives (3.8), with h(r) = a/r’, which can be solved in exactly the same way as in Newtonian theory '' to show that since the orbit of P about O is closed, it must be an ellipse. In this case, since a is directed towards O, it follows by (3.4) and (3.8) that

L =amlro+ mrr“/o . (3.10) Circular orbits are special solutions of (3.8) for constant r, so that using (3.6), Lam)’ P) = wr? => L = amy. (B.11) This equation also follows by (3.10) and (3.5).

Let us now return to the more specific case of a freely moving particle P, of mass m, orbiting a body B, of mass M. Since we wish to compare (3.9) with the corresponding Newtonian equation of motion in this case, we shall re-label a as

GM, where G is simply a new constant, introduced for the sake of convenience, so

$$d^2\mathbf{r}/dt^2 = -(GM/r^3)\mathbf{r} \tag{3.12}$$

This, of course, is now the same as the Newtonian equation of motion, but it is important to note that this has been derived with no reference to arguments involving forces. In contrast, Newtonian dynamics implies that orbital motion is unnatural, caused by an in vacuo force of ‘gravitational attraction’ so that (3.12) is derived by equating the centrifugal force, ma, on P with the artificially introduced ‘gravitational force’ -(GmM/r’)r, induced on P by B.

According to the POAMS thesis, the true (i.e. measurable) force on the particle P is not concealed in its natural orbit, but rather is revealed in the difference between that natural orbit and an ‘unnatural’ one, that is, some state of motion in which the particle is held by a measurable force, of magnitude F. Specifically, let us consider a closed orbit of P about B which is constrained in some way, but for which angular momentum is conserved. In this case, P’s equation of motion necessarily takes the form (3.9). (Of course, in general, a particle P may be artificially made to follow any ‘unnatural’ orbit about B and so may not even have an equation of motion of the form (3.9).) In general, we may let a = GM in (3.9), where G’ is a constant with the same dimensions as the Newtonian gravitational constant G, but only equal to G in any natural (unconstrained) orbit of P. Then the measurable ‘gravitational’ force on P in any

constrained orbit (in the sense just described) has magnitude, F, given by F=|G-—G’| mM’. (3.13) Otherwise, in a natural, ‘force-free’ orbit, G’= G and so F = 0.

We now demonstrate how this approach works in practice. Let P orbit B in a constrained closed orbit for which the angular momentum is constant. For a particular orbit, the parameters r(f) and w(f) are known (they are observational

measures). Then, since from (3.8),

$$\ddot{r} - r\omega^2 = -G'M/r^2 \tag{3.14}$$

in this case, and since the mass M of body B is known, the constant G’ for this orbit can be calculated. Note that G’ does not depend on the mass m of P. The magnitude F of the measurable force exerted on P in its constrained orbit is then given by (3.13). In the context of the type of constrained orbits we have been considering, the obvious and most useful case to consider is the case in which P is constrained to the surface of a body B, and so orbits the centre-of-mass O, of B, where the mass M of B is considered to be concentrated at O. In this case, P’s constrained orbit is a circle with centre O. It follows from (3.14) and (3.5) that, in this special case

$$r\omega^2 = G'M/r^2 \Rightarrow G' = rv^2/M \tag{3.15}$$

where v is the orbital speed of P in its constrained circular orbit of radius r. By calculating G4 we can determine immediately whether or not P is in a natural

orbit, since P is in such an orbit if and only if G’= G.

In general, if P orbits B in a constrained orbit for which Z is constant, then since r(t) and w(t) are known, ZL may be calculated from (3.4). It is then possible to calculate any natural orbit of P, with the same magnitude of angular momentum L, if the orbit of P were not constrained. Let the particle P, in any natural orbit, with magnitude of angular momentum L, have angular speed ao(t) and radial coordinate ro(é). Then by (3.8) with h(r) = GM/r’,

$$\ddot{r}_0 - L^2/(m^2r_0^3) = -GM/r_0^2 \tag{3.16}$$

Knowing L, G, M and m, (3.16) is a second order differential equation for ro(2), and with a given set of initial conditions can be solved to give ro(é). Then wo(?) may be calculated from (3.4), ie. «o(t) = L/mro?(t). Notice that any natural orbit of P is not unique, since it depends on a given set of initial conditions. However, given a constrained orbit of P with constant magnitude of angular momentum Z, there is a unique circular natural orbit for P, with the same constant magnitude of angular momentum, with parameters ro and vo, where vo is the orbital speed of P. In this case, it follows by (3.11), with a = GM, that (3.16) reduces to
