$$v_0 = GmM/L \tag{3.17}$$ and then, by (3.6), $$r_0 = L/mv_0 \tag{3.18}$$

Note that the parameters vg and ro do not depend on the mass m, since (3.17)

implies that $v_0 = GM/r^2\omega$ and then (3.18) gives $r_0 = r^2\omega/v_0$.

EXAMPLE

Consider a mass of 1 kg, say, placed on a weighing-scale somewhere on the earth's equator. The speed of the mass in this constrained, very nearly circular orbit about the earth’s centre, is the revolutionary speed of the earth, namely v = 464.74 m s". The radius of this orbit is the earth's mean equatorial radius, given by r ~ 6.372828 x 10° m. For this constrained circular orbit, G’is given by (3.15) where M is the mass of the earth. Notice that this is not exactly the usual figure quoted for M@ in Newtonian dynamics since in POAMS, according to (3.13)

M(G-G’\ir’ =g, where g is the usual acceleration due to the earth’s ‘gravitational pull’. Hence, by (3.15), gr =M(G-G)=MG-r’* > M=rrgt+ VG. This gives M = 5.989 x 10” kg and it then follows by (3.15) that G’ = 2.298254 x 10? N m? kg”. Now G = 6.67259 x 107! N m? kg®, so that 1 kg mass is not in any natural orbit

about the earth’s centre. It follows by (3.13), that the magnitude of the real,

measurable force on this mass at the earth's equator is $F = M(G - G')/r^2 \approx 9.81\;\text{N}$.

This is the magnitude of the force with which the 1 kg mass seeks to oppose the intervention of the earth's surface and to take up its natural circular orbit. It is this

force that produces 1 kg weight on the weighing-scale.

Since the motion of the mass in its constrained orbit is circular, the

magnitude ofits orbital angular momentum, L, is given by (3.6), so that L= 2.961708 x 10° kg m? 7.

With this relatively small amount of angular momentum, if we imagine the earth's radius shrunk to the size of a super-dense billiard ball, the natural orbital speed, vo, of that kilogram mass, assuming its natural orbit to be circular around the earth's mass, is given by (3:17), so that vo = 1.3493 x 10° ms". Then the radius, ro, of this natural orbit is given by (3.18), so that ro * 2.195 x 10* m. This is approximately 1/290" of the earth's true radius and it follows that the mass would seek to orbit at 289/290" of the distance from the earth’s surface to its centre. A

second example is provided in reference 12.

9.3.1 The POAMS Analogue of Newton&rsquo;s Second Law

In the case of a particle P orbiting a body of mass M, it follows by (3.12) that since there is no implied in vacuo ‘gravitational’ force that determines a natural orbit of P, then there is no implied centrifugal force, ma, felt by the particle P in such an orbit. In other words, although P has an acceleration a, which is non-zero at each point of its orbit, any natural orbit of P is ‘force free’, so that the vector quantity ma is not a force in the classical sense. This implies that Newton’s second law does not hold in its usual form in POAMS, and commits us to considering an

amended version.

The natural, ‘force free’ orbits of POAMS are analogous to the geodesics of GR. Hence, in POAMS, we require a new definition of acceleration, which is zero on any natural orbit, analogous to GR. The obvious definition to make is to

define the natural acceleration, a, for any closed orbit of the particle P, in our

two-body system by

@ =a+(GM/r)r, (3.19) where a is the classical orbital acceleration of P. It then follows by (3.12) that @ = 0 along any natural orbit of P, and along any other orbit of P, the natural

acceleration is

$$\boldsymbol{\alpha} = ((G - G')M/r^3)\mathbf{r}$$

by (3.9) with a = GM and so will be non-zero. Notice that although there is no way of distinguishing between orbital (Newtonian) acceleration in natural and unnatural orbits, the above definition of natural acceleration does provide a way of distinguishing between the two types of orbit. Notice also that when Mf = 0, a =a=0. In this case, it follows by (3.12) that any natural orbit of P is a straight line and so, in POAMS, rectilinear motion is a special case of orbital motion. Hence, Newton’s first law is included as a special case and conservation of

angular momentum implies conservation of linear momentum.

We may then define the natural or intrinsic force, F, felt by the particle P, by Fama, (3.20)

where a is the natural acceleration of P, and this is the POAMS analogue of Newton’s second law. It then follows that F = 0 on any natural orbit of P and

F # 0 on any other orbit. Notice that along any constrained orbit of P, it follows

by (3.20), (3.19) and (3.9) with a = GM, that | Fl = |@— G[ maar?

so that the magnitude of F ’ is the measurable gravitational force on P in any

constrained orbit, which is given by (3.13).

9.4 Spin in POAMS

Recall that in POAMS, the total angular momentum of any system is holistically balanced, as opposed to that balance being mediated by any time-retarded causal interaction between bodies. In general, the total angular momentum does not just consist of contributions from orbital motion. It will also depend on the effect of other forms of angular momentum, and in particular, that due to spin. Spin angular momentum is central to POAMS since it represents the intrinsic angular momentum of any particle. Of course, any effects due to spin can be taken into

account in Newtonian dynamics. In this approach, Newton’s laws may be applied

to any system containing spinning bodies by treating the spinning bodies as being, themselves, systems of many orbiting particles. However, the POAMS approach is entirely different. In POAMS, any effects of spin are instantaneously correlated with the total angular momentum. POAMS takes account of this by postulating that the orbital kinetic energy of an orbiting spinning particle depends on both its spin kinetic energy and the orbital kinetic energy it would have if it were not spinning. This new approach implies that the ‘gravitational constant’ G, has to be

replaced by a more general function G of the orbital parameters.

Only the case of a “freely moving’ particle P, of mass m, in a closed orbit about a body 8 of mass M will be considered here. If the particle P spins on its axis while orbiting, then according to Newtonian dynamics as applied to rigid bodies, the inverse square law has to be applied to each ‘elementary piece’ of the rotating particle, in dealing with which we are faced with the well-known many- body problem, which is notoriously intractable. It can be shown that if the spinning body is perfectly spherical, in applying Newton's laws it acts like a point mass, so that the spin situation is the same as that of the non-spin. In other words, in Newtonian dynamics, the same orbit is predicted in both cases. In contrast, according to POAMS, the system is holistically balanced, so that since any orbital angular momentum is balanced against the total momentum of the system, introducing a spin at any one place has a direct and immediate effect on the system throughout. In other words, P’s spin will cause it to take up a different natural orbit from that if it were not spinning. A convenient way of investigating

the effect of P’s spin on its orbit is by considering the kinetic energy of P.

Suppose for the moment that P does not spin and follows a natural elliptical orbit about B, with radial coordinate ro(t), and with angular speed @o(Z).

As in Newtonian dynamics, P's orbital kinetic energy, \(K_o\), is given by

$$K_o = mv_0^2/2 \tag{4.1}$$

where vo(t) is the orbital speed of P in its natural orbit. It follows by (3.2) and (3.4) that

$$K_o = m(\dot{r}_0)^2/2 + L\omega_0/2 \Rightarrow L = 2K_o/\omega_0 - m(\dot{r}_0)^2/\omega_0 \tag{4.2}$$

where L is the magnitude of P’s orbital angular momentum.

Now let P spin on its axis while orbiting the body 8. In POAMS, we postulate that the orbital parameters of P’s orbit will be directly affected by P’s spin. Hence, by (4.2), when spinning, P follows a new natural orbit given by

L=2K/o,—m(r;\/o; , (4.3) where now K is P’s orbital kinetic energy, and r,(#} and @,(#} are the parameters of this new natural orbit, under the influence of its spin. POAMS postulates that K

depends on both the orbital kinetic energy, K,, of P of the natural orbit it would follow if it were not spinning and its spin kinetic energy, K;. In other words, P’s

spin kinetic energy is transferred directly to its ‘new’ orbital kinetic energy.

Suppose that the particle P, treated as an isolated spinning mass, has angular speed w about its axis of rotation, and moment of inertia 7. Then, as in

Newtonian dynamics, the spin kinetic energy, $K_s$, of P, is given by $$K_s = I\omega^2/2 \tag{4.4}$$

For example, if P is a perfect sphere of uniform density, then J= 2mr?/5, where m

is the mass of the sphere and r is its radius. It then follows that K,=m(ro)’/5 . (4.5)

The magnitude, S, of the P’s spin angular momentum is given by S = Jw. As a rigid body, P spins about its axis of rotation with a constant maximum radius r

and constant rotational speed v. Then v = ro and it follows by (4.4) that S = 2K,/o = 2K,r/v . : (4.6) (Compare (4.6) with (4.2) applied to a circular orbit.)

Clearly, the maximum possible effect due to spin occurs when P either

spins in the same direction as its orbital motion, so that K, acts with K,, or in the

opposite direction to its orbital motion, in which case K, acts against K,. In the

first case we postulate that K=K,+K; (4.7) and in the second case, we postulate that K= |K,-K,|. (4.8)

We maintain that an intermediate effect takes place when P’s axis of rotation is inclined at some angle to its orbital plane. When P spins in the same direction as its orbital motion, its spin angular momentum vector, S, points in the same direction as its angular momentum vector, L. In this case, the magnitude, [| + S|], of the total angular momentum is Z + S and the same is true of any multiples of L and S. It then follows by (4.2) applied to any natural circular orbit, together with (4.6), that K is given by (4.7). When P spins in the direction opposite to that of its orbital motion, $ points in the opposite direction to L and, in this case, ||L + S|| = | L—S|. It then follows, by (4.2) and (4.6) that K is given

by (4.8). In general, if P’s spin angular momentum vector, S, is inclined at angle 0 to its orbital angular momentum vector, L, then the magnitude of the total angular

momentum is given by

|L+S||?=2? +S? + 2ZSc0s0 . We then postulate, by analogy with (4.7) and (4.8) that in this general case, K? = Kj + K2 + 2K,K;cos0 (4.9) In particular, if P spins in a direction perpendicular to the plane of its orbit, then R=K?2+K?. (4.10) This is the minimum possible effect due to spin.

Starting with a natural spin-less orbit of P about B and then determining K from the addition of spin effects, it follows by (4.3) and (3.4) that

$$L = 2\mathcal{K}mr_s^2/L - m^2(\dot{r}_s)^2r_s^2/L \Rightarrow L^2 = 2\mathcal{K}mr_s^2 - m^2(\dot{r}_s)^2r_s^2 \tag{4.11}$$

Since L can be determined by using (4.2) or (3.4) applied to the spin-less orbit, r,(f) may be found by solving the first order differential equation (4.11). Then ,(t) can be found using (3.4). Hence, at least in theory, the parameters of the new natural orbit of P, taking spin effects into account may be determined. The fact . that P’s orbital parameters are affected by its spin gives rise to the fact that the

equation of motion, (3.16), must be adapted to read 2_ 2 by ~%,05 =-GMIr, , (4.12)

that is, G is replaced by a function G which depends on the orbital parameters r, and @;. The function G reduces to G when and only when (4.12) describes any natural, spin-less orbit of P, so that G > G as K > K,. If r, and @, are known,

then the corresponding ‘gravitational factor’, G, may be calculated from (4.12).

If the orbit of P is circular, this process is considerably simplified. In this case; it follows by the definition of orbital kinetic energy that v,, the orbital speed of P, is given by

$$v_s^2 = 2\mathcal{K}/m \tag{4.13}$$

Then r, may be calculated using (3.6), ie.,

r, = Limv, (4.14) Finally in this case, it follows by (3.5) that (4.12) reduces to

$$\mathcal{G} = r_sv_s^2/M \tag{4.15}$$

In summary then, we acknowledge that Newtonian mechanics provides all the means to deal with the more general situation in which self-rotation (spin) is included in the overall angular momentum account. However, as noted by George

Galeczki, the key difference between Newtonian Mechanics and POAMS is that

in POAMS, the spin angular momentum and the corresponding spin energy are given as initial conditions and the change in the total angular momentum is ascribed to a modified ‘gravitational factor’, which replaces Newton’s “gravitational constant’. POAMS predicts that a change in spin causes a change in

G in order that the holistic balance of the angular momentum of the system is

maintained. A major difference then, between the POAMS account of orbital motion and Newton's ‘gravitational’ account, is that in POAMS, the spins of bodies affect their orbital parameters in a way that cannot be accounted for by

purely ‘gravitational’ mass attraction.

9.4.1 &lsquo;Attraction&rsquo; and &lsquo;repulsion&rsquo; in POAMS

According to POAMS, a particle P orbiting a body B in unconstrained motion will follow a natural orbit about B due to considerations of angular momentum and not due to any unseen ‘gravitational force of attraction’ between P and B. The implication is that, in POAMS, all talk of ‘attraction’ and ‘repulsion’: between freely moving particles or bodies becomes redundant. There can only be degrees of relative ‘attraction’ and ‘repulsion’ in the sense that a particle P; orbiting a body B at a mean radius r; can be considered as ‘more attracted’ to B than a particle P) orbiting B at a mean radius r2, if r; < rz. In this case, P, can be considered as ‘more repulsed’ by B than P;. Similarly, a particle constrained to move by a surface of a planet, say, is not considered as being attracted to the surface of the planet. It is simply that the surface of the planet is preventing the particle from taking up its natural orbit about the centre of mass of the planet,

which has a smaller mean radius than the mean radius of the planet’s surface.

We have seen how, in POAMS, the parameters of a freely moving particle P’s natural orbit about a body B are affected by the spin angular momentum of P and hence the spin kinetic energy of P. It follows that P’s spin affects the relative ‘attraction’ of P towards B, in the above sense. This effect can clearly be seen in the case of natural circular orbits. In this case, it follows by (4.15), (4.14) and (4.13) that .

$$\mathcal{G} = r_s v_s^2/M = Lv_s/mM = (L/mM)(2\mathcal{K}/m)^{1/2} \tag{4.16}$$

Hence, for constant m, M and L, it follows that G « K!?. Hence, G increases as K increases and decreases as K. decreases. This indicates that an increase in the

value of K causes the particle P to be ‘more attracted’ to the body B in the

Newtonian sense. This can be seen explicitly, since from (4.14) and (4.13), r, = Limv, = LiQKm)'” , (4.17)

so that, with constant Z and m, r, x 1/K'”.. It then follows that an increase in K produces a decrease in the radius of P’s natural orbit about B, so that P is ‘more attracted’ to B in the Newtonian sense. Similarly, a decrease in the value of K produces an increase in the radius of P’s natural orbit, so that P is ‘less attracted’

to B. Notice that, from (4.13), v, « K', so that P’s orbital speed increases as K

increases and decreases as K. decreases.

For natural elliptical orbits in general, there is no simple relationship between the orbital parameters and K. In particular, r, is not directly proportional to 1/K!”. It must be remembered that on such an elliptical orbit, K, r; and v; will not even be constant. In this more general case, (4.11) gives

r(t) = Li2mK(t) — mrs)" . (4.18) It follows that if | rs. | = 0, ie. r, changes slowly around P’s orbit, then 7, o 1/ Ki? as in the case of a circular orbit. If this is not the case, then although clearly, a change in K(#) produces a change in 7,(f), there may be no direct relationship between these changes. In particular, an increase in K(t) at each point on P’s orbit does not necessarily produce a decrease in the mean radius of this orbit.

Consider now the simplest case of a non-spinning particle P in its natural circular orbit about a body B. POAMS postulates that the natural circular orbit of

that same particle, when spinning, will have a different radius. Recall that if P

spins in the same direction of its orbital motion, then POAMS postulates that its

orbital kinetic energy K is given by (4.7), so that K > K,. It follows by the above

argument that in this case, that the presence of P’s spin kinetic energy produces a decrease in the radius of P’s orbit about B, so that P is ‘more attracted’ to B. Similarly, if P spins in the opposite direction to its orbital motion, then we postulate that K is given by (4.8), so that K < K,. Then the presence of P’s spin

kinetic energy produces an increase in the radius of P’s orbit, so that P is ‘less attracted’ to B. In general, it follows by (4.9) that if P’s spin angular momentum vector points in a direction which is less than or equal to a right angle to its orbital angular momentum vector, then P is ‘more attracted’ to B than if it were not spinning. If P’s spin angular momentum vector points in a direction which is greater than a right angle to its orbital angular momentum vector, then P is ‘less

attracted’ to B than if it were not spinning.

In summary, then, in POAMS, spin affects the relative ‘attraction’ of a particle P to a body B in a way that it cannot according to Newton's thesis, where the addition of spin angular momentum does not change the sign of the uniformly

‘attractive’ ‘gravitational force’ F.

EXAMPLE 1

Consider a spherical steel ball of mass m = 2.52 kg and of radius 4.25 cm. (We are indebted to IEC in Poole, Dorset, who have told us that they can supply such a ball with these physical dimensions.) We shall assume that the ball is situated somewhere on the earth’s equator, so that its orbit around the earth’s centre of mass is approximately circular. As in the example in section 3, the orbital speed of this ball in its constrained orbit is the revolutionary speed of the earth, namely v = 464.74 m s". The radius of this orbit is the earth’s mean equatorial radius, given by r = 6.372828 x 10° m. It follows by (3.6) that the magnitude of its orbital angular momentum is

$$L \approx 7.463504 \times 10^9\;\text{kg m}^2\;\text{s}^{-1}$$

This ball, as an inert mass, would, if allowed to do so, follow its natural circular

orbit with a radius ro, at orbital speed vo. It follows by (3.17), where M is the

earth’s mass as in the example in section 3, that

vo = 1.349294 x 10° ms". Then by (3.18), ro = 2.195006 x 10* m.

Recall that that these parameters are independent of the mass m. It follows by

(3.15), exactly as in the example in section 3, that for the mass’s constrained orbit, G’= 2.298254 x 1073 N m? kg”.

The orbital kinetic energy, K,, of this mass, if allowed to follow its natural circular orbit is given by (4.1), so that

$$K_s \approx 2.293948 \times 10^{-10}\;\text{kg m}^2\;\text{s}^{-2}$$

Now let the spherical steel ball spin at 2000 revolutions per second, in the same direction and in the same plane as its constrained orbit around the earth’s centre of mass. (We are indebted to Colin Evans of the Department of Physics, University of Wales, Swansea, who has told us that it is physically possible to spin such a bali at such a speed without distortion.) The spin kinetic energy, K,, of the bail is given by (4.5) and so in this case

K, = 2.52 x (0.0425 * 2m x 2000)/5 = 1.437567 x 10° kg m? s”.

It then follows by (4.7) and (4.13) that the ball’s orbital speed, v,, in its natural

circular orbit, if allowed to follow that orbit, would be given by Vs = (2K/m)"? = (2(Ko + Kim)!” = 1.349298 x 10° ms", and the radius, r, of that orbit would be given by (4.14), so that r, = Limv, = 2.194999 x 10* m,

Since 7; < ro, it follows that the spinning ball would naturally orbit at a smaller distance from the earth’s centre of mass than if it were not spinning, so that the spinning ball is more ‘attracted’ to the earth’s surface than if it were not spinning.

In other words, the spinning ball should weigh more. This can be immediately

seen, since by (4.15), (3.6) and (3.17), the ‘gravitational factor’, G, for the ball’s

natural circular orbit when spinning is given by G=rv2/M = Lv/(mM) = (GmMv,)/(vomM) = v,G/ - (4.19)

Hence, in this case,

G = 1.000003G. Just how much more the spherical steel ball weighs when it is spinning, in the sense described above, can be calculated by using the technique of the example in

section 3.

The weight of the ball, when not spinning, is given by the weight

equivalent of the force with magnitude F, where F=mM(G-G’)/P = 2.52 x 9.805865 N = 2.52 kg.

On the other hand, the weight of the ball, when spinning in the sense described above, is given by the weight equivalent of the force with magnitude F% where now F’= mM(G— G’)/r° = mM(1.000003G — G’)/r? = 2.52 x 9.805896 N => F’= 1.000003 x 2.52 = 2.520008 kg.

In other words, when spinning, the weight of the ball is increased by

approximately 8/1000" of a gram.

Now suppose that the steel ball spins at 2000 revolutions per second but now in the opposite direction to its constrained orbit about the earth’s centre of mass, and in the same plane. The spinning ball will have the same spin kinetic energy, K; , as above, but now, according to (4.8) and (4.13), its orbital speed in its natural orbit would be

Vs = (2(Ko ~ K;)/m)'? = 1.3492895 x 10°ms7, if the ball was allowed to follow that orbit. The radius of that natural orbit would

be, by (4.14), rs 2.195013 x 10* m,

so that r, > ro, which means that the ball is less ‘attracted’ to the earth than if it were not spinning. From (4.19), the ‘gravitational factor’ for the ball’s natural orbit is now

G = 0.999997G and the spinning ball has a weight equivalent of the force with magnitude

F’= mM(0.999997G — G’)/r? = 2.52 x 9.805834 N => F’= 0.999997 x 2.52 = 2.519992 kg

so that, when spinning, the ball’s weight is decreased by approximately 8/1000" of a gram. The same changes occur, of course, to the ‘acceleration due to gravity’ of a falling spinning ball. It follows from the calculations above that if the spherical steel ball is not spun and is dropped towards the surface of the earth, then its acceleration is about 9.805865 m s*, whereas if it is spun in the same direction and in the same plane as the earth’s orbit and is then dropped, its acceleration is increased by a factor of about 1.000003. Similarly, if the steel ball is spun in the opposite direction and in the same plane as the earth’s orbit and is then dropped, its acceleration is decreased by a factor of approximately 0.999997. It is to be noted that these are tiny effects and, according to our thesis, they are the

maximum possible effects for the specified steel ball.

Although, to the best of our knowledge, experiments involving a spinning ball, as described above have not yet been performed, Hayasaka and his co- workers have performed dropping experiments involving a spinning disc.'*"* In these experiments, a disc of weight 175 gm was used and was spun at 18000

revolutions per minute. The moment of inertia of such a spinning disc is I = 9.6947 x 10° kg m?.

Going through the above calculations shows that if the disc is spun in the same direction and in the same plane as its orbital motion, then its acceleration should increase by a factor of approximately 1.00000005.!” Similarly, if the disc is spun

in the opposite direction and in the same plane as its orbital motion, then its

acceleration should decrease by a factor of approximately 0.99999996. In the Hayasaka experiments, a difference in acceleration was detected when the disc was spun in a particular direction, but not in the opposite direction. Also, the difference in acceleration was very much larger than we predict. The experiments were not performed at the equator and, according to our thesis, the disc was not spun as to produce maximum and minimum effects, so that it is difficult to compare the results of these experiments with our predictions. It is encouraging to note however, that the experiments indicate that there is a change in weight of an object if it is spun. The fact that our theory predicts that the effects detected in these experiments should have been very much smaller than those actually reported accords with the findings of Quinn and Picard of the International Bureau of Weights and Measures,'> who have claimed that the Hayasaka results

are spurious, although Hayasaka has taken issue with this view.'*

Of course, we are far from alone in our predictions of an effect on weight due to spin. Peter Kummel also has predicted changes in weight in the form of gravitational acceleration for spinning bodies. In his theory, spins imparted to freely falling bodies produce anomalous gravitational effects. Phillip Kanarev has argued that conservation of angular momentum changes the ‘gravitational force’ for rotating gyroscopes and hence their free-fall acceleration. GR also predicts

that spinning bodies will not fall with the same acceleration as non-spinning ones.

To date, experiments carried out to detect any possible effects of spin on earth-gravitating masses have made no distinction between the vector-orientations of those spins with respect to the earth’s rotation. For example, Kummel’s experiments involve the Fall Tower in Bremen, where the vertical is inclined at approximately fifty-three degrees north of the earth’s equator, while the Japanese experiments were carried out at Sendai, whose latitude is about thirty-eight degrees north of the equator. So far as POAMS is concerned, this makes all claims to have detected such spin effects entirely equivocal, so that it is scarcely surprising that attempts to replicate the experiments at different places have

yielded different results.

EXAMPLE 2

Consider now the case of a satellite in a natural (unconstrained) circular orbit around the earth at a distance of 400 miles above the earth’s surface say. Let us suppose that the mass of the satellite is m ~ 7.52916642 x 10? kg. This mass just happens to be the mass of a quartz rotor, which orbits the earth at a distance of 400 miles, in the proposed Gravity Probe B experiment, run by a team of scientists at Stanford University.'® However, of course, the following technique applies to any particle in a natural circular orbit about any body. In this case, the satellite orbits at a distance

19 = 7.016548 x 10° m

from the earth’s centre. Since it follows a natural orbit, the orbital speed, vo, of the

satellite is given by (3.15) with G’= G, so that vo’ = GMIro, where M is the earth’s mass. Using the figures in the previous example gives Vo * 7.546796 x 10° ms". It follows by (3.6) that the magnitude of the satellite’s orbital angular momentum is L = 3.986879 x 10° kgm’ s", and by (4.1), the satellite’s orbital kinetic energy is

K, * 2.144086 x 10° kg m’ s*.

Suppose now that the rotor, treated as a spherical ball, spins while orbiting the earth. Its spin kinetic energy is then given by (4.5). As in the proposed Gravity Probe B experiment, let us suppose that the diameter of the rotor is 1.5 inches and

that it spins at 10,000 revolutions per minute. Then K, = m(0.01905 x 2m x 10000 + 60)°/5 = 5.992721 kg m? s*.

It follows that if the rotor spins in the same direction and in the same plane as its

orbit, then by (4.7) and (4.13), the rotor’s orbital speed, v;, in its unconstrained

orbit, with spin effects present, is given by Vs = (2(Ko + Ks/m)"” = 7.546807 x 10° ms"

and so, the radius, 7s, of the rotor’s unconstrained orbit, while spinning is given by (4.14), é.e., rs = Limv, ¥ 7.016538 x 10° m.

In this case, ro ~ r; ~ 10 m, so that POAMS predicts that the rotor will orbit 10 metres closer to the earth than if it were not spinning. Similarly, if the rotor spins in the opposite direction and in the same plane as its orbit, then it follows by (4.8), (4.13) and (4.14) that 7, ~ 79 = 10 m, so that POAMS predicts that the rotor will

orbit 10 metres further away to the earth than if it were not spinning.

In the actual proposed Gravity Probe B experiment, the satellite is equipped with four gyroscope rotors, each of which spins on an axis which permanently points at a guide star on the earth’s equatorial plane, while the satellite orbits the earth from pole to pole. Hence, unfortunately, the spin angular momentum vector of each rotor will be at right angles to the satellite’s orbital angular momentum vector and there will be only a minimal effect of each rotor’s spin on its orbit. In this case, it follows by (4.10) that for each rotor, K- K, = 8.4 x 10 kg m? s*, Going through the calculations using (4.13) and (4.14) gives ro ~ r; = 0.014 mm, so that the effect of spin will be negligible, Another problem with the proposed experiment is that the rotors are permanently spinning, so that it is not possible to detect a difference to the orbit in the spin and no spin cases. In addition, there may well be a precession effect due to the spin orientations, which may effect any predictions that POAMS makes. Finally, the Gravity Probe B experiment sets out to detect very different effects from those predicted by POAMS and, in particular, ‘frame-dragging’ predicted by GR. Hence, on a purely practical level, I believe that terrestrial experiments such as the one proposed in the example in section 9.4.1, hold the most promise for verifying

or disproving the predictions made by POAMS.

Note that there may be inaccuracies in the generally accepted values we

have assumed in these examples. For instance, the accepted value of the Newtonian ‘gravitational constant’, G, was determined accurately by terrestrial experiments, and as such, may need a minor correction in POAMS. For this reason, the predictions in these examples should be interpreted as rather more

qualitative than quantitative.

9.5 POAMS at the quantum level

The fundamental departure of POAMS from Newtonian dynamics as far as the treatment of the effects of spin is concerned may, at first sight, seem unnecessarily radical. Indeed, for normal situations involving macroscopic bodies, spin effects are very small since the total spin angular momentum is very much smaller than the total orbital angular momentum. For example, when studying planetary motion in the solar system, it is not necessary to take the spin of the planets into account. However, the conceptual change dictated by our approach to spin is fundamental to our attempt at unification of the classically conceived ‘forces’ on the macro- and micro-physical levels. For it must be remembered that according to the holistic approach of POAMS, conservation of angular momentum applies not only on the macro-scale but also on the micro-scale. In POAMS, the structure of the ‘universe’, at the micro-physical level, is ultimately discrete, with angular momentum quantised in discrete units of A/2x. We shall demonstrate that in contrast to the macro-level, on the micro-level the intrinsic angular momentum (commonly called ‘spin’) predominates over the orbital angular momentum. This accords well with the fact that those elementary particles conventionally called ‘electrically charged’ are much more volatile in terms of the strengths of the classically conceived forces they exert on one another compared to those of ‘gravity’. Indeed, so spectacularly different are the strengths of these forces that they have been imputed to ‘powers’ that differ not only quantitatively but also qualitatively from that of ‘gravity’. This, we maintain, is why these powers have been allocated special units such as, for instance, ‘coulombs’ for ‘electric charge’. All these other forces, however, are conceived by analogy with ‘gravity’ by the

introduction of an inverse square law, such as Coulomb's law of electrostatics.

However, essentially, no attempt has ever been made to explain what these esoteric ‘powers’ are. They are defined by nothing more than their ability to influence the motions of objects and are therefore ultimately cashable in mechanical units of joules. Although we consider, in particular, electrostatics in this chapter and demonstrate, as agreed by Peter Rowlands, that angular momentum at the micro-level is essentially a representation of charge, the ultimate thesis of POAMS is that these different-strength forces can be unified by expressing them all dynamically, in the same basic units of kilograms, metres and seconds.

The following example demonstrates that the parameters for the hydrogen atom can be derived purely from considerations of angular momentum alone, without the classical assumption of the existence of an electrostatic force. The hydrogen atom constitutes the simplest atomic system and, as such, it has played a crucial role in the history of modern physics as a testing ground for atomic theories. As is well known, Bohr's model of the hydrogen atom supplied an explanation of the Balmer formula for the spectral lines of hydrogen on the basis of the electrodynamical theories of Faraday and Maxwell, with the ad-hoc addition of the Planck relation to provide the essential discreteness of the possible associated energies.'? In this electrodynamical approach, the behaviour of an electron in relation to a proton is explained by ascribing to each particle a purely static property called ‘charge’, of equal magnitude but of opposite sign. The resulting ‘attractive force’ between the two particles, by Coulomb's law, countered by ‘centrifugal force’, is thus thought to determine the orbit of the one around the other by analogy with Newton's account of planetary orbits. In contrast, in POAMS the need to imagine such a counterbalance between in vacuo forces becomes redundant. POAMS conceives the hydrogen atom as an angular momentum system of automatically paired and balanced masses, equivalent to the conventional ‘electron’ and ‘proton’. However, it must be stressed here that we are not attempting to resurrect the Bohr model of the hydrogen atom. The

essential point is that our model is consistent with the underlying philosophy of

POAMS. It is not simply a mathematically convenient description of ‘the atom’ as in quantum mechanics. The fact that our model is not intended to provide a true physical description of the hydrogen atom does not prevent us from associating vectors with the quantities involved, in the same way that vectors can be

associated with the operators of quantum theory.'®

EXAMPLE

Consider once again, a particle P of mass m orbiting a body B of mass M, where now the masses are of micro dimensions. Suppose that P has a circular orbit about B and that m = 9.1093897 x 10°! kg and M = 1.6726231 x 107’ kg. These masses just happen to be the mass of the so-called ‘electron’ and ‘proton’ respectively, in the Bohr hydrogen atom. It must not be supposed that we are implying that the electron physically orbits the proton in a circular orbit, or indeed in any continuous classical orbit. According to POAMS, at the ultimate microphysical level, the paths of the elementary particles are as discrete as the particles themselves, with angular momentum that is quantised in amounts irreducible

below an absolute limit of 2/27.

If it is assumed that there are no spin effects present in this system, taking account of orbital angular momentum alone provides natural orbital parameters and hence the parameters of the hydrogen atom which are clearly nonsensical. For assuming that our two-body system has a total angular momentum whose magnitude is 4/27 and that there are no spin effects present, it follows by (3.17) that

$$v_0 = GmM/L = 2\pi GmM_s/h \tag{5.1}$$

where vp is the orbital speed of P. Taking h/2m © 1.054572749 x 10° Kg ms and G = 6.67259 x 10! Nm’ kg”, (5.1) gives

vo = 9.640627 x 10°4 ms". it then follows by (3.18) that the radius of the natural circular orbit of P about B is

given by

$$r_0 = L/mv_0 = h/(2\pi mv_0) \approx 1.2 \times 10^{-12}\;\text{m}$$

Hence, in this scenario, the ‘electron’ orbits the ‘proton’ at a truly enormous distance, at almost zero speed! This clearly demonstrates that orbital angular momentum alone is not sufficient to explain the parameters of the hydrogen atom. The implication is that, in POAMS, the effects of spin become significant at the quantum level. Notice that the orbital kinetic energy of the mass P in its spin-free

natural orbit is, by above, $K_o = mv_0^2/2 \approx 4.23 \times 10^{-18}\;\text{J}$.

The hypothesis that the ‘electron’, considered as an elementary particle, has an intrinsic angular momentum, as though it were spinning, was first introduced by Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit in 1926." This, of course, was not known to Bohr when he proposed his model for the atom in 1913. According to our approach, it is the presence of this spin angular momentum that provides the correct parameters for the hydrogen atom, without assuming the existence of an electrostatic force. Following the technique introduced in section 4, it is necessary to incorporate the intrinsic (spin) kinetic energy of the ‘electron’ P in the calculation of its orbital kinetic energy for its proper orbit about the ‘proton’ B. We proceed by finding the mechanical energy equivalent, EF, in joules, of the magnitude of the conventional

'electron charge' \(e\). This is given by

$$E = h\nu_e \tag{5.2}$$

where Jp is the ionization potential for hydrogen, i.e. the kinetic energy required to ionize the hydrogen atom.'’ Here, Jy = 13.6 volts and e ~ 1.60217733 x 10° coulomb. In POAMS, it is this mechanical energy-equivalent of the static charge which is interpreted as the intrinsic (spin) kinetic energy, K,, of the ‘electron’ so that by (5.2), it follows in this case that

$$\mathcal{K} = K_o + K_s = E = 2.179 \times 10^{-18}\;\text{J}$$

Notice that the purely orbital kinetic energy, K,, of the natural orbit of P, without taking spin effects into consideration, is almost negligible compared to K, . This

means that in following the technique of section 4, we can take K = K,, where K

is the orbital kinetic energy of the ‘electron’ P in its true ‘circular orbit’, independent of the direction of its theoretical spin in relation to the plane of its theoretical orbit about B. (According to quantum mechanics, there are only two possible spin-states for the ‘electron’, which are conventionally labelled ‘up’ or ‘down’. Analogously, in POAMS it is possible conventionally to assign an intrinsic angular momentum vector to the ‘electron’, which points either parallel to its orbital angular momentum vector or in the opposite direction. Since, from section 4, K = K, + KX, in the first case and K = K, — K, in the second, K ~ K, in either case, so that the direction of spin has no effect on the parameters of the orbit of P.)

It then follows from (4.13) that the orbital speed, v,, of P in its natural

orbit, taking spin effects into account, is v5 = (2K/m)!? = (2K,/m)" = 2.1877 x 10° mst. Then from (4.14), the radius, r,, of the natural orbit of P is given by

$$r_s = (h/2\pi)/mv_s = 5.292 \times 10^{-11}\;\text{m}$$

1'7 and

The parameters v; and 7, are the same as those predicted by Bohr's mode by quantum mechanics for the hydrogen atom.”” In POAMS, these parameters can be explained in terms of an ‘equation of motion’ of the form (4.15) for some

particular value of the ‘gravitational factor’ G. For this ‘natural orbit’ of the

‘electron’, (4.15) gives the value of G as G=ryvZ/M = 1.5142 x 10°? N m? kg”.

In this way, Coulomb's law of electrostatics is replaced with what is virtually the Newtonian gravitational inverse square law, but with a different value of G. It must be remembered however, that in POAMS, this is not due to some unseen force acting in vacuo. The reason, of course, for this huge increase in the value of G is the presence of the relatively enormous amount of the intrinsic kinetic energy of the ‘electron’. This example can be generalized to consider the case of an

electron in what is conventionally called an ‘excited state’ in the hydrogen atom.!”

It needs to be stressed that our aim in this example is to demonstrate philosophically how those parameters, calculated by Bohr in terms of a Newtonian dynamics laced with the electrodynamics of Faraday, Maxwell, Coulomb and others, could logically have been derived from Newtonian-type dynamics alone, simply by altering the value of the ‘gravitational constant’ G. And again it must be emphasized that what we are proposing here is not some new concept of the atom to compete with the current physical models that superseded Bohr's. That is to say, it is by no means an exercise in up-to-date physics. It is essentially a logical demonstration of how, ahistorically, our concepts of motion and distant interaction might have developed in direct observational terms, without postulating invisible intermediaries such as ‘gravitational’ and ‘electrostatic’ forces, linking one atom or part of an atom to

another.

9.5.1 &lsquo;Attraction&rsquo; and &lsquo;repulsion&rsquo; in the hydrogen atom

It is to be recalled that in POAMS, all talk of ‘attraction’ and ‘repulsion’ between orbiting particles and bodies becomes redundant. This was emphasized in subsection 4.2. In POAMS, those arguments apply equally well at the micro- physical level and so it makes no sense to talk of ‘attraction’ and ‘repulsion’ in the hydrogen atom as such, but only degrees of relative ‘attraction’ and ‘repulsion’ in the sense described above, in subsection 4.2. In these terms, we claim that the ‘attraction’ of the ‘electron’ to the ‘proton’ is so much larger than that supplied by “gravitational effects’ alone because of the enormous amount of intrinsic (spin) angular momentum associated with the ‘electron’. If the total spin angular momentum were reduced to zero, then the behaviour of the ‘electron’ with respect to the ‘proton’ would revert to that described by the first part of the preceding example, where the degree of relative ‘attraction’ between the two particles is minimal. This would be tantamount to the sort of ‘repulsion’ and complete ionization that is described in classical electrodynamics. Since the orbital kinetic energy of the electron in its natural, spin-free orbit is negligible compared that of

its spin kinetic energy, the arguments of section 4 imply that the relative

‘attraction’ of the ‘electron’ to the ‘proton’ is independent of the direction of the

electron’s associated spin angular momentum.

The mechanism behind relative ‘attraction’ and ‘repulsion’ in the hydrogen atom can be investigated further by assigning a spin angular momentum to each of the conventional masses. As in quantum theory, we associate an intrinsic angular momentum of magnitude 4/42 with the ‘electron’ assuming, by convention, that it behaves as if its spin angular momentum vector is perpendicular to the plane of its orbit.'® (In the vector approach to quantum theory, the component of the spin angular momentum vector of the electron, perpendicular to the plane of its orbit, is taken to be th/4x.) Since the magnitude of the total angular momentum for the ‘ground state’ orbit of the electron is 4/27, it is logical, in POAMS, to consider the hydrogen atom as if it were a two-body system consisting of a spinning mass P orbiting another spinning mass B, in which both masses have an associated spin angular momentum vector, the

magnitude of the sum of these vectors being h/2z.

For simplicity, in view of the cancelling-out of other possible angular momentum effects, we may suppose that the total angular momentum is derived from the spin angular momenta of both masses, each with magnitude //4z. Since the magnitude of the total angular momentum is //2z, the spin angular momentum vectors of the masses must be considered as pointing in the same direction. Theoretically, if the two vectors were pointing in opposite directions, then the magnitude of the total angular momentum would be zero, so that the whole atom would collapse. In this sense, equal anguiar momentum vectors give rise to a relative ‘repulsive’ effect, without the presence of which the atomic system could not exist. This can be interpreted as implying, in conventional terms, that ‘like spins repel’, and ‘unlike spins attract’.

In summary then, the statement in classical electrostatics that ‘unlike charges attract’ is replaced in POAMS by concluding that the magnitude of the

added spin angular momentum of the electron gives rise to a ‘relative attraction’

between the electron and the proton in the form of the classical Bohr atom. This classical atom does not collapse completely since there is also a relative ‘repulsive’ effect between the electron and the proton in the sense that their associated spins are alike. The conclusion in POAMS is that it is this balance between these relative ‘attractive’ and ‘repulsive’ effects, not that of static

‘charges’ in centrifugal motion, that provides the true picture.

Our preceding analysis of the hydrogen atom, which talks of particle spin as a replacement for the esoteric ‘charge’, could be interpreted as describing an alternative approach to electrostatics. However, according to classical electrodynamics, an orbiting electric charge produces a magnetic field, so that the electron generates a magnetic moment proportional to its orbital angular momentum. Similarly, the spinning electron possesses an intrinsic magnetic moment proportional to its spin angular momentum. This interacts with the magnetic moment proportionally to its orbital angular momentum, so that there is an implicit interaction between the orbital and intrinsic angular momentum of the electron. (This is precisely the interaction between spin and orbital angular momentum that is postulated in POAMS.) Hence, it could be argued that spin angular momentum is more naturally connected with magnetostatics than electrostatics. The implication here is that the POAMS model of the hydrogen

atom contains an element of both electrostatics and magnetostatics.

Pope and I believe that whereas the POAMS pure-angular-momentum paraphrase of ‘electrostatic’ orbits is that of a spin-orbital relation between electron-masses and proton-masses, magnetic effects can be explained in terms of a spin angular momentum relation between electrons only. Naively, the idea is that two electrons which spin in opposite directions, and so have spin angular momentum vectors which point in opposite directions, are more relatively ‘attracted’ (in the sense that has been explained previously in this section), than two electrons that spin in the same direction (analogous to the electron and proton pair in the POAMS approach to the hydrogen atom). However, it must be

remembered that in POAMS, the relative ‘attraction’ due to the directions of the

spin anguiatr momentum vectors is always balanced with the relative ‘attraction’ due to the magnitude of the spin kinetic energies. This accords well with the traditional view, as expounded George Galeczki, who states, ‘At very short distances, like in the helium atom, two electrons form a ‘closed configuration’ due to the attraction between anti-parallel spins, which seems to overcome the strong

“Coulomb repulsion” between the electrons’ (here thought of as orbiting charges).

9.6 Conclusion

The POAMS approach to orbital motion has its roots firmly planted in Pope’s Normal Realist philosophy. Normal Realism represents a new coherent and consistent philosophical approach to physics. One big advantage of POAMS is that it clearly states its philosophical starting point in this way, so that its postulates are based firmly on consistent philosophical criteria. Hence, rather than being simply another physics hypothesis, it represents a whole and completely different approach based on a distinct philosophy. In contrast, we believe that many modem physical theories are too axiomatic in their approach, without any real physical reasoning behind the choice of axioms. There is the general question of whether axioms and postulates are the true bases for physics or for science in general. As Pope sees it, ‘the true bases for science are (empirical) facts, without which so much talk of axioms, postulates, hypotheses etc. is just noise.” We believe that although one cannot build a scientific theory (or synthesis) on empirical facts alone, any postulates for such a theory must have their bases in empirical (observational) fact. Otherwise, the theory is in danger of becoming a sterile, academic exercise, far removed from the physical reality it is attempting to

model.

Unfortunately, the majority of physicists still need to be convinced of both the necessity for, and importance of, a coherent philosophical approach to physics. This being the case, the question that needs to be addressed here is whether POAMS offers other advantages over existing theories, in particular as regards its approach to orbital motion. Recall! that the POAMS approach to motion

is based on two fundamental postulates, i.e., motion is naturally orbital and that

angular momentum is holistically conserved. However, as made explicit in this chapter, there are other assumptions made and so POAMS does not attempt to be a new axiomatic physical theory in any accepted sense but rather represents a synthesis of ideas. One advantage of POAMS is that from these postulates, together with observational and empirical evidence, we are able to deduce the general equation for orbital motion, (3.9), without the assumption of Newton’s inverse square law involving an in vacuo ‘gravitational’ force. In consequence, POAMS supposes that freely moving (unconstrained) objects follow a natural orbit, which is not determined by any ‘gravitational’ force. The disadvantage here of course, is the use of Bertrand’s Theorem,!° which is relatively difficult to prove. Hence, it should be noted that the POAMS account of orbital motion does not make fewer assumptions than the Newtonian approach. However, by supposing that rea! (measurable) forces occur only in orbits that are constrained in some way, POAMS provides, at least in our opinion, a more logical explanation of why objects which are dropped fall towards the earth’s surface and why objects weigh what they do on the earth’s surface, than simply that it is the earth’s

“gravitational pull’.

The POAMS approach to orbital motion naturally incorporates spin, and one big advantage in this lies in explaining the structure of the hydrogen atom purely in terms of angular momentum at the microphysical level, without the need for ‘electrostatic forces’. This shows some promise in attempting to unify the approach to both the micro- and macro-scales by using the common factor of angular momentum, instead of having to introduce the apparently disparate in vacuo field forces at each level, in the form of ‘gravitational’ and ‘electrostatic? forces. However, whether or not the predicted POAMS effect of spin on orbital motion at the macro-physical level, as outlined in section four, agrees with

empirical evidence still remains to be seen.

From a mathematical point of view, a drawback of the POAMS approach to particle motion is that since all motion is supposed to be naturally orbital, all

unconstrained motion is non-linear, as apposed to linear in the case of Newtonian

dynamics. Hence, although there are very clear philosophical reasons for the POAMS approach, it appears that the mathematical reasoning (but not the mathematics itself) behind POAMS is more complicated than in Newtonian dynamics. Certainly, from a fundamental measurement point of view, any linear concept appears more natural and most physicists would argue that it is logical to suppose linear motion is more fundamental than non-linear, orbital motion. On the other hand, if it is verified to be correct, the mathematical analysis of spin effects

in POAMS is simpler than the analysis of spin effects in Newtonian theory.

Normal Realism demands a paradigm shift in physics and POAMS represents such a shift in the treatment of orbital motion and its associated extrapolations down to the micro-level. If must be recognised that POAMS is continually evolving. We have now found that when time dilation is taken into account in POAMS, time dilation effects predicted by GR are obtained as well as the perihelion shift in the orbits of the planets, also as predicted by GR. (See Chapter 11, section 2: Anthony D. Osborne: Further developments in the Pope-

Osborne angular momentum synthesis.)

However, it is debatable as to whether conservation and quantisation of angular momentum are sufficient in themselves to explain all observed phenomena related in some way to ‘orbital motion’. For example, in Peter Rowlands’ view, ‘The angular momentum approach will not work for particle physics, which depends on the recognition that the strong, electric and weak

charges interact only with charges of the same nature.’ -

It is our belief that there is an urgent need for a coherent and consistent philosophical approach to physics. Normal Realism, of which POAMS is a part, addresses this need. This programme dictates that all philosophically incoherent approaches to physics should be jettisoned. However, on a purely practical level, this does not imply that all existing physical theories should be abandoned. (In the same way, for example, Newtonian dynamics was not eliminated upon the advent of SR.) For instance, it is possible to fit Newtonian dynamics into the new model

by simply adopting a consistently phenomenalist stance of the Machian type. The

same is true of the Normal Realist approach to SR and this is discussed more fully in Chapter 5, A Tale Of Two Paradigms.

Chapter 10: How to Get Something From Nothing

Peter Rowlands

Many difficult concepts will be discussed in this chapter, but the basis of all the ideas in it is profoundly simple. It is that the totality of the conceptual schemes we use to structure mathematics and physics is required to be zero at every level. In that case, we can develop a dualistic mathematics which has a very specific relationship to what we conceive of as the physical world. Many physical notions are generated by this process, in particular the idea that we cannot fully explain physical systems without the dualistic notion of time-delayed action between the discrete objects of physical measurement occurring at the same time as unmediated instantaneous correlation-at-a-distance (UICAAD). The analysis is used also to discuss the nature of gravity and inertia, and the quantum/classical transition, and to highlight the significant role played by angular momentum in unifying the fundamental concepts of physics at the quantum level.

10.1 Foundations of mathematics

Many scientists today believe that the concept of nothing has a fundamental role to play in an understanding of the universe and its contents. It is, for example, often stated that the universe itself could have emerged as a quantum fluctuation from an essentially zero state, and that the total zero energy is maintained by the total positive mass energy being equated numerically to the negative gravitational energy generated by this mass through a special application of Mach’s principle. This is the kind of reasoning behind the statement of the science-writer, Peter Atkins, that ‘the seemingly something is elegantly reorganised nothing, and ... the net content of the universe is ... nothing’. However, the concept of nothing may

be even more powerful than this, for, as I have long believed, it may be that the

T Editors? footnote: An example of this is the familiar Newton’s law of equal and opposite action and reaction.

nothingness applies also to the entire conceptual scheme of which matter and the universe are merely components.?* Nothing is unique among conceptual ideas in being infinitely degenerate and it may be that this infinite degeneracy is the key to

an understanding of its special power.

However, as we all know, nihil ex nihilo fit (‘nothing comes from nothing’), and the problem then is to explain how something can ever emerge from nothing. Both mathematics and physics suggest that the answer lies in the concept of duality, for, as Nicholas Young has noted, ‘the idea of duality pervades mathematics’,® and, as I have proposed on many earlier occasions, the scheme required for preserving the conceptual nothingness of mathematical physics, is essentially a dualistic one. However, duality itself is not the most fundamental idea, as it is predicated on the idea of a discrete numbering system, which, contrary to popular belief, is, in my opinion, not the primary source of mathematics. Although there are many ways in which mathematics can be generated from foundational elements, physics seems to indicate that discreteness at the foundational level is always associated with dimensionality, and hence is not a truly primary concept. In any case, a mathematics founded on natural numbers seems to have no obvious system of progression to the reals, which then appear as a nonlogical extension. It would seem to be more logical, therefore, to

suppose that the real numbers are anterior to the integers.

Even real ‘numbers’, however, are too specific a starting point, since they assume a concept of numbering which can only be related to integers. So we begin logically with a category of things (9 ) which is simply unspecified and undefined, but which can only be examined in relation to itself. We now propose that the assumption of any such non-zero category must immediately lead to a return to zero, which, in mathematical terms, means creating a ‘negative’ category or ‘conjugate’ (-#?) corresponding to the original one; the combination must then produce the desired zero state by some process (not yet specified as ‘addition’). A process of ‘self-referencing’, correlation or ‘combination’, which can be

expressed in the form # x 9 (again without assuming a specific mathematical

interpretation), has produced an extended categorisation, from (#7 ) to (#f, —9?). Zero, however, is infinitely degenerate, and the self-referencing process can be imagined as extending to infinity, producing a new categorisation at every stage. The process can be encapsulated in a universal rewrite mechanism, as has been accomplished in a paper written with Bemard Diaz.’ The simple rule is that no non-zero character set or alphabet is complete, so every self-referencing yields a new or extended alphabet, which is necessarily self-conjugated. However, every correlation of an alphabet with respect to a component or subset of the alphabet will yield the alphabet itself.

At the stage where we generate (#, -#7) we have a concept of ordinality, though not of counting, as there is no discreteness or anything fixed within the procedure. However, discreteness comes naturally from extending the alphabet. To extend the alphabet, we need to create new characters, e.g., €, €’, €", ..., each with its own conjugate, —C, -€’, -€", ... , and combinations of these characters, e.g., €€', €€", CC", ... . The general rewrite rules also determine specific rules which must apply to these combinations. In particular, all new characters must be complex forms, that is, have self-combinations, e.g., CC, which are of the original conjugate form —J, and there are infinitely possible or indefinitely possible systems with forms of this nature. In addition, self-combinations of such combined forms as €€' or €€" must themselves be of the form # or -# Significantly, these two options lead to entirely different structural consequences; the first (the ‘commutative’ option) being open-ended and indefinitely extendible, the second (the ‘anti-commutative’ option) being closed and excluding further possibilities. Although we have unlimited choice in our range of possible Structures, if we choose to default on the second option, choosing indistinguish- ability between the Cs in every conceivable respect, we create a uniform ordinal series of discrete algebras, which has the properties of the integer sequence in binary form. We are then free to apply this new concept of integral number to the

original undefined category 9, to create the category of real number, and, if we

choose, to reinterpret #7, C, etc., etc., in terms of real and complex units, 1, i, etc.,

subject to standard arithmeticai operations.

The commutative and anti-commutative options provide foundations for the respective mathematica! structures which we call Grassmann and Hamilton algebras. The Grassmann, or infinite-dimensional vector, algebra leads to the infinite Hilbert vector space of quantum mechanics, while the Hamilton algebra produces the cyclic or 3-dimensional system of quaternions, with components restricted to a set such as €, €', CC’. It is the cyclicity of quaternions which introduces their characteristic discreteness or closure, and it is thus no coincidence

that discreteness in physics is invariably associated with dimensionality.

The character sets or alphabets, according to these rules, represent all those, including #, -9# which are unchanged by operating on themselves, though this

operation then generates the extension of the alphabet:

The order 16 group of complex quaternions is notably equivalent to the ‘real’ dimensional structure of 3-dimensional multivariate vector space, as used in the geometrical algebra of Hestenes and others.*” The components, + 1, + i, + fi, + iy, + , + bi, + i, + hit, can be rearranged and written in the form + 1, +i, +i, + j, t k, + ii, + ij, + ik, where + 1, + i, become the respective scalar and pseudoscalar, and i, j, k, and ii, ij, ik the respective vector and pseudovector terms of this algebra. The order 16 group is also the point at which the extension of the sequence, in the simplest representation becomes one of repetition. The entire range of processes, therefore, is specified once we have defined the scalar, pseudo-scalar, quaternion and multivariate vector groups of order 2, 4, 8 and 16. Taken as independent entities, their units, + 1, + i, + i, + j, + k, + i, + j, + k, combine minimally in the group of order 64, and the algebra of this group is that of the gamma matrices used in the Dirac equation, the one which applies to the most

fundamental components of physical matter.

10.2 Foundations of physics

The mathematical structure created here, though based on what appear to be physical requirements, is also very general, and can be related by mappings internal to the structure to areas of mathematics which are not directly physical. However, mathematics directly related to physics has a special interest as the structure of physics has had to withstand constant testing under experimental conditions. Physics appears to tell us not only what is necessary to a foundational

structure for mathematics, but also what is the most efficient such structure.

Physics follows the mathematical structure outlined here in two different ways, which may be combined when we reach the level of the Dirac equation. In both cases, the ultimate elements are the fundamental parameters space, time, mass and charge. In the first instance, the three fundamental procedures ~ conjugation, complexification, and dimensionalisation — are employed in a way which privileges none; and, in the second instance, each of the four parameters is defined by one of the four algebraic structures required to define the groups of order 2, 4, 8 and 16. The combination of these ideas in the Dirac equation seems to provide the minimum possible structure for returning to zero from a non-zero

starting point.

It has long been a fundamental tenet of my work in physics that the only truly fundamental physical parameters are space, time, and the sources of the four interactions: mass(-energy) for gravity, and the three types of charge — electromagnetic, strong and weak.2> Aithough the symmetry between the three types of charge is broken at normal energies, it is widely believed that, under a set of what may be considered ideal conditions (Grand Unification), all would have identical properties and identical measured strengths. The fact that interactions between identical charges are of opposite sign (repulsive) to those between identical masses (attractive) leads naturally to a quaternionic representation, in which charges take the imaginary units (, j, k), while mass takes the real unit (1). At the same time, the relativistic representation of space and time as a 4-vector,

with respective units i, j, k and i, suggests the existence of a fundamental

symmetry between the parameters, which becomes even more exact when the space units are taken to be multivariate to accommodate spin in a natural manner. It can be seen from this analysis that the four parameters can be represented as

stages in the emergent algebra we have created:

order2 __ real scalar 1 mass order4 —_— pseudoscalar i time order 8 quaternions i,j,k charge order 16 multivariate vectors i,j,k space

However, the symmetry between the parameters goes even deeper, for it extends also to their physical, as well as algebraic properties, and can itself be

represented in a group format:

space nonconserved real dimensional / discrete time nonconserved imaginary nondimensional / continuous mass conserved real nondimensional /continuous charge conserved imaginary dimensional / discrete

The exactness of this physical symmetry has been the basis of all my previous work in physics, and has led to many new results, as well as demonstrations or derivations of ones already known. An exact symmetry, with a perfect distribution between properties and ‘anti-properties’ is also essential to maintaining a conceptual nothingness. The key property here is the one in the first column, for physics structures itself by defining systems in which conserved quantities remain fixed while nonconserved quantities vary absolutely. The property and anti-property, conservation and nonconservation, are both absolute and absolutely opposite to each other, and nearly every fundamental law in physics is an expression of this fact. Differential equations show the variation of space and time coordinates while retaining the fixed values of mass and charge,

and of the quantities which depend upon them, such as energy, momentum and

angular momentum. Quantum mechanics, in fully expressing the nonconservation properties of space and time, is more explicable, in this form, than classical

physics, which is based on imposing sets of restrictions.

In terms of the fundamental algebra we have generated, the symmetry can also be expressed in the following form, with the fundamental properties taking

on the characteristics of those of the emergent algebra:

space nonconjugated real dimensional time nonconjugated complex nondimensional mass conjugated real nondimensional charge conjugated complex dimensional

Conjugated, here, means that positive values cannot be created or destroyed without the simultaneous creation or destruction of negative ones, so a positive charge or unit of mass-energy cannot be created, without also creating a negative one. Non-conjugated means that no such restriction is applied. Dimensional quantities now become identical to discrete ones, exactly as in the fundamental algebra. Only the (3-)dimensional quantities, space and charge, are discrete or countable, and, physically, one cannot imagine a mechanism for dividing the units in a single dimension. Time thus becomes physically irreversible and mass-energy physically uni-polar precisely because neither quantity allows a dimensional discontinuity or origin representing a zero state. In addition to this, the mathematical processes which allow for the continual recreation of new non- integral structures in one-to-one correspondence with the integers, such as rational and algebraic numbers, could not be conceived of in a system without dimensionality. It is significant that, as in conventional mathematics, two versions of the ‘real’ numbers are required for the proposed physical structure: while the uncountable reals of the Cantor continuum and standard analysis are needed for mass (and their complex equivalents for time), the countable reals of the Léwenheim-Skolem arithmetic and Robinson’s non-standard analysis are needed

for space. Without this distinction, the paradoxes of Zeno cannot be resolved.

10.3 The Dirac state

Space, time, mass and charge are separate structures governed by different algebras, but physics has found a way of combining them into a single package, which provides a dramatic short-cut back to the zero origin. This is what we call the Dirac state, and its origins may be found in the algebra. As has been said previously, the simplest group within the structure which contains the groups of order 2, 4, 8 and 16 as individual items is the group of order 64. Essentially, if we take the units 1, i, i, j, k, and i, j, k, for mass, time, charge, and space, and combine them into a single algebra, together with conjugation and non- conjugation, or conservation and nonconservation, to incorporate both + and — units, we obtain the + and — versions of the 32-part Dirac algebra, equivalent to the group of order 64 represented above. Conventionally, the Dirac algebra is represented by the five gamma matrices and their binomial products, but it is more convenient to represent it in a purely algebraic form.'®'' To represent 32 binomial products (64 allowing for sign changes due to anti-commutativity), we need only five basic elements, and mathematically, we can show that a pentad set such as k, iii, iij, ik, ij, or, alternatively, ik, ii, ij, dk, j has the exact format required. Such a set would be exactly isomorphic to the conventional gamma

matrices, and can be used instead of these in writing the Dirac equation.

In principle, we can say that a set of five elements is more fundamental than a set of eight, and the five elements generate the eight units that represent mass, time, space and charge, along with all the other combinations, That is, it is possible to create a more compacted, composite package than the one presented by taking the fundamental parameters themselves as primitives. Mathematically, this can only be done in a pentad form, and it is in creating this package that we also create the Dirac state. In every case, we take one three-dimensional parameter and superimpose its components on each of the other units. In principle, we could use space, but the conservation property makes it more

convenient to use charge. So, we begin with:

time space mass charge i ijk 1 ijk

Then, taking, each of the charge units onto one of the algebraic expressions

representing by time, mass or space,

time space mass charge i ijk i ijk k i j

we obtain the following combinations:

ik ii ij ik j For mathematical convenience, and for compatibility with the conventional way of writing the Dirac algebra, we will often write this in the form:

k iii iij ik y

This process of creating a compacted set of composite units has two

significant physical consequences. The first is that the quaternion units, i, j, k, have changed from being symmetrical and indistinguishable representations of independent charges into composite units whose symmetry is broken, and it is possible, from the composition of ik, (ii, ij, ik), and j, to derive the respective SU(2), SU(3) and U(1) symmetries associated with the weak, strong and electric charges. The second is that the application of the characteristics of the conserved and quantised units of charge to the parameters time, space and mass leads to respective composite units which have the overall characteristics of these parameters, but are also conserved and quantised: namely, the pseudo-scalar Dirac energy (£), the multivariate vector Dirac momentum (p) and the real scalar Dirac rest mass (mm). (Another conserved and quantised quantity, the Dirac angular momentum, relates to the directional properties of the vector term, and, in a sense, the Dirac state as a whole. This will be discussed in more detail later.) The act of

imposing charge’s three-dimensional structure onto the original time, space and

mass is identical to the act of quantisation. Dimensional quantities are necessarily

countable, and charge units are also conserved, so quantisation is the result.

From a physical point of view, the most significant aspect of creating the Dirac state, which, after combining all the vector components into a single momentum term, we may now represent in the form (+ AE + iip + ifm) or (+ ikE + ip + jm), simultaneously incorporating four possible ‘solutions’, is that the three components E, p, and m, are, from the fundamental properties of their parent-parameters time, space, and mass(-energy), characterised by unrestricted real number values. That is, it is possible, using the anti-commuting properties of the quaternion and vector operators, and the fact that at least one term (but not all) must be complex, to find values of the composite expression, whose self- operation (squaring in an algebra based on numerical units) leads to a zero numerical solution. In other words, it is possible, to extend the emergent algebraic structure to infinity, with a zero final solution, by positing an infinite Hilbert space of nilpotent Dirac pentads and their superposition states (or combinations), each of which is uniquely specified with respect to each other. In this way, the Dirac nilpotents (or unique square roots of zero) become the units of an infinite higher algebra, whose units become zero only by self-operation. It is this algebra that provides the basic parameterisation that we describe as physics. The need for incorporating the nilpotents into an infinite higher algebra means, in physical

terms, that all existing quantum states must be entangled.

The Dirac nilpotent describes a conservation state but the picture can be completed only if we also describe the equivalent process of non-conservation. This is the meaning of the Dirac equation. In the fundamental parameter group, space and time are the quantities which are not conserved, and the most absolute way of specifying non-conservation, in the idealised free state, is via the

expression ¢@'~

P") to which we then apply a _nonconservation or differential operator of the form (4 ik /A t + iV + jm) such that the eigenvalue or result

becomes identical to the expression for the Dirac state (+ ikE + ip + jm). The

complete specification involving the nonconserved, as well as the conserved, parameters, is then an example of the self-operation returning to zero. So, we write the Dirac equation for the free fermion, or Dirac state, in the form: mae) - we ce) it par) tik a+ iV + ijm (LKELiiptime “7 =0. In any non-free (i.e. interacting) state, the functional expression would, of course,
