would always be true. Classical systems are simply ones in which multiple interactions have a manifestly large effect on the freedom of variation in space and time.

There can be little doubt that the nilpotent form is the classic representation of the Dirac equation, all other forms yielding only a partial range of its consequences. The nilpotent form, for example, is already second quantised, with the differential operator and the state vector being identical. That is, it already incorporates quantum field theory and the vacuum. If fermions are represented by

state vectors of the form (+ KE + if p + if m), then antifermions are simply

fermions in whose state vectors the E terms are reversed (+ KE + ii p + if m), while bosons are scalar combinations of fermions and antifermions, (LKE+ ii p+ if m) (F KE + ii p + ij m), and baryons are fermions which split the p term into three components. The two signs for the Z term derive from the two signs for the imaginary time parameter, while the two signs for p result from its dimensionality. The negative versions of E are thus fundamentally different in character from the negative versions of p, being ‘mathematical’ rather than ‘physical’ in origin. This is why negative p terms are of equal status to positive ones, but negative E terms do not exist in the ground state of the universe. The m

term, also, remains positive in all cases because mass is uni-polar, and, in the

transition from quantum to classical systems, as a positive scalar, remains

unchanged.

The nilpotent form of the Dirac state vector removes the infrared divergence immediately. It is also intrinsically super-symmetric, fermions creating boson states and bosons creating fermion states in the vacuum, and allowing renormalisation at any level. Pauli exclusion for identical fermion states becomes obvious, as does half-integral spin and the Berry phase.” In principle, the Dirac equation in this form is the classic statement in physics of the combined conservation principles of energy, momentum, angular momentum, and charge, and the basic laws of both quantum and classical physics may be derived by applying the appropriate constraints, ‘

10.4 Relativity

The creation of the Dirac nilpotent has many significant consequences outside of pure quantum mechanics. One is that the units of the individual components must be related numerically. Through the Dirac equation, E and t, and p and r, become conjugate variables, i.e., ones which exchange statements about conservation into equivalent statements about nonconservation, and vice versa. This requires both direct and inverse numerical relations. Numerical relationships must also be established between the units Z and p, and between those of ¢ and r. We are thus required to introduce the constants / and c, here equated, formally, to 1. A third constant, G, is required to relate charge to mass units (or direct relationships to inverse ones). The Planck mass, in this theory, (fic/G)', is the energy at which all four physical forces have the same value. In addition, the conjugate nature of E, p and ¢, r means that we can also establish a nilpotent structure connecting ¢ and r, with another term 7 (described as ‘proper time’) in the position occupied by m. Defining the nilpotent (+ At + iir + ijd or (+ ikt + ir + j2) means that we have essentially established Special Relativity (SR) purely in terms of algebra and a

relationship between the units. Without any physical assumptions, we derive:

$$(\pm i\mathbf{k}t + i\mathbf{r} + j\tau)(\pm i\mathbf{k}t + i\mathbf{r} + j\tau) = -t^2 + r^2 + \tau^2 = 0$$

The ‘physical’ content of ‘relativity’ then becomes whatever interpretation we choose to place upon the mathematical consequences. It depends on whether we decide, in the case of a non-free state, to retain the constancy of c as a convention,

or allow it to vary in an appropriate way.

Momentum-energy, and space-time, are regarded as pure 4-vector combinations in SR. In a quantum system, this is not strictly true, because the Dirac energy and momentum terms are only fully represented mathematically when each has a different quaternion operator applied to its respective pseudo- scalar or vector. When taking the invariant scalar product, of course, these operators disappear, but their significance becomes apparent when we introduce a third term, with yet another quaternion operator (rest mass) to convert them into a nilpotent. The same must apply, in general, to relativistic time and space, whether the system is quantum or classical. The proper time, the additional term which completes the nilpotent, is a real scalar, like rest mass, and its squared value must exactly cancel the scalar product of the time and space components. The fact that it can never be negative, by analogy with rest mass, means that only retarded solutions are possible for the space-time combination. The validity of proper time in both classical and quantum contexts is an indication that the link between these two domains is essentially through the scalar additive nature of the rest mass or

‘inertia’ of the component systems.

It is apparent from this analysis that the space, time, and proper time, or momentum, energy, and rest mass, have a ‘dimensional’ character, which results from the process by which they are quantised. Interestingly, this emphasises the fact that time and proper time are fundamentally different physical concepts, although the proper time is numerically equivalent to the time value in the context of a zero space component (that is, the time value measured by a clock carried with an object). The ‘relativistic’ properties are purely a result of this dimensional property, as are the constants f and c. We may relate this to the 3-dimensional quantised ‘orthogonality’ in the synthesis proposed by Viv Pope and Anthony

Osborne, which also defines ‘relativity’ in an algebraic manner.

The quantisation involved in creating the Dirac state may be thought of as a localisation of energy-related processes, or an expression of time-delayed action between discrete ‘sources’ (charges), with a maximum transmission rate of c for a zero-rest mass system. However, the entire nilpotent structure is founded on the principle of instant correlation of all fermionic state vectors, and is nonlocal in this sense. The specification of uniqueness requires instant correlation, at the same time as the nilpotent nature of the operator and its built-in 4-vector component require time-delayed action between the discrete charges. The fundamentally continuous nature of mass-energy, which is related to the absolute unchanging order of events — the principle of causality required by both SR and Newtonian physics — suggests that the carrier of the nonlocal correlation between the Dirac states is the interaction we describe as gravity, which does not rely, as the others

do, on discrete sources.

10.5 Gravity and inertia

The absolute symmetry of the parameter group requires that both mass-energy and time are absolute continua. In the context of cosmology this would seem to imply that the universe is infinite both in time and in extent (with a single absolute reference frame, presumably coincident with that of the microwave background radiation). Exactly such a continuous mass-energy is required for the weak filled vacuum responsible, via the Higgs mechanism, for supplying inertial mass to fermionic and bosonic matter in the Standard Model of particle physics. This weak filled vacuum, or ‘Higgs field’, is of exactly the same kind as the vacuum Dirac originally postulated to explain the negative energy solutions of his fermionic equation as antifermionic states, and it is significant that, in the Dirac nilpotent formulation, the negative energy operator is also the weak charge operator. Negative fermionic energy states are never directly observed in nature because the ground state of the universe has positive fermionic energy. The predominance of matter over antimatter in the universe is, therefore, a pure result of the continuum nature of mass and is not a product of some cosmological

evolution. It is exactly parallel to the irreversibility of time.

Gravity, in the Pope-Osborne synthesis, is not a force but an expression of the global correlation of angular momentum states. It is certainly of a different nature to the forces produced by discrete charges and, clearly, is necessarily instantaneous. In the theoretical framework described here, it 1s necessarily instantaneous because its source is continuous, and it is clearly responsible for the instantaneous correlation of the infinitely many Dirac nilpotent states that make up the ‘universe’. It is certainly not of the same kind as the forces between

discrete sources, though at Grand Unification its magnitude will be the same.

If gravity really is instantaneous, then the Lorentzian (or gravitationally- affected Lorentzian) space-time of measurement, based on discrete sources, will not supply an inertial frame for a gravitational system. In this sense, Newtonian gravitational equations can only be written in a Newtonian space-time, and using a Lorentzian space-time will have the same effect as using a non-inertial frame in conventional Newtonian mechanics — the introduction of fictitious inertial forces. It is a relatively straightforward procedure to show that the effects of such inertial forces will be of exactly the same kind as the ‘non-Newtonian’ effects predicted by General Relativity (GR). (I describe them elsewhere as the ‘aberration of space’.') However, the theory will no longer require a nonlinear interpretation, and ‘general relativity’ will become a theory of inertia, or the effects of gravity, rather than of gravity itself. The equivalence principle will have the interpretation that gravity causes inertia, but inertia will not be seen as the cause of gravity. In addition, we will not be required to quantise the attractive force of gravity, with a spin-2 gauge boson causing nonlinearity, but rather the fictitious repulsive force of inertia, with a QED-like spin 1 boson (whose energy quantum is the Planck mass), thus preserving the theory’s linearity with no singularities and full renormalisability. Gravitational attractions between discrete sources, however,

will only be detectable through the inertia.

+ Editors’ footnote: See Osborne, Chapter 9 in this volume.

In this theory, it will not be surprising that the universe is flat, Euclidean, and infinite.'* There will be no need to invoke an inflationary expansion, a cosmological constant or quintessence, or even any expansion at all. An application of Mach’s Principle will produce the Hubble redshift, with an apparent acceleration, with deceleration parameter gy = —1, without any continuous creation of matter. Such results, of course, were predicted long before the recent discovery of an expansion in the redshift, with deceleration parameter —1 + 0.4, in a flat, Euclidean, and presumably infinite universe.'*"’ The theory is, in fact, totally independent of any specific cosmological model, and depends only on an application of Mach’s Principle to the theory of inertia, extended here to

include the equivalence of gravitational and inertial mass.

The theory is related to the ideas Peter Graneau has been investigating, and begins with the work of Sciama, who, as early as 1953,'* was investigating the possibility that Mach’s Principle could be explained in terms of a gravitational analogue of the acceleration-independent inductive force of electromagnetic theory,

Since the redshift acceleration is a result of an ‘aberration’ effect in space (or space-time) itself, then this will not be a real radiation from real sources, but an imagined radiation from virtual sources in the filled vacuum. Its appearance as thermal energy can then be interpreted as an illustration of the fact that both redshift and the inertial force

absolute space-time of instantaneous gravitational action. Preliminary (non-

The interpretation of Mach’s Principle given in this analysis is that the value of inertial mass is that which would appear to be produced by the interactions of all the other masses in the universe, since the inertial effect is an apparent, rather than a real, one. The ‘causation’ is thus not a direct physical process, because gravity is not the result of inertia, though the numerical values of the inertial and gravitational quantities have to be equated to maintain the universal balance. In

the universal Machian equation, $r_Hc^2 = Gm_H$, we assume that the gravitational

energy is negative vacuum energy, while the inertial energy is positive, leading to the required zero total. Since the gravitational energy is equivalent here to a vacuum effect we can relate it to the Higgs field, which produces fermionic and bosonic inertial mass, or, equivalently, to the zero-point field using the mass value as a cut-off in the spectral density (a calculation which yields perfectly reasonable results). Another process which produces inertial mass from the vacuum, is that of renormalisation, where we may sum to a finite value of mass for a fermion using a cut-off at the Planck energy; here, the ultimate c-related cut-off value is an

indication that the inertial process involves time delay.

Inertia, in fact, is time-delayed, while gravity is instantaneous. However, the expression for the force of static gravitational interaction, like that for the electrostatic, cannot distinguish between instantaneous and time-delayed transmission. Neal Graneau, who supports the reality of instantaneous electrostatic interaction, has stated that the Casimir effect, which is usually cited as an illustration of the reality of a filled vacuum, with an infinite virtual zero- point energy density, can be explained alternatively by assuming that electrostatic interaction is instantaneous.” According to my own reasoning, an instantaneous

interaction of some kind is exactly equivalent to a filled vacuum. The assumption

that it is electrostatic in origin, rather than gravitational, merely transfers the cause. In principle, the difference between the gravitational and electric forces is not determined by whether either or both are transmitted instantaneously, but

whether the time-delayed effects associated with them are fictitious or real.

in the same context, Graneau argues that the time-delayed aspect observed in the electromagnetic interaction, which results in a finite value for c, the ‘velocity’ of transmission, is a result of the action on a charge of all the other charges in the universe, each acting instantaneously. Again, I believe there is a valid point in this argument, which can be seen more clearly if we reverse it. Charges of any kind ultimately relate to fermionic state vectors, which are necessarily correlated instantaneously, and the behaviour of any charge is determined by that of all the other charges in the universe. Measurable effects involving what is often called ‘energy transfer’, however, are invariably time- delayed, despite results which may superficially appear to deny this, and we could equally well assume that the behaviour of the rest of the charges in the universe was determined by the finite velocity of transmission, but with instantaneous

speed of state vector correlation.

It is the inertial process which creates the concept of ‘localised’ energy. The degree of localisation in the system (inertial rest mass, which is also measured by time delay or by proper time) can be expressed in terms of an apparent metric (‘apparent’ because the Euclidean metric is the only real metric for space) but the mass is not created by the metric. It is probably incorrect also to say that time- delayed action is an indication of ‘transfer of energy’ from place to place. It is likely that energy itself does not move at all, but defines a complete continuum or ‘vacuum’ filling space, its value being infinite in terms of density, but fixed at a particular finite level at each point in space (the Higgs field). This level presumably never changes, and its value (which appears to be 246 GeV) must be determined by the fundamental numerical relation between units of charge and units of rest mass. What really changes is the degree of the realisation of

quantisation in the form of the Z, p and m terms in the Dirac nilpotents — exactly

as in the field excitations of conventional Quantum Field Theory. It is charge, with ‘axes’ k, i, j, which creates this quantisation, irrespective of the occupation or

otherwise of the levels (0 or + 1) on the axes.

10.6 Angular momentum

Angular momentum has a special role to play in this theory, as it does in the synthesis of Pope and Osborne. Here, it is because angular momentum carries the information relevant to charge, which, in this theory, plays the role that ‘matter’ (not to be confused with ‘mass’) does in some others. One well-known result that becomes obvious from the previous analysis is Noether’s theorem, which relates conserved quantities to symmetries or invariance under particular transformations. Invariance under transformation is, effectively, another way of describing nonconservation, and, according to the fundamental duality of the basic theory, every nonconservation concept requires an equivalent conservation concept. The usual ones cited are those involving the conjugate variables. Thus, the translation symmetry of space (non-identity of elements of space) is equivalent to the conservation of the conjugate linear momentum, while the translation symmetry of time (non-identity of elements of time) is equivalent to the conservation of the conjugate energy. At the same time, the rotation symmetry of space (non-identity of directions in space) requires the conservation of a new conjugate parameter,

angular momentum.

Now, as we know, since energy is related to mass by the equation E = mc’, the translation symmetry of time is also linked to the conservation of mass. This is effectively a symmetry which could be taken directly from our parameter group: nonconservation of time implies conservation of mass. It seems reasonable, therefore, to apply the same reasoning to the dimensional parameters, space and charge. So, the translation symmetry of space, or the conservation of linear momentum, implies the conservation of the value of charge — of any type. This we see in the special case of electric charge, which London, in 1927, showed to be identical to invariance under transformations of the electrostatic potential by a

constant representing changes of phase, the phase changes being of the kind

involved in the conservation of linear momentum. In a conserved system, of course, electrostatic potential varies only with the spatial coordinates, so this is, in effect, a statement of the principle that the quantity of electric charge is conserved because the spatial coordinates are not, which is exactly to be expected from the

parameter group, though we could also extend it to weak and strong charges.

However, there is another, more significant result which we can predict from the symmetry of the parameter group. This is a relationship between the rotation symmetry of space; or the conservation of angular momentum, and the conservation of type of charge. That is, we could consider charge units to be arranged along axes which are fundamentally irrotational, so that one type of charge (say, electric) can never be converted into another (say, weak or strong). This is, in fact, the basis of the laws of lepton and baryon conservation, and it is

also the reason why baryon decay has never been detected.

time energy value of translation mass

space angular type of rotation momentum charge

The possibility of such a connection was predicted a long time ago, but only special cases of its application could then be considered. For example, the relation between spin and statistics observed in fundamental particles required fermions and bosons to have different values of spin angular momentum, and they could also be seen to carry weak units of charge. Fermions always had nonzero units of weak charge, while bosons had none. So, at least one type of charge could be linked in this way with a value of angular momentum. However, it is now possible to show that the conservation of angular momentum does indeed require the separate conservation of weak, strong and electric charges, in a much more

fundamental way, through the conservation of the separate properties of

orientation (with respect to the linear momentum), direction, and magnitude.” indeed, it is because of this connection that the electric and weak charges are found to be directly linked. With the strong charge being the one associated with the p operator, it is the combination of the other two charges that affects this,

rather than either taken separately. Defining o, as the spin pseudo-vector component in the z direction; and Pa Ps Pc aS units of quantised angular momentum selected randomly and

independently from the three orthogonal components p,, By, p.., it is even possible to produce a single expression for the entire set of charge structures for fermions

and antifermions available in nature: O2-(é Ba (Sse — 1) +f (Ps — 15m) + & Pe (-1)18 g),

with (d5¢ ~ 1), — 1djm and (~1)%lg g being logical operators derived from the respective SU(3), U(1) and SU(2) symmetries associated with the p, m and iE operators in the Dirac nilpotent. Detailed analysis is not possible here, but the expression incorporates the symmetry-breaking of the Higgs mechanism through the sign of the operator o,, and the actual inertial masses of the various fermionic states are determined by the factors in the rest of the expression which lead to any tendency towards a change in sign. It is through the use of this formalism that it has been possible to predict that an exact Grand Unification of all four forces will

occur at the energy of the Planck Mass.*"!!°

It is clear that the fermionic nilpotent is also, in some sense, an angular momentum operator, although it has other characteristics as well, and that much of the information concerning it, especially with respect to the charge states, is carried by angular momentum terms. This is because, the nilpotent has been structured on the basis of unit charges, the source of quantisation, and the conjugate parameter to the type of charge is angular momentum. In the sense that this makes the Dirac nilpotent an angular momentum operator, with gravity as the

correlation mechanism for Dirac nilpotents, then angular momentum information

is also carried by, or carries, gravity. So, the Pope-Osborne process of using angular momentum to convey information conventionally conveyed by gravity and the electric force is of considerable interest and relevance, and its relationship to the present theory would be worth exploring in considerable detail. However, it should be pointed out that, because of the complicated structure of the sources involved, the angular momentum information carried by a Dirac nilpotent is of a more subtle kind than the U(1)-type absorption or emission process of a single quantum of magnitude #4 would suggest; and the process is also, so far, defined exactly only for an isolated quantum system, or for the type of classical system that can be modelled in the same way. Complications may be expected in respect of the quantum/classical transition, where systems can no longer be defined in an

isolated way.

10.7 The quantum/classical transition

A key result in this context is George Galeczki’s demonstration that there are no microcanonical ensembles in nature.'t In effect, this means that there is no such thing as an isolated system, in particular an isolated quantum system, Every hydrogen atom, if you like, is attempting to be part of a hydrogen molecule with some other hydrogen atom. In fact, for all the apparent similarity between the energy levels between any two given hydrogen atoms, there must always be some subtle difference due to its particular interactions and to Pauli exclusion. Every hydrogen atom necessarily has a unique set of energy levels. It is simply an approximation to assume that there is such a thing as a set of energy levels applicable to hydrogen. The exactness of quantum transitions as countable units is an illusion (just as is the idea that ‘rest mass’ quantises mass). Quantisation is not a process which provides exact counting units. Furthermore, the set of energy levels applicable to any given hydrogen atom must be constantly changing. Each nilpotent structure is unique but never fixed. (This is what allows us to imagine an absolute order for events, a ‘cosmic’ or universal time irrespective of our systems

of time measurement.)

tt Editors’ footnote: See Chapter 8 in this volume.

Pope and Osborne state that there is no such thing as a perfect straight-line motion. This is true, but there is equally no such thing as perfect orbital motion either. Newton found in his Principia that no planet in the solar system ever traversed the same orbit twice — nor does any hydrogen atom. In either case, it is necessary to use the concept of force to show how the defined inertial condition is altered. Orbital systems may be natural on the quantum or even classical scale, but classical measurement is only possible in straight lines, and leads naturally to the conventional definitions of force and linear momentum. The Pope-Osborne synthesis may be a highly efficient way of handling dynamic information in many natural systems, but there may be others in which a different approach is more fruitful. Their analysis of the effect of the spin of a body on its gravitational orbit is certainly impressive and yields new results which can be tested experimentally. A successful result, however, would not be a refutation of standard Newtonian physics, because that theory is valid only in inertial frames, and a spinning body defines a non-inertial frame. Newtonian physics handles such frames by incorporating inertial forces — it doesn’t prescribe the method for handling such forces. It certainly doesn’t deny the conservation of angular momentum, which is

one of the cornerstones of the Newtonian method.

Classical physics differs from quantum physics in being concerned with measurement. As soon as a measurement is made, a system necessarily becomes at least semi-classical. In fact, we could say that, as soon as any interaction occurs to restrict the absolute freedom of space and time variation of a fermionic state (or to deny the canonical nature of a micro-ensemble), a quantum system is already on the way to becoming classical. An isolated, or pure quantum, system will conserve £, p and m within the system, linking it with the total k, i, j charge values. Such a system will remain coherent -- with angular momentum operators aligned, making addition effectively scalar, like that of the charge units. However, for an interacting system, however small the interaction, the exact connection between the conservation laws for charge (k, i, j) and those for angular

momentum and related terms (£, p and m) will be broken, due to decoherence in

the vector terms. Some energy will not be conserved within the system, but lost to the ‘rest of the universe’ with which it interacts. This is the meaning of the second law of thermodynamics, which may be seen as a necessary consequence of the entanglement of all the universe’s state vectors. It is also apparent why this process is our only way of apprehending the direction of time. The sequence of

events is irreversible precisely because it is unique.

10.8 Conclusion

The basis for all the ideas in this chapter is that the totality of the conceptual schemes we use to structure mathematics and physics is required to be zero at every level. From this we can develop a dualistic mathematics which has a unique relationship to what we conceive of as the physical world. Significant among the many physical notions which this generates is the idea that we cannot fully explain physical systems without the dualistic notion of time-delayed action between the discrete objects of physical measurement occurring at the same time

as unmediated instantaneous correlation- at-a-distance.

Chapter 11: Further Developments

In final preparation for this present publication, the following additional

developments were reported.

11.1 Viv Pope: Further developments in Normal Realism and POAMS

IL.L1 The ‘photon’

During my most recent visit, in August 2004, to the annual meeting of the Alternative Natural Philosophy Association (ANPA), of which I am a long- standing member, I was handed the following letter from Professor James Lindesay, of Howard University, Washington, USA. At this time of writing, he is presently spending a sabbatical year at SLAC (The Super Linear Accelerator

Centre) in California, USA, and this is what he wrote:

Viv (Pope) was giving a talk at ANPA in the mid 1990s in which he challenged the participants to provide proof of the existence of the ‘photon’ as a travelling light particle. At the time, no-one was able to give a convincing proof.

However, one participant, James Lindesay, decided to take up the challenge and, back at university, SLAC, Stanford, USA, worked for several months, together with a colleague who specialises in quantum optics (laser physics), to determine whether, indeed, the ‘photon’ can be shown to exist independently of sources and sinks. Arguments based on black body radiation (which Planck originally explained in terms of sources he called “resonators’) were explored. This is in QED (Quantum Electrodynamics) which studies the scattering of photons. Feynman, along with his colleague Wheeler, had hypothesised that one cannot discriminate between photons that are with and those that are without sources and sinks. Lindesay felt that his best hope of meeting Pope’s challenge was via the Casimir effect, in which the vacuum energy together with the photons are supposed to create a measurable effect — also in the absence of photons, which has been verified, Since the effect was measurable both in the absence and presence of photons, this seemed to be the best candidate for Pope’s criteria for proving the existence of photons.

However, Lifshitz and others have demonstrated that the same effect can be explained in terms of the zero point motions of the materials in the sources and sinks resulting in van der Waals forces entirely, without invoking ‘photons’.

These arguments convinced Lindesay that accounts of electromagnetic interactions need not invoke ‘photons’ but can correctly be described solely in terms of quantised transitions of electrons within the sources and sinks themselves.

In this way, as Lindesay described it to me, he and his colleagues at SLAC have confirmed, to their satisfaction, that all talk of ‘photons’ in this electrodynamics

context is redundant. As a consequence of these studies carried out at SLAC,

those interactions between these sources and sinks that are customarily called ‘electromagnetic’ are more economically conceived as instances of pure, unmediated at-a-distance resonance in the way Normal Realism describes. As Lindesay reported to Mary Pope, at a conference in Cambridge: “We set out to

prove Viv wrong and ended up proving him right.’

11.1.2 The Mössbauer effect

In discussions following the second Swansea Workshop, George Galeczki challenged us to explain, in Normal Realist terms, the results of the Mössbauer experiment.' Since this experiment is concerned with the effect of motion between interacting atoms, let us first recall what our lateral paradigm predicts regarding atomic interactions. This is that the condition of direct inter-resonance between atoms may be inhibited by any change As in the distance s = vtp between them that disturbs that resonance, as, for instance, by any relativistic Doppler shift due to relative motion. This is confirmed in the case of the Mössbauer experiment, which employs a method of measuring the statistical numbers of direct inter- resonances between nuclear emitters and absorbers of coherent gamma radiation. The period of this radiation is so short that even the smallest movements between the emitter and the absorber, on a scale where even a snail’s pace is extremely large, create Doppler shifts in the radiation sufficient to de-tune and therefore inhibit the transaction of gamma radiation between the nuclei of atoms. This confirms what was reasoned in section 4.5 of Chapter 5 in this volume, concerning ‘cinematic’ at-a-distance correlation or resonance, namely, that ‘without this condition of resonance being satisfied, there can be no transfer of energy’. Normally, the experimenters report, the recoils of the emitting nucleons cause Doppler changes sufficient to desynchronise them. Tuning adjustments, however, made by either inhibiting the recoil or by moving the emitter and absorber relatively to one another, or else by varying the heat-motion of their individual atoms, enable the nucleons to resonate. By this means, relativistic

Doppler shifts in the nuclear periods are measured very exactly, in a way which

precisely confirms the predictions of SR, hence also of its Normal Realist

Pythagorean paraphrase.

11.1.3 The POAMS &lsquo;New Move&rsquo;

A significant advance in the Pope-Osborne Angular Synthesis (POAMS) occurred as the result of a long-running and highly volatile but altogether productive e-mail debate between myself and some American scientists concerning the earth- orbiting Global Positioning System (GPS) satellites. POAMS was challenged to explain, in its own terms, and without theoretical adulteration, the corrective adjustments that have to be made to the satellite clocks, as compared with the clocks on earth, in order for them to give correct readings of geographical locations. These challenges were addressed, and the results, gained by purely syllogistical argument, proved to be qualitatively correct. This was by the simple expedient of using the POAMS equation for circular orbits , ie, v = GM/r. By substituting this equivalent expression for v’ in the linear time-dilation equation, a

formula for orbital time-dilation was directly obtained ($t_p = t\sqrt{1 - GM/(rc^2)}$).

However, although conceded to be qualitatively correct in predicting what is well known empirically, that clocks run more slowly as they approach large masses (as the GPS and the Russian GLONASS satellites do with decrease in their height above earth), there was much dispute over ‘the numbers’ produced by this very simple formula. Further discussions with colleagues confirmed, however, that the formula and the reasoning that had led to it were perfectly valid and was indeed, as Rowlands put it, no more than ‘bog standard’ as a way of obtaining orbital time-dilation, Further development of the formula, by Osborne (see next section), revealed that not only does the revised formula produce, exactly, ‘the numbers’ quoted by the GPS people but also that its predictions for orbital time-dilation, orbital ‘rosette motion’, the Schwarzschild radius, etc., are

the same as those made by GR.

The significant difference, however, is that in POAMS this is achieved by pure syllogistic reasoning applied in a completely non-theoretical, logico-

philosophical way to basic propositions of physics language (pace Wittgenstein).

This supports the Normal Realist contention of POAMS that much, if not all, of the evolved metaphysical super/infra-structure of modern relativistic and quantum

physics, useful though it may have been in the past, is now redundant.

11.2 Anthony D. Osborne: Further developments in the Pope-Osborne angular momentum synthesis

The Pope-Osborne Angular Momentum Synthesis (POAMS) was described in Chapter 9. Recall that the fundamental postulates of this synthesis are that all motion is naturally orbital and that orbital angular momentum is holistically conserved. More explicitly, bodies naturally travel in orbits of automatically paired and balanced angular momentum, as summarised in the following

principle, due to Jon Blay.

The Principle of Angular Inertia: All bodies move angular-inertially. Angular inertia is the resistance of a body to any change in its angular momentum relations and is the natural tendency of a body to follows the path of least resistance {i.e. least action) in accordance with the conservation of angular momentum,

On the other hand, Pope’s Normal Realist philosophy dictates that the standard time dilation formula of SR holds and can be derived in a very simple way, as shown in section 1 of Chapter 5. A criticism that has sometimes been levelled at the Normal Realist approach is that this time dilation formula explicitly refers to rectilinear motion, whereas in POAMS, such motion is unnatural. Recently, this criticism has been addressed by Osborne and Pope ? by a ‘new move’ in POAMS that consists of ‘marrying’ the time dilation formula into its angular momentum synthesis in order to derive orbital time dilation effects. These effects are in agreement with the predictions of GR. Moreover, in deriving these known effects in such a simple way, POAMS also predicts the observed perihelion shift effect of the planets as in GR, without the need for the Einstein field equations. A summary

of this derivation is given below.

As demonstrated in Chapter 5, if a clock X moves along a rectilinear path at constant speed v relative to an observer O, then in passage of time ft as recorded

by O, X’s clock records a passage of proper time t given by

$$t = \sqrt{1 - v^2/c^2}\;\bar{t} \tag{1}$$

Now suppose clock X moves with non-constant velocity v(#) relative to observer O and let v(t) = || v@) || denote the speed of the clock relative to O. It follows by

(1) that in this case, the relatively moving clock X records proper time t given by t=] V1 -v(/c’) dt. (2)

SR, the underlying mathematical structure is Minkowski space-time.

We now wish to apply the time dilation formula (2) to orbital motion in POAMS. Consider the case of an isolated system consisting of a particle P of mass m orbiting a body of mass M, situated at the origin O of some frame of reference. This is what POAMS calls deep space time (DST)./ It follows by (8) that the closer P is to the mass M, the greater the time dilation effect. In other words, clocks on circular geodesics further away from the mass M run faster than clocks nearer to M, relative to DST. Clearly, in (8) there is a necessary lower bound placed on r

perihelion shift phenomenon.

It must be remembered that POAMS does not begin by postulating that all geodesic orbits are elliptical. Rather, POAMS postulates that all motion is orbital and that angular momentum is conserved. For initial simplicity, it is assumed in POAMS, that in any isolated two-body system, orbits are closed. Then Bertrand’s theorem gives (6) as the equation of motion and, as a consequence, geodesic orbits are ellipses. However, once time dilation effects are taken into consideration, POAMS predicts, just as in GR, that the geodesic orbits are given by (21). In this case, the corresponding geodesic orbits are approximately ellipses with a small perihelion shift, and so are no longer closed. In other words, when time dilation effects are taken into account, Newton’s ‘Inverse Square Law of Gravitation’ no longer holds. This can be seen from (21), since letting wu = I/r,

with K’ = Km/I? and B’ = L’/m’, as above, gives (1/r*)(dr/d0)? + Vr? = Kn IL? + 2Me?nP (1?) + 2MIP?

where £ is the constant magnitude of the angular momentum of the orbit. It

follows by (5) that on any planar orbit,

$$dr/d\theta = (dr/dt)(dt/d\theta) = (mr^2/L)(dr/dt)$$

Hence (21) becomes

$$(dr/dt)^2 + L^2/(m^2r^2) = K + 2\mathcal{M}c^2/r + 2\mathcal{M}L^2/(m^2r^3)$$
$$\Rightarrow \ddot{r} - r(d\theta/dt)^2 = -GM/r^2 - 3GML^2/(c^2m^2r^4) \tag{22}$$

and not A(r) = GM/r’ as dictated by Bertrand’s theorem for closed orbits. The second term in /(r) here represents the correction term which takes time dilation into consideration.

In conclusion, although for initial simplicity in Chapter 9, it was assumed that in any isolated two-body system, orbits are closed, once time dilation effects are taken into consideration, POAMS predicts, just as in GR, that the geodesic orbits are approximately ellipses with a small perihelion shift, and so are no

longer closed.

Chapter 12: General Conclusion

It is practically a tautology that just about every significant advance that was ever made in science was a heresy in its day, as the fortunes of people like Copernicus and Galileo plainly attest. Today’s heresy is to suggest, in the face of Einstein’s opinions to the contrary, that bodies may influence one another across space instantly, in advance of the limiting speed of light. Yet how do the stars in a galaxy maintain equilibrium with one another at opposite ends of that galaxy, as they plainly do, if no influence can pass between them faster than the speed of

light — which in a typical spiral galaxy would take about 100,000 years?

However, to some people, the very suggestion that there should be such a thing as instantaneous action-at-a-distance, or even instantaneous correlation-at-a- distance, is absurd. How, they say, can any such action or correlation between distance-separated and independently moving bits of matter be possible without there being, between them, some material interconnection of either solid or fluid construction? Anything else, they think, would have to be some kind of magic,

which is not allowed in scientific explanation.

But is it magic which ensures that in a random cluster of molecules, no single one of those molecules may move in such a way as to disconserve the energy of the whole? What is it that makes each individual molecule in its freedom of motion within that cluster, conform so precisely to the overall law? Is it magic? Is it unscientific? Plainly not, since the laws in question are scientific

laws.

And as with clusters of molecules, so with clusters of stars. Do the motions of the stars in a galaxy conform any less to the overall law of energy conservation than those of the molecules in, say, a steam cylinder? How truly independent, then, are objects that are distanced from one another in what we

think of as random motion? How does the whole manage to exert such an

influence on the parts? These are not questions of magic, they are truly scientific questions, and they are the sorts of questions that have been addressed, in various ways, in this compilation. This presents the reader with a choice of explanations of the problem that has become known in quantum physics as ‘non-locality’. The fact that the chapters in this volume express no consensus solution to that problem

enhances the richness of the choice.

So at least, nowadays, there is a choice. At one time it was the case — and still is among many science publishers — that the mere mention of ‘instantaneous action’ in the title of a manuscript would have got it rejected out of hand. The book that led to these Swansea Workshops was entitled Jnstantaneous Action At a Distance in Modern Physics: Pro and Contra, (Eds. A.E. Chubykalo, V. Pope and R. Smirnov-Rueda). One of the three editors of that book, Viv Pope, was invited to a meeting at Oxford to discuss a follow-up to that work on behalf of the pro- IAAAD section — which, as it turned out, drew by far the larger proportion of contributions. There, at Oxford, a retired American professor — who is one of the contributors to this present volume — shook Pope by the hand and said that in all of his long years of academic experience he had never imagined that he would ever see a book of that title grace a table in the ‘hallowed halls’ of Oxford University. It was at this professor’s instigation that the Workshop was eventually

convened.

Given, then, that all those who attended the Swansea Workshop were prepared, in the interests of scientific progress, to flout the mere conventions, their main point of agreement seems to be that IAAAD is assured by the principle of conservation of some quantity, such as energy, momentum, charge, angular momentum or whatever. Disputes and disagreements arose as to which of these candidates for IAAAD best fits the known facts. Despite the main point of agreement, that distant instantaneity exists, it has to be stressed that what are presented in this volume are — or, at least, started out as — very much rival theories as to how, physically, that agreed distant action/correlation is facilitated. Is it by

means of some material or quasi-material influence travelling at a superluminal

speed from one body to another, or does conservation in itself link all the bodies together? For some contributors (such as those in the Normal Realist group) distant instantaneity is automatically assured by the fact that in Special Relativity (SR), light itself, which in relative time is c, in its own proper-time is instantaneous, so that the whole issue of ‘instantaneous versus delayed’ distant interaction is spurious. Others, such as Galeczki and the Graneaus take the dissident view of SR, according to which that theory is fundamentally flawed. For Galeczki, this is on account of Relativity’s banishment of a universal, or cosmic, frame of reference for mechanical motion as well as, in particular, the ‘universal time’ of classical science by which these motions are measured. Galeczki’s view is supported by the Graneaus for whom a main flaw in Relativity is what they see as that theory’s uncritical acceptance of the absolute constancy of ¢ in all relatively moving systems of reference. Neal Graneau cites, on grounds of electrodynamical experiments with which he and his father are involved, what he sees as findings conflicting with the standard result of the Michelson and Morley experiment on which SR is based. Their conclusion, from these and other experiments and in accord with other dissident scientists is that, counter to the claims of the relativists, the ‘velocity c’ is not constant but obeys the classical rule of the composition of velocities, In contrast to this, Normal Realism endorses Einsteinian Relativity in accepting the constancy of c in all observational frames, but as a constant of dimensional projection, or perspective, not as a velocity, far less a velocity which is constant with respect to all relatively moving observers as

well as to space (qua vacuum) itself.

Although the chapters present different and sometimes conflicting perspectives on the question of instantaneous distant action, these views appear, for the most part, complementary to the extent that the level of overlap and agreement, in our opinion, is clearly greater than the level of disagreement. The “conflicting perspectives’ on the all-agreed existence of instantaneous action-at-a- distance ([AAAD) are however far from trivial. Such as they are, the differences

seem to be philosophical rather than what might be called ‘purely scientific’

These differences were more pronounced in the Workshop discussions, both ¢- mailed and verbal, than in the papers presented as chapters of this book. For example, for the ‘scientists’ of the Workshop group, Mach’s principle essentially implies a purely physical space-time holism in which all masses are somehow linked together inertially, whereas for the ‘philosophers’ it implies Mach’s whole

relativistic approach to physics, in the form of phenomenalism.

The ‘scientists’, therefore, in this deep but more or less tacit divide are those contributors for whom the correct conduct for what they regard as physics is to disregard all ‘philosophical’ arguments and simply measure and record, without conceptual bias, what is ‘plainly observed’ in hands-on experimental and observational situations. For these people, any undue concern for philosophical or even logical coherence among their findings inhibits their freedom to make exciting and unexpected new discoveries. Such a concern for logical stringency they regard as ‘undemocratic’, hence akin to dogma in slowing the pace of scientific progress. Both these different attitudes towards physics, the eclectic and the philosophical, are discernible in this book, offering a selection from which the

reader may freely choose according to his lights.

There is, however, more coherence between these two seemingly opposite approaches to physics than their proponents may have realised. They are both essentially empirical insofar as they base themselves equally on observation and experiment. The only difference is that for the philosophers, conceptual interpretations of experimental/observational information need to seek overall logical consistency and coherence, so as to distinguish clearly between one philosophical paradigm and another in the search for a natural truth that is free

from subjective interpretation, whereas for the eclectics there is no such need.

Although it is more by accident than design, the chapters in this book cover the whole spectrum of physics; from the quantum to the relativistic, from atoms to galaxies and from mechanics to electrodynamics. The Pope-Osborne Angular Momentum Synthesis brings together the quantum and relativistic in a

way that is radical and algebraically minimalist yet philosophically,

observationally and logically consistent. Their approach offers the possibility of a solution to the unification of forces in a ‘unified field theory’ by suggesting that those forces do not exist: Pope and Osborne would have gravity go the way of phlogiston. The Graneaus are perhaps not so radical but offer a different kind of unifying principle, by postulating an instantaneous action at a distance paradigm that encompasses gravitational, magnetic and electric interactions within a single Newtonian inverse square law framework. Like the Graneaus, Roscoe invokes Mach’s principle, but does so in relation to the observed mass distribution of Spiral galaxies. Rowlands develops a beautifully elegant mathematical structure, from first principles, which shows a direct mapping to the fundamental physical properties, space, time, mass and charge. He then uses and extends this mapping to elucidate properties such as inertia and angular momentum. Rowlands’ bottom- up approach is perfectly complemented by Galeczki’s chapter, which considers a top-down ‘systems’ approach. Galeczki brings together much of the work of this book by comparing and contrasting its authors’ different approaches within an ultimately closed and hierarchical universe. Thus, in a way that the editors neither designed nor anticipated, the different approaches to immediate distant action and correlation presented in this book show a much higher level of agreement than

disagreement and are remarkable in their complementarity.

In any event, the issue of whether there is or is not distant instantaneity is crucial to that relic of twentieth-century physics, the apparently fundamental conflict between Relativity Theory and Quantum Theory. With this in mind, the arguments in this compilation of papers should be seen, not so much as seeking to refute one another as to present a set of logically distinct and clear-cut alternative solutions to this so far unsolved problem. As to which, if any, of these alternatives fulfils the expectations of a twenty-first-century solution to that chronic dilemma,

Epilogue: Tribute to Alan Smart

Viv Pope

One of the staunchest supporters of the Normal Realist philosophy was Alan Montague Smart, who, very sadly, died suddenly, at the age of sixty-four, only

weeks prior to the second Swansea Workshop which he was due to attend.

From the very emergence of the ideas which led to the synthesis that has now been named POAMS, Alan was privy to every stage in its development. As a telecommunications engineer, and friend and colleague of Viv Pope in that profession, Alan came to agree that a paradigm shift from a mechanics-based to an information-based physics is essential. His final conviction, in this regard, was that all our knowledge of nature stems not from the physis of traditional atomism but from a /ogos of informational data, the ultimate irreducible unit of which is

Planck’s action-quantum A,

As a student of theology (he had been a pupil at Swansea Bible College), Alan was critical of the tacit ‘God’s-eye-view’ that underlies contemporary physics — as implicit, for instance, in Einstein’s famous rejection of quantum uncertainty in the words, ‘God does not play dice’. Alan emphasised to us the distinction theologians make between deism and theism. As he explained, deism is the incipient belief, especially among physicists, in a deity which they envisage as a Cosmical Machine-Designer whose primordial, first and only act was to provide the determinate mechanical ‘Laws of Nature’, divined by the likes of Galileo and Newton, by which ~ so the deists believe — the whole future of the universe and all life in it are predetermined. Theism, in contrast, is a belief in an information- transacting deity, to whom supplication, in the form of prayer, may be contemplated without logical contradiction. The standard conception of a fundamental conflict between Science and Religion, therefore, as Alan saw it, is a

fallacy. The real conflict, he argued, is not between scientific reason and religious

belief but, implicitly, between two forms of religious belief, namely, scientific deism on the one hand and theism on the other, leaving no question of any

rationally sustainable position of ‘atheism’.

Moreover, as Alan would argue, there can be no social value in a religion that is without morality. And since there can be no morality in a machine, then in replacing the determinate atomic mechanisms of classical physics with informational sequences of indeterminate quanta, Normal Realism signified for him a removal of the age-old conflict between physical Science and rational

Theology.

For Alan, personally, this was very satisfying. His satisfaction was enhanced when, in the late 1970s, Viv Pope reported back to him, after a visit to the Electronic Engineering Department at Hull University, that the Head of Department, Dennis Midgley, had declared it to be something of a revelation to have identified and disposed of this deistic ‘God’s-eye-view’ of space, time and matter. This incipient deism was, as Midgley came to see it, a prime source of interference and inhibition among those ‘dissidents’ who continually fail to understand relativity. Something else that Alan was pleased to learn was that in these same discussions at Hull, it had been confirmed by these professionals that there can be no information in determinacy, whether it be that of mechanics or geometry. For a sequence of points on a line or of events in time to be determinate, those elements have to be continuously connected, with no gaps or uncertainties. In a seminar involving Midgeley, Rod Goodman, Alan Winfield and Viv Pope, it had been agreed that only in the purely stochastic sequences of discrete and indeterminate events can there be any informational content. As Goodman had demonstrated mathematically, at a level where the informational elements merge into a continuum, the probability of succession becomes certainty and the information-content reduces, correspondingly, to zero. Nor was it lost on Alan that the causal Second Law of Thermodynamics, or ‘Law of Entropy’, which Boltzmann had conceived in terms of the continuous trajectories and determinate

collisions of molecules, was no less causally efficient when stated in terms of

completely indeterminate or stochastic events-sequences in Claude Shannon’s information-theoretical rendering of that same law. So far as Alan was concerned, all this came logically and coherently together in what he called his ‘paradigm

flip-over’ from a mechanics-based to an information-based natural philosophy.

The publication of a paper, ‘The New World Synthesis’,! which encapsulated these innovative ideas, was enthusiastically endorsed by Alan as what he would advocate, to all and sundry, as his ‘Handbook’ for the paradigm change. It articulated for him the ‘flip-side’ implications of the New Approach to relativity, 7 not only for his theological convictions but also for pure physics, and it became the foundation of his life-long support for the thesis which has since become known as Normal Realism. He could clearly see that if light is to be regarded as the a priori source of physical information, rather than something travelling in space (See Pope in this volume), then the same language that is customarily used to describe phenomena in areas such as Mechanical Engineering and Astronomy cannot be used in the same way to describe the events and processes at the information-datum level of the quanta into which those phenomena are analysed. At the level of the quantum data, the language that classical scientists such as Galileo and Newton had developed to describe physical actions in terms of their ‘Natural Laws’ has to be switched from a mechanical- engineering form of language into an information-theoretical mode, the sort employed in Midgeley’s department at Hull. In other words, as Alan would frequently say, at that level ‘We have to switch from talking steam-engines to talking computers.’ With his telecommunications engineering training, it was easy for him to contemplate physics, at its most fundamental microphysical level, having to abandon its customary ‘analog’ approach to quantum physics and, as he

put it, ‘go digital’.

' Pope, N.V., Philosophia Mathematica, V1 Vol. 4, No. 1 pp 23-29, (1989)

? Pope N.V. & Osborne, A.D. ‘A New Approach to Special Relativity’, International Journal of Mathematical Education in Science and Technology, 18, 2 (1987).

Speaking in this way, Alan would all too often mystify his listeners, in pubs around the Gower area, with what many regarded as an off-putting enthusiasm for what he called this ‘paradigm flip-over’. Some of Alan’s critics judged that his personal ‘religious’ satisfaction with the Normal Realist information-based approach to physics made him uncritical of, if not over-zealous towards that holistic, top-down approach, as opposed to that of ‘hard-nosed experimental physics’. Alan’s answer to these critics was that in real commonsense situations, contextual judgements are both the initial and final criteria in any evaluation of advancing scientific knowledge. Also, with his leanings towards Christianity, he pointed out to us, interestingly, that the word ‘holy’ comes from the same root as ‘holism’, and he stated that he was a

confirmed Holist, in both senses of the word.

He also emphasised that the word ‘health’ stemmed from the same root. He was therefore never ‘fazed’ by those who adopted a hard-line ‘experimental physicist’ stance in opposition to what they misconceived as his ‘religious’ arguments. He was, in any case, by no means mathematically illiterate. As a well- qualified telecommunications engineer, it was essential for him to be at least reasonably competent in mathematics and physics. The Normal Realist argument, therefore, for holistic interconnection and causality on the macrolevel, based on stochastic indeterminacy at the ultimate microlevel, was not swallowed by him uncritically. Not only had he contested it vigorously and competently at every stage in its development, but his criticisms often led to an enhancement of the

thesis and, sometimes, to the correction of logical and mathematical errors.

In the early days, as a director of Phi Philosophical Enterprises, with Viv Pope and Arwyn Evans, Alan also acted as Distribution Manager for the company’s publication, Phi the Philosophical Viewspaper, copies of which were distributed free to every university philosophy department in the United Kingdom. They were also distributed to the Copyright Libraries as well as some universities and institutions overseas. The radical, anti-establishment nature of this

Viewspaper generated some interesting reactions which, in his free-thinking

independence of the Academic establishment, Alan lampooned with relish. In conversations at the ‘Blue Anchor’, on the Gower Peninsula, near Swansea, he would hold forth about it (very much in the vernacular) with an amusing and

refreshing wit and lack of reserve.

For a while, with Alan as one of its mainstays, this ‘Blue Anchor Group’, as it came to be called, became a redoubtable source of critical literature on the subject of the commonsense shortcomings of contemporary Physics. This was to the extent that the name of ‘Blue Anchor’ became bandied about in Physics circles, not only in Cambridge but also in Stanford, USA, where one of the Professors offered to deliver to ‘the group’ a series of talks, unaware that his venue for this occasion would be a fairly typical and academically unencumbered Welsh ‘pub’, albeit one which had often hosted, within the group, several well- appointed and — at the end of the evening — genially expansive academics. It is a measure of the influence that Alan maintained on this group that, at his demise,

the ‘Blue Anchor Group’ dispersed.

Alan’s death has meant that J, in particular, have lost a much appreciated source of friendship, support and advice. This loss was also felt by the newly formed NRIG (Normal Realist Interest Group) of which Alan would have been a

valued member. We shall miss him.
